=====================================================================
MISSION ORDER FOR GEMINI: FLY8 STUDY, NP1-NP5 REVIEW-NOTE EXPANSION
(Philippine nursing licensure exam / PNLE review content) - PACK NP4
=====================================================================

READ THIS WHOLE ORDER FIRST. DO NOT START WORKING YET.
When you have read everything and opened the files in PART 2, reply ONLY with:
  1. the word READY,
  2. a 10-line-max summary of what you understood,
  3. your proposed batch plan (which file and which notes come first),
  4. any question you have.
Then STOP and WAIT. Do nothing else until I (the owner) type the single word: GO
After GO, do exactly ONE batch per reply (see PART 6), then stop and wait for me to type NEXT.
If at any point I type STOP, stop immediately.


---------------------------------------------------------------------
PART 1. WHO YOU ARE AND WHAT THE PROJECT IS
---------------------------------------------------------------------
You are a research-and-writing agent working for the owner of "FLY8 Study",
a personal Android study app for the Philippine Nurse Licensure Examination
(PNLE). The owner is a nursing reviewee, not a programmer. Another AI (Claude)
builds and maintains the app; it will check and merge what you produce. You do
NOT edit the GitHub repository directly. You produce exact, ready-to-merge
JSON patches in your replies (format in PART 5). The owner pastes them back to
Claude.

The app holds about 974 short review notes. Each note is a JSON object inside
a "pack" file. Your job: EXPAND and STRENGTHEN the NP1 to NP5 notes so every
disease / condition / problem note follows one clinical structure, is
Philippine-based, is fact-checked, and carries real references.

The five PNLE areas used in this app (current understanding, verify if you can):
  NP1  Community Health Nursing (+ communicable diseases, PH health programs)
  NP2  Care of Healthy/At-Risk Mother and Child (OB, newborn, pediatrics, growth and development)
  NP3  Physiologic and Psychosocial Alterations, Part A (cardio, respiratory, blood/hema, oncology,
       perioperative, fluids-electrolytes and renal, burns)
  NP4  Physiologic and Psychosocial Alterations, Part B (GI, endocrine, neuro, eye and ear, ortho)
  NP5  Physiologic and Psychosocial Alterations, Part C (psychiatric nursing, emergency, disaster, critical care)
  (Fundamentals, pharmacology, ethics/laws run through all five. They are NOT in scope for this mission.)


---------------------------------------------------------------------
PART 2. THE FILES (ALL INCLUDED BELOW IN THIS SAME DOCUMENT)
---------------------------------------------------------------------
You do NOT need GitHub or any link. Everything you need is pasted at the end of this
document, between lines that start with "===== FILE:" and "===== END FILE".
This pack covers NP4 ONLY. Work only on the files in this pack, in this order:
  1. data/presets/np4-gitendo.json
  2. data/presets/np4-neurosenses.json
  3. data/presets/np4-ortho.json

Also included for context: data/presets/bundles.json (which subject each note belongs to).
Use the file path shown after "===== FILE:" as the "target" in your JSON patches.
Do not touch anything that is not in this pack. Other NP areas come in separate packs.
If a file below looks cut off or will not parse, say which one and STOP.

---------------------------------------------------------------------
PART 3. WHAT A NOTE LOOKS LIKE TODAY (keep these fields)
---------------------------------------------------------------------
Each note is a JSON object like this (shortened):
{
  "id": "np1-cd-dengue",
  "area": "NP1",
  "title": "Dengue Fever",
  "body": "## What it is\n...markdown...\n\n## Related topics\n[[Malaria]], [[Filariasis]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n...",
  "tags": ["communicable disease", "..."],
  "priority": "high",
  "outlineRefs": ["..."],
  "aiWritten": true,
  "nurseReviewed": false
}
Rules for fields:
- NEVER change "id", "area", "outlineRefs". Keep "tags" (you may ADD tags, never remove).
- "priority" stays as is unless clearly wrong (then explain why in your batch notes).
- "aiWritten" must be true. "nurseReviewed" must stay false. You are not a nurse reviewer.
- "body" is markdown with only: "## " headings, paragraphs, "- " bullets, "1. " numbered lists,
  **bold**, *italic*, and [[Wikilinks]] to other note titles. No tables, no HTML, no images, no code fences.
- Keep [[Wikilinks]] that exist. Only add a [[Link]] if a note with that exact title exists in the packs.


---------------------------------------------------------------------
PART 4. THE NEW STRUCTURE FOR EVERY DISEASE / CONDITION NOTE
---------------------------------------------------------------------
Rewrite the "body" of each disease, disorder or clinical-problem note into EXACTLY these sections,
in this order. Use plain, exam-ready wording. Short bullets. Philippine context everywhere it applies.

## What it is
  1-3 sentences: definition, who gets it, why it matters on the PNLE.

## Causative agent
  The organism (genus/species, type: virus/bacteria/parasite/fungus), or the cause/risk factors
  if not infectious (e.g. autoimmune, genetic, trauma). Include mode of transmission, incubation
  period and period of communicability for communicable diseases. Include the vector or reservoir
  common in the Philippines (e.g. Aedes aegypti, Anopheles flavirostris, Oncomelania snail).
  If there is no causative agent (e.g. a nursing concept), write "Not applicable" and the main risk factors.

## Main problem
  The core pathophysiology in 1-3 sentences: what goes wrong in the body (the "why" behind the signs).

## Initial manifestations
  The EARLY / first signs and symptoms (what the nurse sees first). Mark pathognomonic signs with
  "(pathognomonic)" only if a reference supports it.

## Other manifestations
  Later, severe or complicating signs; warning signs; danger signs that need referral.

## Laboratory and diagnostic data
  Key tests and the expected abnormal result (with normal ranges ONLY when a reference gives them,
  in SI or conventional units as used in the Philippines). Confirmatory test vs screening test.

## Nursing diagnoses
  2-5 priority nursing diagnoses in NANDA-I style (problem related to cause as evidenced by signs),
  ordered by priority (ABC, Maslow, safety). Mark the top one "(priority)".

## Nursing interventions
  Numbered, in priority order. Each line: the action, then " - " and a short rationale.
  Include: assessment first, safety, infection control/isolation type, positioning, fluids/nutrition,
  medications by CLASS (drug names only if the reference names them; DOSES ONLY if a cited
  reference states the dose, otherwise write "dose per physician order / current guideline"),
  health teaching, referral, and prevention.

## Philippine context
  DOH program or strategy that covers it (e.g. EPI, National TB Program DOTS, Dengue Prevention and
  Control Program, Rabies Prevention and Control Program, Integrated Management of Childhood Illness),
  relevant Republic Act or DOH Administrative Order (number + name, only if verified),
  local terms (Filipino names, e.g. "bulutong", "tigdas"), common local exam traps.

## Exam traps
  3-5 bullets: classic PNLE-style confusions and the correct thinking (e.g. "fever falls in the
  critical phase of dengue = danger, not improvement").

## Related topics
  Keep the existing [[Wikilinks]]; add only links to notes that exist.

## References
  2-5 numbered references you ACTUALLY opened and read. Format:
  1. Organization or author. Title. Year (or "accessed YYYY-MM-DD"). URL
  Each key fact you added must be supported by at least one reference in this list.

## Check against your textbook
  Keep the existing text, then add any fact you could NOT verify, written as
  "UNVERIFIED: ..." so the owner can check it in their textbook.

For NON-disease notes (programs, laws, concepts, procedures, growth and development stages,
therapeutic communication, etc.): do NOT force the disease sections. Use only the sections that
make sense (always keep "What it is", add "Philippine context", "Exam traps", "References",
"Check against your textbook"). Say in your batch notes which notes you treated as non-disease.


---------------------------------------------------------------------
PART 5. VERIFY AND REFERENCE (SEARCH FOR THESE SOURCES)
---------------------------------------------------------------------
You MUST search the web and verify facts. Prefer, in this order:
  1. Philippine Department of Health: https://doh.gov.ph (programs, Administrative Orders,
     clinical practice guidelines, disease fact sheets), DOH Epidemiology Bureau.
  2. Official Philippine law and issuances: https://www.officialgazette.gov.ph , https://lawphil.net
     (Republic Acts, e.g. RA 9173 Philippine Nursing Act of 2002; verify every RA number).
  3. Professional Regulation Commission (PRC): https://www.prc.gov.ph (PNLE scope / table of
     specifications if available).
  4. WHO and WHO Western Pacific Region: https://www.who.int , https://www.who.int/westernpacific
  5. Philippine medical societies' public guidelines (e.g. Philippine Society for Microbiology and
     Infectious Diseases, Philippine Pediatric Society, Philippine Heart Association), PhilHealth.
  6. US CDC / NIH MedlinePlus / StatPearls (NCBI Bookshelf) for pathophysiology and lab data.
  7. NANDA-I terminology for nursing diagnoses (name the diagnosis; do not paste copyrighted definitions).

Search queries you should use (adapt per topic):
  "<disease> DOH Philippines", "<disease> clinical practice guideline Philippines",
  "<disease> administrative order DOH", "<disease> WHO fact sheet", "<disease> StatPearls",
  "<program name> DOH manual of operations", "Republic Act <number> <title> lawphil".

Verification rules:
- Open every source you cite and confirm the fact is on that page. Never cite a page you did not open.
- Never invent a URL, a law number, a guideline year, a page number, an author, or a drug dose.
- If two good sources disagree, state both and which is current (newest DOH guidance wins for PH practice).
- If the existing note contains a fact that your sources contradict, FIX it and list the fix in your
  batch notes as "CORRECTED: old -> new (source #)".
- If you cannot verify something, keep it out of the main sections and put it under
  "Check against your textbook" as UNVERIFIED.
- Write in your own words. Do not copy paragraphs from sources or textbooks. Short facts and
  official names/terms are fine.
- NEVER use, copy or reconstruct real PNLE board questions or "recalled questions" sites.
  They are confidential/copyrighted. Do not write any test questions in this mission.
- No patient-specific medical advice. This is exam review content.


---------------------------------------------------------------------
PART 6. HOW TO DELIVER (EXACT OUTPUT FORMAT, ONE BATCH PER REPLY)
---------------------------------------------------------------------
Batch size: 5 notes per reply (fewer if the notes are long). Work file by file in the PART 2 order,
NP1 first. Inside a file, do disease/condition notes before non-disease notes.

Each reply after GO / NEXT must contain exactly these 3 parts:

(1) A heading line:  BATCH <n> - <file path> - notes <i>-<j> of <total in file>

(2) ONE fenced JSON code block (this is the patch; it must be valid JSON, nothing else inside it):
```json
{
  "target": "data/presets/np1-comm.json",
  "batch": 1,
  "ops": [
    {
      "op": "update",
      "id": "np1-cd-dengue",
      "note": { ...the FULL updated note object with all fields... }
    }
  ]
}
```
  - "op": "update" replaces the note with that id in the target file (send the FULL note, not a diff).
  - "op": "add" adds a NEW note (only if an important PNLE disease/condition is clearly missing from the
    NP area). New ids: "<area lowercase>-x-<short-slug>", e.g. "np1-x-leptospirosis". Check every pack
    first so you do not duplicate an existing note under another title. Put a new note in the pack file
    of the same subject. Mark it "priority": "medium" unless it is a core PNLE topic.
  - Escape newlines inside "body" as \n and quotes as \". The block must parse as JSON.

(3) "BATCH NOTES" in plain text, short:
  - which notes were treated as disease vs non-disease,
  - every CORRECTED item,
  - every UNVERIFIED item,
  - sources you could not open,
  - suggested new notes for later (title + why), without writing them yet.

Then stop and wait for NEXT. Do not start the next batch on your own.
If your reply would be too long, split the batch smaller. Never cut a JSON block in half.


---------------------------------------------------------------------
PART 7. QUALITY BAR (CHECK BEFORE SENDING EACH BATCH)
---------------------------------------------------------------------
[ ] Every disease note has all sections of PART 4 in order (non-disease notes: the sensible subset).
[ ] Causative agent, main problem, initial and other manifestations, lab data, nursing diagnoses,
    interventions are present and specific (no filler like "monitor the patient" without what/why).
[ ] Philippine context is real (program names, RA/AO numbers verified, local terms).
[ ] Every reference was opened; URLs are real; each added fact traces to a reference.
[ ] No invented doses, laws, years, page numbers or URLs. Unverified items are labelled.
[ ] id/area/outlineRefs unchanged; aiWritten true; nurseReviewed false.
[ ] Body uses only allowed markdown (no tables/HTML/images/code).
[ ] The JSON block parses (no trailing commas, quotes escaped).
[ ] No PNLE board questions or recalled-question content anywhere.


---------------------------------------------------------------------
PART 8. COMMANDS I (THE OWNER) WILL USE
---------------------------------------------------------------------
  GO          start BATCH 1
  NEXT        do the next batch
  REDO <n>    redo batch n with my comments
  SKIP        skip the current note and continue
  JUMP <file> move to another file in PART 2
  STATUS      tell me which file/notes are done and what is left (no new work)
  STOP        stop now

Remember: right now, only reply READY + summary + batch plan + questions, then WAIT for GO.

"id": "np4-ge-digestive-tract-regions",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Digestive Tract Parts and Abdominal Regions",
"body": "## What it is\nThe GI tract is one long tube (roughly 23-26 feet) running from the mouth through the esophagus, stomach, small intestine, large intestine, rectum and anus.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- **Esophagus:** about 25 cm long, sits in the mediastinum in front of the spine and behind the trachea; it only carries food from mouth to stomach.\n- **Stomach:** a stretchy muscular pouch that holds about 1,500 mL and stores food while enzymes begin breaking it down.\n- **Small intestine:** the longest segment and the place where nutrients are absorbed. Three parts in order: duodenum, jejunum, ileum.\n- **Large intestine:** ascending, transverse, descending and sigmoid colon, then rectum. Waste arrives here and elimination begins.\n\n## Physical examination\n- Order of abdominal assessment: inspection, auscultation, percussion, palpation. Palpation and percussion come after listening because touching can change bowel sounds.\n- The handout lists palpation starting at the right lower quadrant, then right upper, left upper, left lower (a clockwise sweep).\n\n## Abdominal regions and what lies under them\n- **Right hypochondriac:** right liver lobe, gallbladder, part of duodenum, hepatic flexure, upper right kidney.\n- **Epigastric:** aorta, pyloric end of stomach, pancreas, part of liver.\n- **Left hypochondriac:** stomach, spleen, tail of pancreas, splenic flexure, upper left kidney.\n- **Right lumbar:** ascending colon, lower right kidney. **Umbilical:** omentum, mesentery, lower duodenum, parts of jejunum and ileum.\n- **Right inguinal:** cecum, appendix, end of ileum, right ureter, ovary or spermatic cord. **Left inguinal:** sigmoid colon, left ureter, ovary or spermatic cord.\n- **Hypogastric:** ileum, plus bladder or uterus when enlarged.\n- Use this map to reason about pain location: RUQ points to liver/gallbladder, RLQ to appendix.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Digestion, Absorption and Digestive Enzymes]], [[Appendicitis]], [[Cholelithiasis and Cholecystitis]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nMost textbooks say to palpate lightly and start in the quadrant farthest from the painful area; the handout's fixed order is only one convention.",

"id": "np4-ge-digestion-absorption-enzymes",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Digestion, Absorption and Digestive Enzymes",
"body": "## The three jobs\n- **Digestion:** enzymes and secretions break proteins, fats and sugars into small molecules.\n- **Absorption:** small molecules, vitamins and minerals pass through the intestinal wall into the blood.\n- **Elimination:** waste is removed after digestion and absorption.\n\n## Step by step\n- **Mouth:** chewing and swallowing start digestion. About 1.5 L of saliva a day comes from the parotid, submaxillary and sublingual glands. Salivary amylase begins starch digestion. Swallowing starts voluntarily, then the swallowing center in the medulla takes over.\n- **Stomach:** secretes strongly acidic fluid (hydrochloric acid). Chief cells make pepsinogen, which becomes pepsin, the key protein-digesting enzyme. The gastric lining also makes **intrinsic factor**, which binds dietary vitamin B12 so it can be absorbed. Food stays from 30 minutes to several hours depending on volume, osmotic pressure and chemical makeup.\n- **Small intestine:** amylase (starch), lipase (fat), trypsin (protein). Bile is made by the liver, stored in the gallbladder and emulsifies fat so it can be digested and absorbed. Daily secretions: about 1 L pancreatic juice, 0.5 L bile, 3 L from small intestine glands. Segmentation contractions mix and churn; peristalsis pushes contents toward the colon.\n- **Colon:** bacteria finish breaking down waste. Slow, weak peristalsis lets the colon reabsorb water and electrolytes, which is its main job. Strong intermittent waves move stool to the rectum in about 12 hours.\n\n## Why it matters\n- Loss of intrinsic factor (damaged stomach lining) leads to vitamin B12 deficiency, which is why gastritis lists B12 deficiency.\n- Fat digestion needs bile plus pancreatic lipase, so gallbladder or pancreas disease causes fatty stools.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Gastritis]], [[Acute Pancreatitis]], [[Liver, Gallbladder and Pancreas Structure and Function]], [[Digestive Tract Parts and Abdominal Regions]]",

"id": "np4-ge-barium-studies",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Upper and Lower GI Series (Barium Studies)",
"body": "## What they are\n- **Upper GI series (barium swallow):** contrast outlines the upper GI organs and sphincters. Detects ulcers, varices, tumors, regional enteritis, malabsorption and structural or functional problems.\n- **Lower GI series (barium enema):** x-rays of the lower GI tract after barium is given by enema. Takes about 15-30 minutes.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- **Upper GI:** clear liquids then NPO from midnight. Advise against smoking, chewing gum and mints because they stimulate gastric motility and spoil the picture. Increase fluids afterwards so stool and barium are flushed out. Oral medicines are usually held that morning and resumed in the evening, but each client's regimen is reviewed individually.\n- **Lower GI:** first ask about **allergy to iodine or contrast**. The bowel must be empty and clean: low-residue diet 1-2 days before, clear liquids, NPO after midnight, cleansing enemas until returns are clear. Laxative before and after.\n- **After the test (both):** push fluids and check that the client passes the barium. Retained barium can harden and cause constipation or obstruction, so report white or absent stools.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- \"Which instruction is correct before a barium enema?\" Pick bowel cleansing, low residue diet, and allergy check.\n- \"Which finding after barium means follow-up?\" No stool passed for days or abdominal distention.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Esophagogastroduodenoscopy (EGD) Nursing Care]], [[ERCP, Colonoscopy and Laparoscopy]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe advice that retained barium causes obstruction and that stool turns white is standard teaching I added; it is not in the handout.",

"id": "np4-ge-egd-nursing-care",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Esophagogastroduodenoscopy (EGD) Nursing Care",
"body": "## What it is\nDirect look at the esophagus, stomach and duodenal lining through a lighted flexible scope. It takes about 30 minutes. The client may feel nausea, gagging or choking.\n\n## Before the procedure\n- NPO for 8 hours.\n- Local anesthetic gargle or spray; moderate sedation (midazolam [Versed]) to relieve anxiety. Atropine may reduce secretions and glucagon may relax smooth muscle.\n- Position **left lateral** so secretions drain from the mouth and the scope enters smoothly.\n- A mouth guard stops the client biting the scope. Protect the scope: the fiberoptic bundles break if bent sharply.\n\n## After the procedure (priority order)\n1. **Airway first.** Topical anesthesia and sedation depress the gag reflex, so monitor and maintain the oral airway (Maslow: airway before comfort).\n2. Check level of consciousness, vital signs, oxygen saturation and pain.\n3. Watch for **perforation**: pain, bleeding, unusual difficulty swallowing, rapid rise in temperature.\n4. Keep NPO until the gag reflex returns, then offer lozenges, saline gargle or oral analgesics for a sore throat.\n5. Sedated clients stay in bed until fully alert.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nA common question: \"Which action first after EGD?\" Check gag reflex and airway before offering food or fluids.\n\n## Related topics\n[[ERCP, Colonoscopy and Laparoscopy]], [[Barrett's Esophagus]], [[Peptic Ulcer Disease: Gastric versus Duodenal Ulcer]]",

"title": "ERCP, Colonoscopy and Laparoscopy",
"body": "## ERCP\n- An endoscope plus x-ray (contrast) to see the common bile duct, pancreatic duct and hepatic ducts, entering through the ampulla of Vater in the duodenum.\n- Used for gallstones, bile duct blockage and pancreatic problems.\n\n## Colonoscopy\n- Direct look at the whole large intestine: anus, rectum, sigmoid, transverse, descending and ascending colon.\n- Therapeutic use: visible polyps can be removed with a snare and cautery through the scope.\n\n## Laparoscopy\n- Direct view of organs inside the abdomen to find growths, abnormalities and inflammation; biopsies can be taken.\n- Carbon dioxide is injected into the peritoneal cavity (**pneumoperitoneum**) to lift the intestines away from the pelvic organs.\n- Usually done under general anesthesia; the stomach and bowel sometimes need decompressing first.\n\n## Nursing points\n- Clients need bowel preparation for colonoscopy and NPO status before ERCP and laparoscopy, as ordered.\n- Monitor after any scope for perforation (pain, bleeding, fever) and recovery from sedation or anesthesia.\n- After laparoscopy, shoulder pain is common because of leftover gas (textbook point, see below).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Esophagogastroduodenoscopy (EGD) Nursing Care]], [[Gallstone Treatment and Biliary Drainage Care]], [[Diverticulosis and Diverticulitis]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout gives few nursing details here. Bowel prep, post-procedure monitoring and shoulder pain after laparoscopy are standard teaching I added, so verify them.",

"id": "np4-ge-gerd",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Gastroesophageal Reflux Disease (GERD)",
"body": "## What it is\nBackward flow of stomach and duodenal contents into the esophagus because the **lower esophageal sphincter (LES)** is weak or incompetent.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Classic symptom: **pyrosis** (burning behind the breastbone). Others: dyspepsia, dysphagia, hypersalivation, esophagitis.\n- Symptoms can mimic a heart attack. History helps tell them apart, but chest pain is still treated as cardiac until ruled out (Maslow: circulation/oxygenation first).\n- Tests: endoscopy or barium swallow, 12-36 hour ambulatory esophageal pH monitoring, bilirubin monitoring (Bilitec).\n\n## Treatment\n- **Antacids** neutralize acid.\n- **H2 receptor blockers** lower acid by blocking histamine at the parietal cells.\n- **Proton pump inhibitors (PPIs)** slow the acid pump of parietal cells; more potent than H2 blockers.\n- **Prokinetic agents** increase propulsive movement of the gut.\n- Surgery: **Nissen fundoplication**, wrapping the gastric fundus around the lower esophagus.\n\n## Client teaching (the usual test question)\n- Avoid what lowers LES pressure or irritates: caffeine, tobacco, beer, carbonated drinks, spicy and fatty foods.\n- Low-fat diet, keep a normal weight, wear loose clothing.\n- Do not eat or drink 2 hours before bed and do not lie down after meals.\n- Raise the head of the bed 6-8 inches.\n\n## Complications\nLong-standing reflux can lead to [[Barrett's Esophagus]]. Related: [[Hiatal Hernia]], [[Peptic Ulcer Drugs and Zollinger-Ellison Syndrome]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout says prokinetics enhance \"colonic transit\"; they actually speed gastric emptying and upper gut movement. It also lists milk as something to avoid; confirm with your textbook.",

"id": "np4-ge-barretts-esophagus",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Barrett's Esophagus",
"body": "## What it is\nA change in the lining of the lower esophagus caused by long-term acid reflux (GERD). The normal squamous cells are replaced by a different cell type (metaplasia). It is a **precursor to esophageal cancer**.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Same symptoms as GERD: pyrosis, dyspepsia, dysphagia, hypersalivation, esophagitis.\n- Diagnosis: EGD with **biopsy** (biopsy proves the cell change).\n\n## Treatment\n- Control reflux first (see [[Gastroesophageal Reflux Disease (GERD)]]).\n- Destroy abnormal cells: **photodynamic therapy** or **laser thermal ablation**.\n- **Esophagectomy** for advanced disease: removal of the esophagus with a wide tumor-free margin and nearby lymph nodes.\n\n## Nursing focus\n- Teach that untreated reflux can become cancer, so follow-up endoscopy matters.\n- Teach reflux control: small meals, head of bed raised, avoid late meals.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n\"Complication of long-term GERD that raises cancer risk?\" Answer: Barrett's esophagus. \"Test to confirm?\" EGD with biopsy.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Esophagogastroduodenoscopy (EGD) Nursing Care]], [[Hiatal Hernia]]",

"id": "np4-ge-hiatal-hernia",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Hiatal Hernia",
"body": "## What it is\nThe opening in the diaphragm (hiatus) through which the esophagus passes becomes enlarged, and part of the upper stomach moves up into the lower chest.\n\n## How to reason about it\nCause -> finding -> action: a wider hiatus -> the stomach and the lower esophageal sphincter sit in the wrong place -> acid and food flow back (reflux) and the stomach may be squeezed -> heartburn, regurgitation and chest pain. Gravity and small meals reduce reflux, which is why the teaching focuses on position and meal size. The trap: confusing the two types. Sliding is the common type with reflux. Paraesophageal hernia can trap the stomach and needs earlier attention.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- **Sliding hernia:** the upper stomach and the gastroesophageal junction slide upward and out of the thorax opening.\n- **Paraesophageal hernia:** all or part of the stomach pushes through the diaphragm beside the esophagus while the junction stays in place.\n- Surgery is needed in about 15% of clients (handout); the operation is **Nissen fundoplication**.\n\n## Assessment\n- Heartburn, regurgitation, dysphagia, a sense of fullness after eating, and chest pain.\n- Chest pain: rule out cardiac causes before assuming it is hernia (Maslow: circulation).\n- Diagnosis: x-ray, barium swallow, fluoroscopy.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Chest pain: assess for cardiac cause first, because the symptoms mimic a heart attack.\n2. Teach **small, frequent meals**.\n3. Teach not to recline for 1 hour after eating and to raise the head of the bed.\n4. Give prescribed antacids, H2 blockers or proton pump inhibitors as for [[Gastroesophageal Reflux Disease (GERD)]].\n5. Post-surgery: monitor breathing and swallowing and follow the surgeon's diet progression (standard teaching).\n\n## Treatment and medications (general terms)\nSame drugs as GERD: antacids, H2 receptor antagonists, proton pump inhibitors; surgical repair when symptoms persist or complications develop.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nStrangulation or obstruction of the stomach (severe pain, vomiting, inability to swallow), bleeding, aspiration (standard teaching).\n\n## Client teaching\nEat small meals, avoid tight clothing and bending after meals, avoid heavy lifting and straining (standard), lose weight if overweight, limit caffeine, alcohol and spicy foods, raise the head of the bed.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n\"Best position after eating?\" Upright; avoid reclining for an hour. \"Which meal pattern?\" Small frequent meals.\n\n## Memory aid\nSliding = \"slips up and down with reflux\"; Paraesophageal = \"pushes in beside the pipe\".\n\n## Related topics\n[[Barrett's Esophagus]], [[Esophagogastroduodenoscopy (EGD) Nursing Care]], [[Upper and Lower GI Series (Barium Studies)]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nWeight loss, avoiding straining, and strangulation warning signs are standard teaching, not in the handout. Check which type usually needs surgery in your textbook.",

"area": "NP4",
"title": "Gastritis",
"body": "## What it is\nInflammation of the stomach lining.\n\n## Causes\nRepeated irritants (highly seasoned food), overuse of **aspirin and NSAIDs**, excess alcohol, bile reflux, radiation therapy, swallowing strong acid or alkali, and the bacterium ***Helicobacter pylori***.\n\n## Manifestations\nAbdominal discomfort, headache, lassitude, nausea and vomiting, hiccupping, heartburn after eating, belching, intolerance of spicy or fatty foods, and vitamin B12 deficiency (the damaged lining loses intrinsic factor).\n\n## Assessment and diagnosis\n- Low or absent stomach acid (hypochlorhydria, achlorhydria).\n- Upper GI series or endoscopy; biopsy of tissue.\n\n## Treatment\n- H2 blockers, PPIs, and antibiotics (amoxicillin, clarithromycin) when H. pylori is present.\n- Surgery rarely: gastrojejunostomy (joining jejunum to the stomach to bypass the pylorus).\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- Remove the cause: no alcohol, spicy or fatty foods, aspirin or NSAIDs until symptoms settle; discourage caffeine.\n- **Watch for hemorrhagic gastritis**: hematemesis, tachycardia, hypotension. Report at once (bleeding is a circulation emergency).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Peptic Ulcer Disease: Gastric versus Duodenal Ulcer]], [[Peptic Ulcer Drugs and Zollinger-Ellison Syndrome]], [[Digestion, Absorption and Digestive Enzymes]]",

"id": "np4-ge-peptic-ulcer-gastric-duodenal",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Peptic Ulcer Disease: Gastric versus Duodenal Ulcer",
"body": "## What it is\nA sore (excavation) in the lining of the stomach, pylorus or duodenum. Main cause: H. pylori infection. Contributors: excess acid from caffeine, spicy food, smoking, alcohol, NSAIDs and stress.\n\n## Duodenal versus gastric ulcer (the exam favorite)\n- **Duodenal:** age 30-60; about 80% of peptic ulcers; acid **hypersecretion**; possible weight gain; pain 2-3 hours after meals and **relieved by eating**; vomiting uncommon; hemorrhage less likely; melena more common than hematemesis; malignancy rare.\n- **Gastric:** usually age 50 and over; about 15% of peptic ulcers; normal or low acid; **weight loss**; pain 30-60 minutes after meals and **aggravated by eating**; vomiting common; hemorrhage more likely; hematemesis more common than melena; cancer possible occasionally.\n- Risk factors listed: alcohol, smoking, stress, H. pylori (duodenal); H. pylori, gastritis, alcohol, NSAIDs, stress (gastric).\n\n## Memory aid (made up)\nDuodenal = \"Dines, Dulls\" (eating relieves it, pain returns hours later, client gains weight). Gastric = \"Gets worse with Grub\" (eating hurts, client loses weight).\n\n## Nursing management\n- Stress reduction and rest, stop smoking.\n- Avoid foods and drinks that irritate: alcohol, coffee, milk, spicy foods, soft drinks, tea, NSAIDs, aspirin.\n- Watch for bleeding (black tarry stools, vomiting blood) and perforation (sudden severe pain, rigid abdomen), which need emergency action.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Gastritis]], [[Peptic Ulcer Drugs and Zollinger-Ellison Syndrome]], [[Gastric Surgeries: Vagotomy, Pyloroplasty and Billroth]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nMilk was once advised for ulcers but is now often discouraged because it stimulates acid; the handout lists it as an irritant.",

"id": "np4-ge-peptic-ulcer-drugs-zes",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Peptic Ulcer Drugs and Zollinger-Ellison Syndrome",
"body": "## Drug groups\n- **H2 blockers** (ranitidine, cimetidine): reduce acid by blocking histamine.\n- **Proton pump inhibitors** (omeprazole): strongest acid reduction.\n- **Antibiotics** to clear H. pylori.\n- **Antacids:** neutralize acid already made.\n- **Cytoprotectants** (sucralfate, misoprostol): sucralfate becomes a thick, sticky layer in acid that coats the ulcer base and blocks digestion by pepsin.\n\n## Zollinger-Ellison syndrome\nSevere, recurring peptic ulcers plus extreme stomach acidity caused by **gastrin-secreting tumors** (benign or malignant).\n\n## Nursing points\n- Give sucralfate on an empty stomach and separate it from other drugs and antacids as ordered (standard teaching, not in the handout).\n- Reinforce triggers to avoid: NSAIDs, alcohol, smoking.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n\"Which drug protects the ulcer surface?\" Sucralfate. \"Tumor causing extreme acid and ulcers?\" Zollinger-Ellison.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Peptic Ulcer Disease: Gastric versus Duodenal Ulcer]], [[Gastric Surgeries: Vagotomy, Pyloroplasty and Billroth]], [[Gastritis]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nRanitidine has been withdrawn in many countries over a contaminant concern; check which H2 blockers your syllabus expects. Misoprostol is a prostaglandin analogue and is dangerous in pregnancy (my addition).",

"id": "np4-ge-gastric-surgeries-billroth",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Gastric Surgeries: Vagotomy, Pyloroplasty and Billroth",
"body": "## When surgery is used\nFor ulcers that do not respond to drugs, bleed, perforate or block the outlet. The aim is to cut acid or remove the acid-making area.\n\n## The operations\n- **Vagotomy and pyloroplasty:** cut the vagus nerve branches that stimulate acid secretion, and widen the pylorus so the stomach empties well.\n- **Billroth I (gastroduodenostomy):** remove the lower antrum (the area with gastrin-secreting cells) and a bit of duodenum; join the remaining stomach to the duodenum.\n- **Billroth II (gastrojejunostomy):** remove the lower stomach and join it to the jejunum; the duodenal stump is closed (oversewn).\n- **Gastrojejunostomy** alone bypasses the pylorus.\n\n## Memory aid (common, not from handout)\nBillroth I joins to the first part (duodenum); Billroth II joins to the second part further down (jejunum).\n\n## Complications to expect\nRapid gastric emptying causes [[Dumping Syndrome]] after these operations. Also watch for bleeding and leaks.\n\n## Nursing focus\n- After surgery check vital signs, bleeding, and nasogastric tube drainage as ordered (my addition).\n- Teach small frequent dry meals, as in dumping syndrome.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Peptic Ulcer Disease: Gastric versus Duodenal Ulcer]], [[Peptic Ulcer Drugs and Zollinger-Ellison Syndrome]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nPostoperative gastric care (NG tube care, diet progression) is not in the handout; use your surgical nursing text.",

"id": "np4-ge-dumping-syndrome",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Dumping Syndrome",
"body": "## What it is\nA group of GI and circulatory symptoms after gastric surgery or vagotomy. Food leaves the stomach too fast (rapid gastric emptying), so it is poorly mixed with pancreatic and bile secretions.\n\n## Manifestations (about 30 minutes after eating)\n- Nausea, vomiting, abdominal fullness and cramping, diarrhea, borborygmi.\n- Palpitations, tachycardia, sweating, weakness, dizziness.\n- Steatorrhea (fat in stool).\n\n## Priority nursing actions and teaching\n- **Lie down after meals** (slows emptying and helps blood pressure).\n- Avoid sugar, salt and milk; keep carbohydrates low and avoid concentrated sweets; fat is allowed as tolerated.\n- Antispasmodic drugs may be ordered to slow emptying.\n- **Do not drink fluids with meals.** Drink up to 1 hour before or 1 hour after. Meals should contain more dry foods than liquids.\n- Small, frequent meals (standard advice).\n\n## Why it works\nLiquids and sugary food are the fastest to wash through the stomach and pull water into the bowel, causing cramps, diarrhea and a drop in circulating volume.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n\"Which statement shows the client understands?\" Correct answer: \"I will lie down after eating and drink fluids between meals.\"\n\n## Related topics\n[[Gastric Surgeries: Vagotomy, Pyloroplasty and Billroth]], [[Peptic Ulcer Disease: Gastric versus Duodenal Ulcer]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nLate dumping (low blood sugar 1-3 hours after eating) is in most textbooks but not this handout.",

"id": "np4-ge-diverticular-disease",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Diverticulosis and Diverticulitis",
"body": "## What it is\nA diverticulum is a small pouch of bowel lining pushed through a weak spot in the muscle layer. Most are in the **sigmoid colon**.\n- **Diverticulosis:** many pouches, no inflammation or symptoms.\n- **Diverticulitis:** pouches inflamed or infected, because food and bacteria are trapped.\n\n## Manifestations\nIrregular bowel with spells of diarrhea, nausea, anorexia, bloating, narrow stools, constipation (sometimes obstruction), and signs of infection (fever, raised WBC, left lower quadrant pain, my addition).\n\n## Diagnosis\nColonoscopy, barium enema, abdominal x-rays, and **CT scan, the test of choice for diverticulitis** (can show abscesses).\n\n## Treatment\n- Antibiotics, analgesics and anticholinergics for spasm.\n- The handout says meperidine for pain and says **morphine is contraindicated** because it raises pressure inside the colon.\n- Avoid lifting, straining, coughing and bending (raise abdominal pressure).\n- Diet: soft, **high fiber** for diverticulosis; **low fiber** during an acute diverticulitis flare until infection settles. Avoid gas-forming foods.\n- Surgery: colon resection with primary anastomosis; temporary or permanent colostomy if inflammation is severe.\n\n## Complications to monitor\nPerforation, hemorrhage, fistula, abscess, peritonitis.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Peritonitis]], [[Appendicitis]], [[Inflammatory Bowel Disease Treatment and Ileostomy Surgeries]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nOpioid choice varies between sources (the old meperidine-over-morphine rule is debated); use the teaching your school uses. Colonoscopy is usually avoided during acute inflammation (my addition).",

"area": "NP4",
"title": "Crohn's Disease",
"body": "## What it is\nLong-term inflammation that goes through **all layers** of the bowel wall (transmural). It most often affects the ileum and colon but can occur anywhere from mouth to anus. It leads to thickening, scarring, narrowing, ulcers, **fistulas** and abscesses, giving a classic **cobblestone** look. Areas can be affected with normal bowel between them (skip lesions, my addition).\n\n## Manifestations\n- Fever and raised WBC.\n- Cramping, colicky pain **after meals**.\n- Semi-solid diarrhea, sometimes with mucus or pus.\n- Abdominal distention, anorexia, nausea, vomiting, weight loss.\n- Anemia, dehydration, electrolyte imbalance.\n\n## Compare with ulcerative colitis (made-up contrast)\n- Crohn's: whole thickness, patchy, pain after meals, weight loss, fistulas.\n- UC: surface layers only, continuous from the rectum upward, bloody liquid stools. See [[Ulcerative Colitis]].\n\n## Priority nursing actions\nRelieve inflammation (drugs), correct fluid and electrolyte loss, support nutrition, and watch for perforation, peritonitis and bleeding. Details are in [[Inflammatory Bowel Disease Treatment and Ileostomy Surgeries]].\n\n## Related topics\n[[Ulcerative Colitis]], [[Diverticulosis and Diverticulitis]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nSkip lesions and pain-after-meals mechanism are partly my additions. Diagnostic tests for Crohn's are not listed separately in the handout.",

"body": "## What it is\nRecurring ulceration and inflammation of the **mucosa and submucosa** of the colon and rectum.\n\n## Risk factors in the handout\nHigher prevalence in Caucasians and people of Jewish descent; NSAIDs make inflammatory bowel disease worse.\n\n## Manifestations\n- Anorexia, weight loss, malaise.\n- **Diarrhea, 10-20 liquid stools a day.**\n- Left lower quadrant tenderness and cramping.\n- **Rectal bleeding.**\n- Dehydration and electrolyte imbalance, anemia, hypocalcemia, vitamin K deficiency.\n\n## Diagnosis\nColonoscopy, sigmoidoscopy, barium enema, CBC, abdominal x-ray, stool examination.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- Fluid and electrolyte replacement; NPO during an acute attack with IV fluids.\n- Monitor stool count, weight, hemoglobin and signs of bleeding or perforation.\n- See [[Inflammatory Bowel Disease Treatment and Ileostomy Surgeries]] for drugs, diet and operations.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nA client with bloody diarrhea and left lower cramping: think UC. Which surgery cures it? Total removal of the colon.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Crohn's Disease]], [[Diverticulosis and Diverticulitis]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nUC is limited to the colon and rectum (this handout agrees). Colonoscopy and barium enema are usually avoided in a severe flare (my addition).",

"title": "Inflammatory Bowel Disease Treatment and Ileostomy Surgeries",
"body": "## Drug therapy (priority: relieve inflammation)\n- **Salicylate compounds:** for mild-moderate disease and to prevent relapse in maintenance.\n- **Corticosteroids:** for severe, fulminant disease; oral for outpatients, IV in hospital.\n- **Immunosuppressants:** alter the immune response (exact action in IBD unclear).\n- **Anti-diarrheals:** reduce peristalsis to rest the bowel; continued until stools return near normal.\n\n## Nursing care\n- NPO and IV fluids and electrolytes in acute attacks; parenteral nutrition if ordered.\n- Diet when eating: **low residue, high protein, high calorie**, vitamin supplements and iron.\n- Avoid gas-forming foods.\n- Monitor for perforation, peritonitis and hemorrhage.\n\n## Surgery\n- **Proctocolectomy with permanent ileostomy:** removal of colon and rectum; an ileal stoma on the abdominal wall.\n- **Kock (continent) ileostomy:** a pouch made from distal ileum with a stoma that is emptied by catheter.\n- **Restorative proctocolectomy:** the procedure of choice when the rectum can be spared; makes an ileal reservoir that acts as a \"new\" rectum, keeping sphincter control, with no permanent ileostomy.\n- **Ileoanal anastomosis:** ileum pouch connected to the anus after removing the colon and rectal mucosa.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Crohn's Disease]], [[Ulcerative Colitis]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout is vague about names for pouch operations; \"ileorectostomy\" in textbooks usually means ileum joined to the rectum, not an ileoanal pouch. "title": "Appendicitis",
"body": "## What it is\nInflammation of the appendix, a finger-like pouch about 10 cm long attached to the cecum below the ileocecal valve. Most common between ages 10 and 30. It is blocked or kinked by a **fecalith**, tumor or foreign body.\n\n## Assessment findings\n- Vague epigastric or periumbilical pain that moves to sharp, well-localized **right lower quadrant** pain.\n- Low-grade fever, nausea and vomiting, loss of appetite.\n- **Rebound tenderness** (pain on release of pressure).\n- **Rovsing's sign:** pressing the left lower quadrant causes pain in the right lower quadrant.\n- Raised WBC; imaging by x-ray, ultrasound or CT (right lower quadrant density). Pregnancy test rules out ectopic pregnancy.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- Suspect appendicitis, keep NPO and report; do not give laxatives or apply heat (standard teaching, my addition) because they may cause rupture.\n- IV fluids, antibiotics, analgesics as ordered.\n- **Appendectomy** as soon as possible to prevent perforation. If abscess, place a drain.\n- Watch for sudden relief of pain then fever and rigid abdomen: perforation.\n\n## After surgery\n- **High Fowler's position** to reduce tension on the incision and organs.\n- Teach: sutures removed on day 5-7, incision care, avoid heavy lifting; normal activity within 2-4 weeks.\n\n## Complications\nPerforation, [[Peritonitis]], abscess, portal pylephlebitis (septic thrombosis of the portal vein).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Digestive Tract Parts and Abdominal Regions]], [[Diverticulosis and Diverticulitis]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nMcBurney's point and the no-laxative/no-heat rule are standard but not in the handout. Some books say semi-Fowler's after surgery.",

"title": "Hemorrhoids",
"body": "## What it is\nSwollen veins in the anal canal.\n- **Internal:** above the internal sphincter; usually painless until they bleed or prolapse.\n- **External:** outside the external sphincter; painful when thrombosed because of inflammation and edema.\n\n## Causes\nAge around 50, straining or shearing of the lining during defecation, and pressure from pregnancy.\n\n## Manifestations\nItching, pain, **bright red bleeding**.\n\n## Treatment\n- Bulk-forming agents (psyllium), analgesic ointments and suppositories, astringents such as witch hazel.\n- Procedures: infrared photocoagulation, bipolar diathermy, laser, sclerosing injections, rubber-band ligation, cryosurgery, hemorrhoidectomy.\n\n## Nursing management and teaching\n- Good personal hygiene.\n- Avoid straining; **high-residue diet** with fruit and bran; more fluids.\n- **Warm compresses and sitz baths**; bed rest during flare-ups.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n\"Best advice to prevent recurrence?\" Fiber, fluids, no straining. Bright red blood on tissue points to rectal source, while black tarry stool suggests upper GI bleeding.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Portal Hypertension and Ascites]], [[Diverticulosis and Diverticulitis]]",

"id": "np4-ge-hepatobiliary-structure-function",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Liver, Gallbladder and Pancreas Structure and Function",
"body": "## Liver\n- Largest gland of the body, with four lobes: left, right, caudate, quadrate.\n- Cell types include hepatocytes and Kupffer cells.\n- Helps regulate blood glucose by making and storing glycogen in hepatocytes.\n- Converts **ammonia** (from protein breakdown and gut bacteria) into **urea**. Failure of this job explains [[Hepatic Encephalopathy]].\n\n## Gallbladder\nPear-shaped organ under the right lobe of the liver. Normally holds 30-50 mL of bile; up to 70 mL when fully distended. Bile emulsifies fats.\n\n## Pancreas\nSlender, fish-shaped organ lying behind the stomach, from the duodenum toward the spleen. It has both exocrine and endocrine roles. Pancreatic juice contains amylase, lipase and trypsin. The endocrine side (insulin, glucagon) is covered in [[Pancreatic, Kidney and Gonadal Hormones]].\n\n## Why it matters\nLiver, gallbladder and pancreas share the common bile duct opening (ampulla of Vater), so a stone or inflammation in one often involves another, for example gallstone pancreatitis.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Liver Cirrhosis]], [[Cholelithiasis and Cholecystitis]], [[Acute Pancreatitis]]",

"title": "Viral Hepatitis A to E",
"body": "## What it is\nInflammation of liver tissue from viruses (A, B, C, D, E) or toxins and drugs. Damage comes from the immune response and causes local cell death.\n\n## The three stages\n- **Pre-icteric:** vague flu-like illness: malaise, fatigue, headache, muscle aches, anorexia, nausea, vomiting, diarrhea.\n- **Icteric** (days to weeks later): jaundice, dark urine, light-colored stool, steatorrhea, enlarged liver.\n- **Post-icteric (convalescent):** lasts a few weeks; fatigue lessens and appetite returns.\n\n## The five viruses (handout values)\n- **A:** fecal-oral; incubation 15-50 days; usually mild with recovery.\n- **B:** blood, perinatal, sexual; incubation 28-160 days; may be severe.\n- **C:** blood transfusion, sexual; incubation 15-160 days; often becomes chronic, carrier state, liver cancer.\n- **D:** same routes as B; incubation 21-140 days; like B but carrier state likelier (occurs only with B).\n- **E:** fecal-oral; incubation 15-65 days; like A but very severe in pregnant women.\n\n## Diagnosis and management\nLiver function tests and serum and urine bilirubin are raised. Treatment is supportive: immunoglobulins, vaccines (A and B), antivirals, rest, good nutrition, and preventing spread.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\nStandard precautions; enteric (fecal) precautions for A and E; hand washing; blood and body fluid care for B, C, D. Encourage rest and small high-calorie meals.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Liver Cirrhosis]], [[Hepatic Encephalopathy]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nIncubation periods differ between books (for example hepatitis B 45-160 days); use your course numbers. Vaccine mention and precautions are my additions.",

"title": "Liver Cirrhosis",
"body": "## What it is\nChronic liver disease where liver cells are widely destroyed and replaced by scar tissue and irregular regrowth.\n\n## Types\n- **Laennec's (alcoholic):** alcohol plus long-term poor nutrition.\n- **Biliary:** from bile duct disease blocking bile flow.\n- **Post-hepatic (postnecrotic):** after hepatitis.\n\n## Manifestations\nEnlarged, firm liver, chronic dyspepsia, constipation or diarrhea, gradual weight loss, **ascites**, splenomegaly, spider angiomas, **caput medusae**, portal hypertension, mental deterioration.\n\n## Diagnosis\nLiver biopsy, liver scan, liver function tests (ALT, AST), serum proteins, prothrombin time.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- Safety first: confusion risk, so orient the client, limit visitors, raise side rails, assist walking.\n- Reduce ascites with diuretics; protect fragile skin; reduce bleeding risk (antacid or H2 blocker prevents GI bleeding).\n- No alcohol.\n- Nutrition: vitamins and supplements; **high protein early** to help healing; **low protein late** when ammonia rises and encephalopathy threatens.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Portal Hypertension and Ascites]], [[Esophageal Varices and Balloon Tamponade]], [[Hepatic Encephalopathy]], [[Viral Hepatitis A to E]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nMany textbooks no longer restrict protein long term in cirrhosis; follow your syllabus. "id": "np4-ge-portal-hypertension-ascites",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Portal Hypertension and Ascites",
"body": "## What it is\nHigh pressure in the portal vein because blood cannot flow freely through a diseased liver. The blocked flow backs up and causes:\n- Varicose veins in the esophagus and rectum (hemorrhoidal).\n- Fluid collecting in the abdomen (**ascites**).\n- Enlarged spleen.\n\n## Manifestations\nAscites, rapid weight gain, shortness of breath, fluid wave and liver dullness on percussion, dilated veins around the umbilicus (**caput medusae**), enlarged spleen, fluid and electrolyte imbalance.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- Bed rest; diuretics as ordered.\n- **Measure abdominal girth and weigh daily** (same time, same scale) to follow fluid change.\n- Reduce edema: elevate head of bed for breathing, restrict sodium (standard teaching).\n- Assist with **paracentesis** (client empties bladder first, my addition).\n- Monitor serum ammonia and electrolytes.\n\n## Why shortness of breath?\nFluid pushes up on the diaphragm. Position to ease breathing, a Maslow (oxygenation) priority.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Liver Cirrhosis]], [[Esophageal Varices and Balloon Tamponade]], [[Hepatic Encephalopathy]]",

"body": "## What it is\nSwollen, twisted veins in the lower esophagus caused by portal hypertension. They can rupture and bleed massively. Alcohol history is common.\n\n## Manifestations\n**Hematemesis and melena**, signs of massive hemorrhage, plus signs of cirrhosis (hepatic encephalopathy, ascites, dilated abdominal veins).\n\n## Diagnosis\nEndoscopy; ALT, AST, bilirubin raised; portal pressure measurements.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. **Airway, breathing, circulation:** bleeding is life-threatening. Monitor level of consciousness, vital signs, urine output, and give blood as ordered.\n2. Check for other bleeding: bruises, nosebleeds, petechiae, bleeding gums.\n3. **Sengstaken-Blakemore tube** (balloon tamponade): four openings for gastric aspiration, esophageal aspiration, gastric balloon inflation, esophageal balloon inflation.\n   - Keep **scissors at the bedside** to cut the tube if the client has breathing difficulty (it can migrate up and block the airway).\n   - ICU monitoring; prevent the client pulling on or displacing the tube.\n4. **Vasopressin** is listed as initial drug therapy.\n5. **Sclerotherapy:** afterwards watch for bleeding, esophageal perforation, aspiration pneumonia, stricture.\n6. **Variceal band ligation:** elastic band placed on the varix through an endoscope; complications are ulceration, dysphagia, chest discomfort, strictures.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Portal Hypertension and Ascites]], [[Liver Cirrhosis]], [[Hepatic Encephalopathy]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nToday octreotide is often used instead of vasopressin, and beta blockers prevent rebleeding (my addition). "id": "np4-ge-hepatic-encephalopathy",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Hepatic Encephalopathy",
"body": "## What it is\nA brain syndrome caused by liver failure. The failing liver cannot turn ammonia into urea, so ammonia reaches the brain and depresses the central nervous system.\n\n## Triggers raising ammonia\nGI bleeding, high-protein diet, bacterial growth in the gut, uremia (and severe liver damage itself).\n\n## Manifestations\n- Mental changes from slight confusion to coma.\n- **Asterixis (liver flap):** with arms out and hands dorsiflexed, the hands drop and snap back.\n- **Fetor hepaticus:** sweet, slightly fecal breath odor.\n- Constructional apraxia: deteriorating handwriting, cannot copy a star.\n- Raised ammonia and bilirubin, prolonged prothrombin time.\n\n## Management\n- **Lactulose:** keeps ammonia in the ionized state (ammonium) and lowers colon pH, causes bowel evacuation, and changes gut bacteria so less ammonia is made.\n- **Neomycin:** reduces ammonia-producing bacteria in the colon.\n- Check ammonia daily; monitor neurological status for impending coma.\n- Reduce or stop dietary protein if coma is near.\n- Use **conservative doses** of sedatives or analgesics; the damaged liver cannot clear drugs.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\nSafety and airway first in a drowsy client, then treat the ammonia source (bleeding, constipation, protein load).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Liver Cirrhosis]], [[Esophageal Varices and Balloon Tamponade]]",

"body": "## Definitions\n- **Cholelithiasis:** stones (calculi) in the gallbladder, caused by changes in bile makeup or bile stasis (infection, cirrhosis, pancreatitis).\n- **Cholecystitis:** acute or chronic gallbladder inflammation, usually from a stone blocking the cystic duct; also from trauma, burns, surgery, gram-negative sepsis or too much opioid use.\n\n## Risk factors\nObesity, women with multiple pregnancies, certain ethnic groups (Native American, Southwestern US Hispanic), frequent weight change or rapid weight loss, high-dose estrogen, ileal disease or resection, cystic fibrosis, diabetes.\n\n## Manifestations\n- Stones: episodic cramping pain in the **right upper quadrant** or epigastrium, sometimes to the back near the right shoulder blade; nausea, vomiting, fat intolerance, epigastric distress, fever, jaundice.\n- Cholecystitis: biliary colic, RUQ tenderness and rigidity, **Murphy's sign** (pain and breath-holding when you press under the right rib margin as the client breathes in), fever, nausea, heartburn, flatulence, vitamin deficiency.\n\n## Diagnosis\nAbdominal x-ray; **ultrasound is the test of choice**; cholescintigraphy (IV radioactive agent); cholecystography (iodine contrast); ERCP.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Gallstone Treatment and Biliary Drainage Care]], [[Acute Pancreatitis]], [[ERCP, Colonoscopy and Laparoscopy]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nMemory aid for risk (common, not from handout): fat, female, fertile, forty. "title": "Gallstone Treatment and Biliary Drainage Care",
"body": "## Medical measures\n- **Ursodeoxycholic acid (UDCA)** dissolves small radiolucent stones.\n- Analgesics (morphine in the handout) and antacids.\n- Diet: low-fat liquids, high in protein and carbohydrate.\n\n## Stone fragmentation (non-surgical)\n- **Intracorporeal lithotripsy:** laser pulses break stones in gallbladder or common bile duct.\n- **Extracorporeal shock wave lithotripsy:** repeated shock waves from outside the body.\n\n## Surgery\n- **Laparoscopic cholecystectomy:** small punctures, one at the umbilicus.\n- **Open cholecystectomy:** gallbladder removed after the cystic duct and artery are tied; a drain may be left near the gallbladder if bile leaks (removed after about 24 hours). **Bile duct injury is a serious complication.**\n- **Choledochostomy:** incision in the common bile duct to remove stones.\n\n## Biliary drainage tube care\n- Bile that does not drain may back up into the liver and blood (obstruction).\n- The tube or collection bag may be kept **above abdominal level** if ordered, to prevent bile loss.\n- Every 24 hours measure the amount and record color and character of bile.\n- After several days the tube may be clamped 1 hour before and after meals so bile reaches the duodenum.\n- Tube is removed in about 7-14 days.\n- Protect skin around the tube from irritation by bile.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Cholelithiasis and Cholecystitis]], [[Acute Pancreatitis]], [[ERCP, Colonoscopy and Laparoscopy]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nMany textbooks place bile drainage bags at or below abdominal level; follow the surgeon's order. "area": "NP4",
"title": "Acute Pancreatitis",
"body": "## What it is\nThe pancreas digests itself with its own enzymes, mainly **trypsin**. It ranges from mild, self-limited illness to fatal hemorrhagic pancreatitis. Causes: alcoholism, gallbladder disease (cholecystitis), surgery on or near the pancreas.\n\n## Manifestations\n- Abdominal tenderness with back pain.\n- Nausea, vomiting, diarrhea, steatorrhea.\n- Fever, jaundice, mental confusion.\n- Hypotension and signs of hypovolemia.\n- Bleeding signs: **Cullen's sign** (bluish periumbilical discoloration) and **Turner's sign** (flank or back discoloration).\n\n## Diagnosis\nElevated **amylase and lipase**, raised WBC, **hypocalcemia**.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Pain relief (the handout names morphine as the drug of choice).\n2. **NPO** to rest the pancreas and stop enzyme release.\n3. Maintain fluids and electrolytes (hypovolemia is a main danger; Maslow circulation).\n4. Medicines: H2 blockers or PPIs to cut acid, which stimulates the pancreas.\n5. Later: promote nutrition.\n6. Watch calcium: hypocalcemia signs include tetany, Chvostek's and Trousseau's signs.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Chronic Pancreatitis]], [[Cholelithiasis and Cholecystitis]], [[Hypoparathyroidism and Post-Thyroidectomy Tetany]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nOlder textbooks prefer meperidine (morphine can cause spasm of the sphincter of Oddi); the handout says morphine. "title": "Chronic Pancreatitis",
"body": "## What it is\nLong-lasting inflammation that causes permanent damage to pancreas tissue, from repeated acute attacks. More than half of cases involve alcoholism: long-term alcohol makes pancreatic juice rich in protein, which plugs ducts and forms stones.\n\n## Manifestations\n- Recurring severe upper abdominal and back pain.\n- Weight loss and anorexia.\n- **Steatorrhea:** frequent, frothy, foul-smelling, fatty stools from poor fat digestion.\n\n## Diagnosis\nRaised lipase and amylase, raised WBC, ERCP to show pancreatic calcification, abnormal glucose tolerance test (insulin-making cells damaged).\n\n## Management\n- Pancreatic enzymes with meals, non-opioid pain relievers, antacids, H2 blockers, PPIs.\n- Relieve pain, promote comfort, treat new attacks.\n- **Avoid alcohol, caffeine** and foods that cause discomfort.\n- Treat diabetes with diet and insulin or oral agents.\n- Surgery: pancreatic jejunostomy (Roux-en-Y) to drain the duct, or a Whipple resection (pancreaticoduodenectomy) for pain.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Acute Pancreatitis]], [[Diabetes Mellitus: Diagnosis and Types]]",

"title": "Peritonitis",
"body": "## What it is\nInflammation of the peritoneum, the membrane lining the abdominal cavity and covering organs. Usually bacterial.\n\n## Causes\nInfection, trauma, spread from an outside organ, appendicitis, perforated ulcer, diverticulitis, bowel perforation, abdominal surgery, peritoneal dialysis.\n\n## Manifestations\n- Diffuse pain that becomes constant and localized, worst over the site of irritation.\n- **Rigid, tender abdomen** and rebound tenderness.\n- Paralytic ileus, anorexia, nausea, vomiting.\n- Fever and rapid pulse.\n\n## Diagnosis\nRaised WBC; altered potassium, sodium and chloride; x-ray shows distended bowel loops.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- Treat the dehydration: fluid and colloid replacement.\n- Analgesics, antiemetics, antibiotics, oxygen by nasal cannula or mask.\n- **Intestinal intubation and suction** to relieve distention and restore bowel function.\n- Position on the side with knees flexed to reduce abdominal tension.\n- Protect drains from being pulled out.\n- Surgery to remove or repair the cause (excision, resection, repair, drain abscess).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Appendicitis]], [[Diverticulosis and Diverticulitis]], [[Peptic Ulcer Disease: Gastric versus Duodenal Ulcer]]",

"area": "NP4",
"title": "Hypothalamus and Pituitary Hormones",
"body": "## Basics\n- Glands release hormones straight into the blood; hormones act on target cells with matching receptors.\n- Hormones regulate and integrate metabolism and work together with the nervous system.\n\n## Hypothalamus\nMakes releasing and inhibiting hormones that control the pituitary: CRH, TRH, GHRH, GnRH.\n\n## Pituitary (hypophysis, the \"master gland\")\n- **Anterior:** growth hormone (GH: bone and muscle growth, protein synthesis, fat use, less carbohydrate use); ACTH (stimulates adrenal cortex); TSH (thyroid); FSH (female: follicle growth and ovulation; male: sperm production); LH (female: corpus luteum, ovulation, estrogen and progesterone; male: testosterone); prolactin (prepares breasts for lactation); MSH.\n- **Posterior:** **ADH (vasopressin)** makes the kidney reabsorb water; **oxytocin** contracts the pregnant uterus and ejects milk.\n\n## Links to diseases\nToo much GH: [[Gigantism]], [[Acromegaly]]. Too little: [[Pituitary Dwarfism and Hyperprolactinemia]]. ADH problems: [[SIADH (Syndrome of Inappropriate ADH)]] (too much) and [[Diabetes Insipidus]] (too little).\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout labels \"somatostatin\" together with growth hormone; somatostatin is a separate hormone that inhibits GH and TSH release. Treat GH and somatostatin as different.",

"id": "np4-ge-adrenal-thyroid-parathyroid-hormones",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Adrenal, Thyroid and Parathyroid Hormones",
"body": "## Adrenal cortex (stimulated by ACTH)\n- **Aldosterone** (mineralocorticoid): keeps sodium, loses potassium in the kidney.\n- **Cortisol** (glucocorticoid): affects all nutrients, raises blood glucose, anti-inflammatory, helps with stress.\n- Adrenal androgens: weak; turned into testosterone elsewhere.\n\n## Adrenal medulla\nReleases **epinephrine and norepinephrine** (sympathetic \"fight or flight\"): epinephrine turns liver glycogen into glucose and raises cardiac output; norepinephrine causes strong vasoconstriction.\n\n## Thyroid (butterfly-shaped, lower neck)\n- **T3 and T4** raise metabolic rate, protein and bone turnover. Most T3 comes from conversion of T4.\n- **Calcitonin** lowers blood calcium and phosphate and blocks bone breakdown, released when calcium is high.\n\n## Parathyroid (usually four, behind the thyroid)\n**Parathormone (PTH)** raises blood calcium by drawing it from bone, kidney and intestines. They can be removed by accident in neck surgery, causing [[Hypoparathyroidism and Post-Thyroidectomy Tetany]].\n\n## Quick opposites\nCalcitonin down, PTH up for calcium. Insulin down, glucagon up for glucose.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Addison's Disease]], [[Cushing's Syndrome]], [[Hyperthyroidism and Graves' Disease]], [[Hyperparathyroidism]]",

"id": "np4-ge-pancreas-kidney-gonad-hormones",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Pancreatic, Kidney and Gonadal Hormones",
"body": "## What it is\nThe hormones made by the pancreatic islet cells, the kidneys, the testes and the ovaries. Together with the pituitary, thyroid and adrenal hormones they regulate glucose, blood cells, calcium, blood pressure, and sexual development.\n\n## How to reason about it\nFor each hormone ask: where is it made, what does it raise or lower, and what disease results when it is too high or too low. Insulin and glucagon are opposites that keep glucose steady; kidney hormones link kidney failure to anemia, weak bones and high blood pressure. The trap: forgetting the kidney as an endocrine organ. A client with chronic kidney disease often has anemia and bone disease for hormonal reasons.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- **Pancreas** (slender organ lying horizontally behind the stomach; both exocrine and endocrine):\n  - **Glucagon (alpha cells):** raises blood glucose by glycogenolysis (breaking down stored glucose) and gluconeogenesis (making new glucose from amino acids and other substances).\n  - **Insulin (beta cells):** lowers blood glucose by helping glucose cross cell membranes into muscle, liver and fat cells.\n  - **Somatostatin (delta cells):** delays intestinal absorption of glucose.\n- **Kidney** (paired organs behind the peritoneum, between the 12th thoracic and 3rd lumbar vertebrae):\n  - **1,25-dihydroxy vitamin D:** increases calcium absorption from the gut.\n  - **Renin:** starts the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system.\n  - **Erythropoietin:** increases red blood cell production.\n- **Testes** (two almond-shaped organs in the scrotum): testosterone, which drives male organ development, secondary sex traits and sperm production.\n- **Ovaries** (two almond-shaped organs in the anterior pelvis): estrogen (female organs and secondary traits) and progesterone (regulates the uterine lining and maintains pregnancy).\n\n## Assessment\nLink the hormone to the finding: low insulin -> high glucose; low erythropoietin -> anemia in kidney failure; low vitamin D activation -> low calcium and bone disease; low estrogen -> amenorrhea; high or low testosterone -> changes in sexual development and function.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- Glucose problems: check blood glucose first and treat the abnormal level ([[Hypoglycemia in Diabetes]], [[Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA)]]).\n- Kidney hormone problems: monitor hemoglobin, calcium and blood pressure, and give erythropoietin or vitamin D as ordered (see [[Chronic Kidney Disease: Findings by Body System]]).\n- Reproductive hormone problems: refer to the proper clinic and offer privacy and support.\n\n## Treatment and medications (general terms)\nInsulin for diabetes; erythropoietin and vitamin D for kidney failure; sex hormone replacement as prescribed.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nSevere glucose swings, anemia, bone pain and fractures, osteoporosis.\n\n## Client teaching\nExplain why insulin is needed, how to prevent lows and highs, and why kidney patients take vitamin D and iron.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nMatch the hormone to its source (beta cells -> insulin) or to its effect (glucagon -> raises glucose; erythropoietin -> red cells).\n\n## Memory aid\nAlpha = Always raise (glucagon). Beta = Bring it down (insulin).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Diabetes Mellitus: Diagnosis and Types]], [[Insulin Types: Onset, Peak and Duration]], [[Hypothalamus and Pituitary Hormones]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout does not give disease links for the gonadal hormones. Read your reproductive and renal notes for detail.",

"title": "Gigantism",
"body": "## What it is\nToo much growth hormone in **children**, before the growth plates close, so the person grows very tall (7-8 feet). Noticed around puberty. Head, hands and feet enlarge.\n\n## Cause\nUsually a pituitary tumor of GH-making cells, which can raise intracranial pressure.\n\n## Manifestations\nVery tall, weak and lethargic, severe headaches, visual disturbance, double vision, loss of color discrimination, bone decalcification.\n\n## Diagnosis\nCT, MRI, serum pituitary hormone levels.\n\n## Treatment\n- Bromocriptine (handout: dopamine antagonist) and octreotide (Sandostatin).\n- Surgery: hypophysectomy; stereotactic radiation therapy.\n\n## Nursing care\nRecord height and head circumference, perioperative and radiation care, and help the child interact normally with peers (body image).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Acromegaly]], [[Hypophysectomy Nursing Care]], [[Pituitary Dwarfism and Hyperprolactinemia]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nBromocriptine is a dopamine agonist, and octreotide is a somatostatin analogue (it lowers GH); the handout states both differently.",

"id": "np4-ge-acromegaly",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Acromegaly",
"body": "## What it is\nToo much growth hormone in **adults** (growth plates already closed). Bones thicken in width, soft tissues and organs enlarge, but height does not increase.\n\n## Manifestations\nBroad skull, protruding jaw (prognathism), larger hands and feet, thick heel pads, heavy lips, enlarged tongue, coarse features, enlarged heart, brain and organs.\n\n## Diagnosis\nCT, MRI, serum pituitary hormones.\n\n## Treatment\nBromocriptine, octreotide, hypophysectomy, stereotactic radiation.\n\n## Nursing management\nPrepare for irradiation or surgery. After surgery monitor for hemorrhage, transient diabetes insipidus, **clear nasal drainage (rhinorrhea = possible cerebrospinal fluid leak)**, adrenal and thyroid insufficiency, meningitis (fever, stiff neck, headache), visual changes. Also watch for high blood sugar, heart and neurologic problems.\n\n## Exam angle\nGigantism = child, tall. Acromegaly = adult, bone widening, no height gain.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Gigantism]], [[Hypophysectomy Nursing Care]], [[Diabetes Insipidus]]",

"title": "Hypophysectomy Nursing Care",
"body": "## What it is\nPartial or complete removal of the pituitary gland, via transfrontal, subcranial or oronasal transsphenoidal approach.\n\n## Before surgery\nTeach deep breathing (coughing and straining afterwards are restricted).\n\n## After surgery (priority points)\n- **Raise head of bed** (handout: at least 2 weeks) to reduce pressure on the sella turcica and encourage drainage.\n- Avoid activities that raise intracranial pressure (straining, coughing, bending).\n- **Watch nasal drainage:** post-nasal drip, and test for glucose; glucose-positive clear fluid suggests CSF leak, which risks meningitis.\n- Monitor intake and output and daily weight (watch for diabetes insipidus).\n- Blood glucose monitoring and insulin therapy if needed; ulcer medicine; check stools for blood.\n- Warm saline mouth rinses (no vigorous toothbrushing, my addition).\n- Lifelong hormone replacement is needed if the whole gland is removed (my addition).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Acromegaly]], [[Diabetes Insipidus]], [[Gigantism]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nRanking of precautions varies; confirm with neurosurgical nursing text.",

"id": "np4-ge-pituitary-dwarfism-hyperprolactinemia",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Pituitary Dwarfism and Hyperprolactinemia",
"body": "## What it is\nTwo anterior pituitary disorders. **Dwarfism** is generalized limited growth from too little growth hormone (GH) during childhood. **Hyperprolactinemia** is too much prolactin, usually from a prolactin-secreting tumor.\n\n## How to reason about it\nCause -> finding -> action. Low GH while the growth plates are open -> short stature with childlike proportions and delayed puberty -> replace GH early and support the child's body image. High prolactin -> suppresses the reproductive axis -> amenorrhea and galactorrhea in women, gynecomastia and low sex drive in men -> find and treat the tumor. The trap: mixing dwarfism with gigantism. Too little GH in a child is dwarfism; too much GH in a child is gigantism; too much GH in an adult is acromegaly.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- GH comes from the anterior pituitary and drives bone and muscle growth, protein building and fat use (see [[Hypothalamus and Pituitary Hormones]]).\n- Prolactin prepares the breast for milk production.\n\n## Assessment\nDwarfism (handout): overweight for height, underdeveloped jaw, abnormal tooth position, high voice, delayed puberty. Tests: x-ray, CT and MRI, blood tests.\nHyperprolactinemia (handout): women have amenorrhea and galactorrhea; men have gynecomastia, decreased sex drive and impotence.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Assess and record height, weight and development on growth charts (standard teaching).\n2. Give prescribed GH replacement correctly (somatrem [Protropin], somatropin [Humatrope] in the handout).\n3. Provide psychological support and acceptance of body image change, because the child may be teased.\n4. Help with walking if needed and avoid high-impact activities (handout).\n5. For a prolactin tumor, prepare for tests and possible treatment as ordered (see [[Hypophysectomy Nursing Care]]).\n\n## Treatment and medications (general terms)\nGH replacement for dwarfism. Prolactin-secreting tumors are treated with drugs, surgery or radiation depending on the case; the handout does not list the drugs.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nPsychosocial problems and delayed puberty in dwarfism; headache and visual changes if a pituitary tumor presses on nearby structures (standard teaching).\n\n## Client teaching\nExplain that treatment works best when begun early, give injection technique to the family, encourage normal peer activity and school, and offer counseling.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n\"A child who is short for age and overweight for height\" points to GH deficiency. \"A woman with no periods and milk from the breasts\" points to prolactin excess.\n\n## Memory aid\nProlactin = \"Lactation\": too much gives milk where it should not be.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Gigantism]], [[Acromegaly]], [[Hypothalamus and Pituitary Hormones]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout lists only the findings, not the cause of hyperprolactinemia beyond tumors or its drug treatment. Check drug names and drug causes in your textbook.",

"area": "NP4",
"title": "SIADH (Syndrome of Inappropriate ADH)",
"body": "## What it is\nToo much ADH even though the blood is too dilute. The kidneys hold water, urine cannot be diluted, and **dilutional hyponatremia** develops. Mnemonic (made up): SIADH = Salt Is Absent, water Held.\n\n## Causes\nLung cancer (bronchogenic), severe pneumonia, pneumothorax, other malignancies, head injury, brain surgery or tumor, infection, some drugs.\n\n## Findings\n- Low serum osmolality (<280 mOsm/kg) and low sodium; **urine sodium and osmolality high**; ADH high.\n- Decreased urine output, **weight gain**, headache, confusion, lethargy, seizures, coma in severe hyponatremia, delayed deep tendon reflexes.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- **Restrict fluids** as ordered.\n- Monitor intake and output, daily weight, labs and neurological status; assess mental status regularly.\n- Seizure safety if sodium is very low.\n- Drugs: furosemide (Lasix) to remove water; demeclocycline makes the kidneys less sensitive to ADH.\n- Treat the underlying cause; explain procedures to the client.\n\n## Compare with DI\nSIADH: too much ADH, concentrated urine, fluid overload, low sodium. [[Diabetes Insipidus]]: too little ADH, dilute urine, dehydration, high sodium.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Hypothalamus and Pituitary Hormones]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nSevere hyponatremia is treated with hypertonic saline in hospital (my addition). Furosemide use is usually with sodium replacement.",

"id": "np4-ge-diabetes-insipidus",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Diabetes Insipidus",
"body": "## What it is\nDeficiency of ADH (vasopressin) from the posterior pituitary. The kidneys cannot concentrate urine, so large volumes of dilute urine are lost.\n\n## Causes\n- **Central:** head trauma, brain tumor, pituitary surgery or radiation, CNS infections (meningitis, encephalitis, TB), metastatic tumors.\n- **Nephrogenic:** kidneys do not respond to ADH (low potassium, high calcium, lithium, demeclocycline).\n\n## Manifestations\nExtreme thirst (2-20 L fluid a day), **dilute urine specific gravity 1.001-1.005**, polyuria and nocturia, dehydration, weight loss, tachycardia, hypotension, weakness.\n\n## Diagnosis\nHigh plasma osmolality and sodium. **Water deprivation test:** kidneys cannot concentrate urine. **Vasopressin test:** if urine concentrates after ADH, it is central; if not, it is nephrogenic.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- Replace fluids; let the client drink in response to thirst.\n- Monitor intake and output, daily weight, specific gravity, vital signs.\n- **Desmopressin (DDAVP)** intranasal (or IM) for central DI; thiazides, mild salt restriction and prostaglandin inhibitors for nephrogenic DI.\n- Teach medication use, follow-up and wearing a medical ID.\n\n## Related topics\n[[SIADH (Syndrome of Inappropriate ADH)]], [[Hypophysectomy Nursing Care]]",
