"id": "np4-ge-goiter",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Goiter",
"body": "## What it is\nEnlargement of the thyroid gland, large enough to be seen as swelling in the neck. It can occur with normal, high or low thyroid function.\n\n## How to reason about it\nCause -> finding -> action: too little iodine -> the thyroid cannot make enough hormone -> the pituitary releases more TSH -> the gland grows to try to catch up -> visible swelling, sometimes with pressure on the windpipe. Prevention is iodine in the diet. The trap: assuming every goiter means hyperthyroidism. Non-toxic goiter is euthyroid.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- **Classification:** toxic goiter goes with hyperthyroidism; non-toxic goiter goes with a euthyroid (normal function) state.\n- **Endemic (iodine-deficient) goiter:** the most common type. Also called simple or colloid goiter. Often no symptoms apart from swelling; tracheal compression if very large.\n- **Nodular goiter:** areas of overgrowth (hyperplasia) that enlarge slowly. Can cause pressure symptoms in the chest. Some are malignant or are overactive.\n- **Thyroid cancer:** risk rises with external radiation to the neck or chest in infancy or childhood.\n\n## Assessment\n- Inspect and palpate the neck gently; ask about difficulty swallowing, hoarseness, breathing trouble.\n- Look for signs of hyperthyroidism or hypothyroidism (see [[Hyperthyroidism and Graves' Disease]], [[Hypothyroidism and Myxedema]]).\n- Diet history: iodine intake; thyroid function tests and thyroid scans as ordered.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Check airway and swallowing if the gland is large: tracheal compression is the emergency risk (oxygenation first).\n2. Give iodine replacement as ordered (supplements, iodized salt, SSKI in the handout).\n3. Teach about iodized salt, especially for communities with low-iodine soil.\n4. Prepare for surgery or further tests when nodules are suspicious.\n\n## Treatment and medications (general terms)\nSupplementary iodine for iodine deficiency; hormone or antithyroid treatment depends on whether the gland is under or over-active; surgery for pressure symptoms or cancer.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nBreathing or swallowing difficulty, a hard fixed nodule, rapid growth (standard teaching).\n\n## Client teaching\nUse iodized salt; report neck swelling that grows, hoarseness, trouble breathing or swallowing; take medicines as ordered.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n\"Most common cause worldwide of goiter?\" Iodine deficiency. \"Best prevention?\" Iodized salt.\n\n## Memory aid\nGoiter = \"Gland Growing Because Iodine Is Low\".\n\n## Related topics\n[[Thyroid Blood Tests]], [[Radioactive Iodine Uptake and Thyroid Scan]], [[Hyperthyroidism Treatment and Thyroidectomy Care]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout does not give tests or surgery for goiter. Check the thyroid scan, ultrasound, biopsy and surgical options.",

"title": "Hyperthyroidism and Graves' Disease",
"body": "## What it is\nToo much thyroid hormone. The usual cause is Graves' disease, an autoimmune disorder where antibodies stimulate the thyroid (diffuse toxic goiter).\n\n## Manifestations (everything speeds up)\n- Nervousness, irritability, tremors, palpitations, tachycardia.\n- Heat intolerance, sweating, warm moist flushed skin.\n- Increased appetite with weight loss, diarrhea, amenorrhea.\n- **Exophthalmos** (bulging eyes, startled look); von Graefe's sign (lid lag on looking down); Dalrymple's sign (upper lid retraction).\n- Goiter, osteoporosis, enlarged heart muscle.\n\n## Diagnosis\nThrill and bruit over the neck; **low TSH**, **high free T4**, **high radioactive iodine uptake**.\n\n## Nursing management\n- Nutrition: high calorie, high protein; avoid highly seasoned foods and stimulants; weigh and record intake.\n- Coping: calm, unhurried approach; quiet, uncluttered environment.\n- Temperature: keep the room cool; cool baths and fluids.\n- Eye care: protective drops or ointment; discourage smoking.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Hyperthyroidism Treatment and Thyroidectomy Care]], [[Hypothyroidism and Myxedema]], [[Goiter]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThyroid storm signs (high fever, severe tachycardia, agitation) are not described in the handout; know them as an emergency.",

"title": "Hyperthyroidism Treatment and Thyroidectomy Care",
"body": "## Radioactive iodine\nDestroys overactive thyroid cells. Teach about thyroid storm; propranolol can control symptoms. Not for pregnant or breastfeeding women.\n\n## Antithyroid drugs\n- **PTU and methimazole (Tapazole)** block thyroid hormone synthesis.\n- **Agranulocytosis** is the most serious side effect: report sore throat, fever, mouth ulcers; stop the drug and have blood counts done.\n- Handout: methimazole is treatment of choice in pregnancy.\n\n## Iodine compounds (adjunct)\nPotassium iodide, Lugol's solution, SSKI reduce hormone release and gland blood supply. Give in milk or juice, through a straw to protect teeth. Beta blockers (propranolol) control sympathetic symptoms.\n\n## Subtotal thyroidectomy (about five-sixths removed)\n- Before: PTU until hyperthyroid signs resolve; propranolol for heart rate; iodine to reduce bleeding; stop aspirin and other drugs that prolong clotting for several weeks.\n- **Iodism (iodine toxicity)** needs immediate stopping: swollen mouth lining, excess saliva, runny nose (coryza), skin rash.\n- After surgery: airway first (see [[Hypoparathyroidism and Post-Thyroidectomy Tetany]] for laryngospasm and low calcium). Support the neck, check for bleeding behind the neck and hoarseness (standard teaching).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Hyperthyroidism and Graves' Disease]], [[Hyperparathyroidism]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nMost books prefer PTU in first trimester and methimazole later; the handout simply says methimazole. "id": "np4-ge-hypothyroidism-myxedema",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Hypothyroidism and Myxedema",
"body": "## What it is\nToo little thyroid hormone, commonly from autoimmune thyroiditis. **Myxedema** is the thick, mucus-like swelling of tissue seen in severe disease.\n\n## Manifestations (everything slows down)\nHair loss, brittle nails, dry thick skin, hoarse voice, slow speech, bradycardia, low temperature, cold intolerance, weight gain, constipation, fatigue, masklike face, enlarged tongue, amenorrhea, loss of libido, deafness, numbness and tingling, personality and thinking changes.\n\n## Myxedema coma\nDepression, drowsiness, lethargy, low respiratory drive (narcosis), and coma. It is an emergency: airway and breathing first.\n\n## Management\n- **Levothyroxine** (Synthroid) lifelong.\n- Monitor for angina, dysrhythmia, or MI as metabolism rises (cholesterol and coronary disease are common).\n- Thyroid hormone can raise blood glucose and cause bone loss.\n- **Sedatives, analgesics and anesthetics act longer**: use caution.\n\n## Nursing care\n- Fatigue: space activities, assist with self-care, gentle stimulation.\n- Cold intolerance: extra blankets and clothing; avoid cold and drafts.\n- Constipation: fluids, fiber, mobility; sparing use of laxatives.\n\n## Compare\n[[Hyperthyroidism and Graves' Disease]] is the opposite picture.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Goiter]]",

"id": "np4-ge-hyperparathyroidism",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Hyperparathyroidism",
"body": "## What it is\nToo much parathormone, causing bone decalcification and calcium kidney stones. Calcium is high.\n\n## Manifestations (memory aid: \"stones, bones, groans, moans\")\nRenal stones, bone and skeletal pain, pathologic fractures, shorter stature; nausea, vomiting, constipation; apathy, fatigue, muscle weakness; hypertension and dysrhythmias.\n\n## Diagnosis\nHigh calcium and parathormone, bone x-ray changes, ultrasound, MRI, thallium scan, fine-needle biopsy.\n\n## Complication\n**Hypercalcemic crisis**: neurologic, cardiovascular and renal symptoms; life-threatening.\n\n## Treatment\nAntacids, stool softeners, phosphate therapy, calcitonin, bisphosphonates, cytotoxic agents. **Parathyroidectomy** (criteria in the handout include age under 50, poor follow-up, very high calcium, high urine calcium, and 30% fall in renal function).\n\n## Nursing management\n- Fluids 2000 mL or more a day (cranberry juice listed); **avoid thiazide diuretics**.\n- Walk and keep moving; do not restrict or add calcium.\n- Before surgery: force fluids, strain urine for stones.\n- After surgery: check for renal stones, hematuria or flank pain, assist ADL, encourage weight bearing, relieve constipation, and watch for hypocalcemia ([[Hypoparathyroidism and Post-Thyroidectomy Tetany]]).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Adrenal, Thyroid and Parathyroid Hormones]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThiazides raise calcium so are avoided; the handout's surgery numbers are written unclearly (1.0 mg/dL above normal, urine calcium over 400 mg/day).",

"title": "Hypoparathyroidism and Post-Thyroidectomy Tetany",
"body": "## What it is\nToo little parathormone, usually after surgery that removes or damages parathyroid tissue (thyroidectomy, radical neck dissection). Result: **low calcium, high phosphate**.\n\n## Manifestations\nTetany, numbness, tingling and cramps, stiff hands and feet, **bronchospasm and laryngospasm**, carpopedal spasm (**positive Trousseau's sign**), **positive Chvostek's sign**, dysphagia, photophobia, dysrhythmia, seizures, anxiety and irritability.\n\n## Diagnosis\nSerum calcium very low (5-6 mg/dL or lower per handout), high phosphate, x-ray calcification.\n\n## Priority nursing actions (airway first)\n1. **Keep calcium gluconate IV at the bedside**; give for tetany.\n2. Watch for laryngeal spasm and keep a **tracheostomy set** available.\n3. Seizure precautions; low-stimulus room (no noise, drafts, bright lights).\n4. Long term: oral calcium salts, vitamin D (improves calcium absorption), parenteral parathormone (watch allergy), aluminum hydroxide after meals to bind phosphate.\n5. Diet high in calcium, low in phosphorus; avoid milk, milk products and egg yolks (high phosphorus).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Hyperthyroidism Treatment and Thyroidectomy Care]], [[Hyperparathyroidism]], [[Acute Pancreatitis]]",

"title": "Pheochromocytoma",
"body": "## What it is\nUsually benign tumor of the adrenal medulla (chromaffin cells) that releases too much epinephrine and norepinephrine. It is a cause of hypertension curable by surgery, but fatal if missed.\n\n## Manifestations\nVertigo, blurred vision, sweating, air hunger, palpitations, tachycardia, tremor, flushing, anxiety, nausea, vomiting. **Five H's:** hypertension, hyperhidrosis, hypermetabolism, hyperglycemia, headache.\n\n## Diagnosis\n- **24-hour urine VMA** (coffee, tea, bananas, chocolate, vanilla, aspirin may distort results).\n- Plasma catecholamines drawn after 30 minutes of supine rest.\n- Clonidine suppression test if unclear; CT, MRI, ultrasound.\n\n## Treatment\nAlpha blockers (phentolamine, phenoxybenzamine), nitroprusside, calcium channel blockers (nifedipine), beta blockers (propranolol). Surgery: **adrenalectomy**.\n\n## Nursing management\n- Bed rest with head of bed raised (reduces blood pressure).\n- Monitor ECG, arterial pressure, fluids, electrolytes, glucose.\n- Surgery: hydrate before, during and after; after, hypotension and hypoglycemia occur as catecholamines suddenly fall. Bilateral removal needs lifelong steroid replacement; teach never to skip doses.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Adrenal, Thyroid and Parathyroid Hormones]], [[Cushing's Syndrome]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nBeta blockers should only be started after alpha blockade (otherwise blood pressure can rise); the handout does not say this. "title": "Addison's Disease",
"body": "## What it is\nAdrenal cortex insufficiency: not enough cortisol (and aldosterone). Usually autoimmune or idiopathic atrophy. Pituitary ACTH is high.\n\n## Manifestations\nMuscle weakness, anorexia, fatigue, weight loss, **dark pigmentation**, hypotension, **low glucose, low sodium, high potassium**, leukocytosis.\n\n## Addisonian crisis\nCirculatory shock: cyanosis, pallor, anxiety, rapid weak pulse, rapid breathing, low BP. Precipitated by stress or infection.\n\n## Diagnosis\nLow early-morning cortisol, high ACTH, low glucose, low sodium, high potassium.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Crisis: **IV hydrocortisone**, then dextrose 5% in normal saline; vasopressors if hypotension continues; antibiotics if infection.\n2. Increase glucocorticoid during stress or illness.\n3. Teach: eat sodium-rich foods during GI illness or hot weather, take replacement exactly as prescribed, adjust dose in illness, avoid stress, **inform dentists and other providers**, wear medical alert bracelet.\n\n## Compare\n[[Cushing's Syndrome]] is the opposite (too much cortisol).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Adrenal, Thyroid and Parathyroid Hormones]]",

"id": "np4-ge-cushings-syndrome",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Cushing's Syndrome",
"body": "## What it is\nToo much cortisol. Most often from long-term corticosteroid drugs; less often from adrenal hyperplasia or a pituitary tumor.\n\n## Manifestations\nBuffalo hump, **central obesity with thin limbs**, moon face, thin fragile skin, bruising, slow healing, acne, weakness, hypertension, weight gain, hirsutism and virilization, breast atrophy, loss of libido.\n\n## Labs\nDexamethasone suppression test (cortisol not suppressed in true disease, my addition). Findings: high sodium, high glucose, **low potassium**, fewer eosinophils, shrunken lymphoid tissue, raised plasma and urinary cortisol.\n\n## Treatment\n- Adrenal enzyme inhibitors: metyrapone, aminoglutethimide, mitotane, ketoconazole.\n- Surgery: transsphenoidal hypophysectomy or bilateral adrenalectomy (lifelong replacement), pituitary radiation. Hydrocortisone after adrenalectomy.\n\n## Nursing management\n- Protective environment (infection risk, fragile skin); meticulous skin care.\n- Rest periods through the day.\n- Diet: high protein, calcium, vitamin D; **low carbohydrate, low sodium**.\n- Monitor glucose, BP, fluid retention.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Addison's Disease]], [[Hypophysectomy Nursing Care]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout's heading and description mix Cushing's disease (pituitary) with Cushing's syndrome (any cause); the dexamethasone test explanation is garbled.",

"id": "np4-ge-dm-diagnosis-types",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Diabetes Mellitus: Diagnosis and Types",
"body": "## What it is\nA group of metabolic diseases with high blood glucose from faulty insulin secretion, insulin action, or both.\n\n## Risk factors\nFamily history, obesity, certain ethnic groups, age over 45, hypertension, low HDL (<35) and high triglycerides (>250).\n\n## Classification\nType 1, type 2, gestational diabetes, and diabetes from other conditions.\n\n## Diagnostic criteria (any one)\n- Symptoms (polyuria, polydipsia, weight loss) plus **random glucose 200 mg/dL or more**.\n- **Fasting glucose 126 mg/dL or more** (no calories for at least 8 hours).\n- 2-hour glucose 200 mg/dL or more in an OGTT using a 75 g load.\n\n## Type 1\nImmune destruction of beta cells, so insulin is absent. Result: fasting high glucose, glucose cannot be stored after meals, **glycosuria** when glucose exceeds renal threshold (about 180-200 mg/dL), **osmotic diuresis** (loss of fluid and electrolytes), fat breakdown with **ketone bodies**, acidosis: [[Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA)]]. Needs lifelong insulin.\n\n## Type 2\nInsulin resistance plus impaired insulin secretion, often with metabolic syndrome (hypertension, high cholesterol, abdominal obesity). Enough insulin remains to block ketone formation, so [[Hyperglycemic Hyperosmolar Nonketotic Syndrome (HHNS)]] is the typical emergency.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Type 2 Diabetes Diet, Exercise and Monitoring]], [[Insulin Types: Onset, Peak and Duration]]",

"title": "Type 2 Diabetes Diet, Exercise and Monitoring",
"body": "## Nutrition\n- Weight loss of 1-2 pounds a week: subtract 500-1000 calories a day.\n- Carbohydrates higher than fat or protein; sugars allowed in moderation (they are usually high in fat and low in nutrients).\n- Dietary cholesterol under 300 mg/day.\n- More fiber may improve glucose, reduce insulin need and lower cholesterol and LDL.\n- Alcohol is absorbed first and needs no insulin; read labels of \"health food\" snacks for hidden carbohydrate and saturated fat.\n\n## Exercise\n- Lowers glucose by increasing muscle uptake and improving insulin use.\n- Same time, same amount daily; walking is safe.\n- **Eat a 15 g carbohydrate snack before moderate exercise** to avoid hypoglycemia.\n- Proper footwear, inspect feet after exercise, avoid extreme heat or cold and avoid exercise when control is poor.\n\n## Monitoring\n- **Self-monitoring of blood glucose** prevents and detects both highs and lows.\n- **HbA1c** shows average glucose over about 2-3 months.\n- **Urine ketones** signal insulin deficiency: control of type 1 is worsening and DKA risk is high.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Diabetes Mellitus: Diagnosis and Types]], [[Oral Antidiabetic Drugs]], [[Hypoglycemia in Diabetes]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout says large amounts of alcohol raise DKA risk; most textbooks stress alcohol causing hypoglycemia. "title": "Insulin Types: Onset, Peak and Duration",
"body": "## Why insulin\nIn type 1 diabetes, insulin must be given for life.\n\n## Types (handout values)\n- **Rapid-acting:** lispro (Humalog) onset 10-15 min, peak 1 h, duration 2-4 h; aspart (Novolog) 5-15 min, peak 40-50 min, 2-4 h; glulisine (Apidra) 5-15 min, peak 30-60 min, 2 h. For fast glucose lowering, mealtime highs, and nocturnal hypoglycemia prevention.\n- **Short-acting (regular):** onset 0.5-1 h, peak 2-3 h, duration 4-6 h. Give 20-30 minutes before meals. **Clear. The only insulin for IV use.**\n- **Intermediate (NPH, lente):** onset 2-4 h, peak 4-12 h, duration 16-20 h. Cloudy.\n- **Long-acting (glargine [Lantus], detemir [Levemir]):** onset about 1 h, **no peak**, 24 h. Basal dose, once daily.\n\n## Hypoglycemia timing\nHypoglycemia is most likely at each insulin's **peak**.\n\n## Mixing rule\nRegular is drawn up first when mixed with NPH (\"clear before cloudy\").\n\n## Related topics\n[[Insulin Storage, Mixing and Injection Technique]], [[Insulin Therapy Complications]], [[Hypoglycemia in Diabetes]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nHandout contains typos (\"Determir\", product names). Do not mix glargine with other insulins (my addition). Human insulin acts faster/shorter than animal insulin reasoning in handout is unusual; verify.",

"id": "np4-ge-insulin-technique-storage",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Insulin Storage, Mixing and Injection Technique",
"body": "## Storage\n- Unopened and spare vials in the refrigerator; never freeze; keep out of sunlight.\n- The vial in use may stay at room temperature to reduce injection-site irritation.\n- Always keep a spare vial.\n- Gently roll or invert cloudy insulin; do not shake. Discard if a frosted whitish coating (flocculation) appears.\n\n## Mixing and withdrawing\n- Inject air into the vial equal to the dose, then withdraw.\n- When mixing, **regular insulin is drawn first**, longer-acting insulin must be well mixed first.\n\n## Injection sites\n- Four areas: abdomen, back of upper arms, front of thighs, hips.\n- Absorption is fastest in the **abdomen**, then arm, thigh, hip.\n- Rotate within one area; keep each injection 0.5-1 inch from the last; avoid reusing a site within 2-3 weeks.\n- **Do not inject into a limb about to be exercised**; absorption speeds up and may cause hypoglycemia.\n\n## Technique\n- Let alcohol dry first (wet alcohol burns and irritates).\n- 90-degree angle for normal or overweight clients; aspiration generally not recommended in self-injection.\n- Dispose of sharps in a puncture-resistant container.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Insulin Types: Onset, Peak and Duration]], [[Insulin Therapy Complications]]",

"id": "np4-ge-insulin-complications",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Insulin Therapy Complications",
"body": "## What it is\nProblems that can follow insulin injections: allergic reactions, local fat changes, resistance, and the swings of morning glucose. Hypoglycemia is the most common complication but is covered separately.\n\n## How to reason about it\nCause -> finding -> action: repeated injections in the same spot -> local fat change and erratic absorption -> unpredictable glucose. Immune response to insulin or additives -> local then possibly generalized allergy -> stop and report. The trap: the exam asks about site rotation and expects rotation within an area, not random body-wide jumping.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- **Local allergic reaction:** redness, swelling, tenderness and induration or a 2-4 cm wheal 1-2 hours after the injection.\n- **Systemic allergic reaction:** an immediate local skin reaction that spreads into generalized urticaria (hives). Rarely it comes with generalized edema or anaphylaxis.\n- **Lipodystrophy:** a local reaction, either lipoatrophy (loss of fat, dimpling or pitting) or lipohypertrophy (fat build-up), at injection sites.\n- **Insulin resistance:** most clients have some at times; obesity is the most common cause and can be overcome by weight loss.\n- **Morning hyperglycemia:** insulin waning, the dawn phenomenon or the Somogyi effect (see [[Morning Hyperglycemia: Dawn Phenomenon, Somogyi Effect and Insulin Waning]]).\n- **Hypoglycemia:** see [[Hypoglycemia in Diabetes]].\n\n## Assessment\n- Inspect and palpate all injection sites; ask about itching, burning and hives.\n- Review glucose logs for unexplained highs or lows.\n- Check weight trend and the technique used for injection.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Systemic allergic reaction (hives, swelling, breathing trouble): airway first, emergency help, stop the insulin and report.\n2. Local reaction: report, assess, and ask the provider about a different preparation.\n3. Teach systematic **rotation of sites within one anatomic area**: each injection 0.5-1 inch from the last, not reusing a site within 2-3 weeks.\n4. Avoid lipodystrophic areas, since absorption there is erratic.\n5. Support weight loss when resistance is related to obesity.\n\n## Treatment and medications (general terms)\nSwitching insulin type, antihistamines for mild reactions, desensitization for severe allergy (standard teaching).\n\n## Complications and red flags\nAnaphylaxis, uncontrolled glucose from poor absorption, nocturnal hypoglycemia.\n\n## Client teaching\nRotate sites, let alcohol dry before injecting, never inject into a limb about to be exercised, report rash, swelling or hives right away, and keep a glucose log.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nA client has dimples in the thigh where insulin was injected: \"What is this and how to prevent?\" Lipoatrophy; rotate sites.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Insulin Storage, Mixing and Injection Technique]], [[Insulin Types: Onset, Peak and Duration]], [[Type 1 versus Type 2 Diabetes Comparison]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nTreatment of allergy (desensitization, antihistamines) is beyond the handout. Check how your course names the types of insulin reaction.",

"id": "np4-ge-morning-hyperglycemia",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Morning Hyperglycemia: Dawn Phenomenon, Somogyi Effect and Insulin Waning",
"body": "## What it is\nHigh blood glucose on waking, caused by too little insulin action overnight. The handout lists three causes: insulin waning, the dawn phenomenon and the Somogyi effect. They look identical in the morning and need opposite fixes, so the exam likes this topic.\n\n## How to reason about it\nLook at the glucose at about 3 AM, then choose the fix. High all night -> not enough insulin -> raise it. Normal then rising -> early-morning hormone surge -> shift the timing. Low at 3 AM then high -> too much insulin caused a low, and the body overcorrected -> reduce insulin or add a snack. The trap: increasing the evening insulin dose in a Somogyi client, which causes a deeper low and a bigger rebound.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- **Insulin waning:** a progressive rise in glucose from bedtime to morning. Fix: increase the evening (pre-dinner or bedtime) dose of intermediate- or long-acting insulin, or add a pre-dinner dose if none is given.\n- **Dawn phenomenon:** glucose relatively normal until about 3 AM, when it begins to rise. Fix: change the evening intermediate-acting insulin from dinnertime to bedtime.\n- **Somogyi effect:** normal or high at bedtime, a drop to hypoglycemic levels at 2-3 AM, then a rise from counter-regulatory hormones (glucagon, epinephrine, cortisol, growth hormone). Fix: **decrease** the evening dose of intermediate-acting insulin, or increase the bedtime snack.\n\n## Assessment\n- Ask about night sweats, nightmares, morning headache (possible nighttime low).\n- Check blood glucose at bedtime, 2-3 AM and on waking for several nights.\n- Review the insulin schedule and the evening snack.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Safety first: if the client has symptoms of a low at night, treat the low (see [[Hypoglycemia in Diabetes]]).\n2. Obtain the 3 AM glucose and report the pattern to the provider, who adjusts the dose.\n3. Teach not to change insulin doses without direction.\n4. Provide a bedtime snack plan as ordered.\n\n## Treatment and medications (general terms)\nAdjustment of the dose or timing of intermediate- and long-acting insulin, per the cause.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nSevere nocturnal hypoglycemia, seizures; persistent poor control and high HbA1c.\n\n## Client teaching\nKeep a glucose log with a 3 AM reading; do not skip the bedtime snack when advised; report night sweats and morning headaches.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nA glucose log showing 3 AM low and 7 AM high: \"What should be done?\" Decrease the evening insulin dose or add a bedtime snack.\n\n## Memory aid\nSomogyi = \"Slump then Surge\". Dawn = \"Daybreak Surge\" (normal until early morning).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Insulin Therapy Complications]], [[Insulin Types: Onset, Peak and Duration]], [[Hypoglycemia in Diabetes]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nWording of the dawn phenomenon fix differs among books. Follow your syllabus.",

"title": "Oral Antidiabetic Drugs",
"body": "## Drug groups\n- **Sulfonylureas:** stimulate beta cells to release insulin and may improve insulin receptor binding. Hypoglycemia risk.\n- **Biguanide (metformin):** lowers glucose made by the liver, improves tissue sensitivity to insulin, and lowers cholesterol synthesis. It is the only biguanide.\n- **Alpha-glucosidase inhibitors:** slow absorption of complex carbohydrates in the intestine.\n- **Meglitinides and phenylalanine derivatives (non-sulfonylurea secretagogues):** stimulate insulin release.\n- **Thiazolidinediones (glitazones):** make tissues more sensitive to insulin.\n- **DPP-4 inhibitors:** prolong incretin action, which raises insulin release and lowers glucagon.\n\n## Nursing points\n- Teach hypoglycemia signs for insulin-releasing drugs.\n- Give with meals as ordered; oral agents do not replace diet and exercise.\n- Metformin cautions (kidney function, contrast dye) are standard teaching I added.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Type 2 Diabetes Diet, Exercise and Monitoring]], [[Hypoglycemia in Diabetes]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nHandout spells \"dipeptide-pepidase\" incorrectly; it is dipeptidyl peptidase-4. "title": "Hypoglycemia in Diabetes",
"body": "## What it is\nLow blood glucose (handout: below 50-60 mg/dL) caused by too much insulin or oral drug, too little food, or extra exercise.\n\n## Signs by severity\n- **Mild:** sweating, tremor, tachycardia, palpitations, nervousness, hunger.\n- **Moderate:** poor concentration, headache, confusion, memory lapses, numb lips and tongue, slurred speech, poor coordination, mood change or combative behavior, double vision, drowsiness.\n- **Severe:** needs help from another person; disorientation, seizures, hard to wake, unconsciousness.\n\n## Priority actions\n1. If awake and able to swallow: give fast-acting sugar (about 15 g), then a snack.\n2. If unconscious or cannot swallow: **glucagon 1 mg SC or IM**; in hospital 25-50 mL of **50% dextrose IV** (check the IV line is patent, it irritates veins).\n3. Recheck blood glucose; follow with food when awake.\n\n## Teaching\nEat extra when active, test glucose routinely, wear diabetic ID, always carry a simple sugar, avoid high-calorie, high-fat desserts to treat lows (they absorb slowly).\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nSweating and shakiness on insulin: treat first, then investigate. Do not wait for a lab value when the client is symptomatic.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Insulin Types: Onset, Peak and Duration]], [[Morning Hyperglycemia: Dawn Phenomenon, Somogyi Effect and Insulin Waning]], [[Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA)]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nMost books define hypoglycemia as below 70 mg/dL. "id": "np4-ge-diabetic-ketoacidosis",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA)",
"body": "## What it is\nCaused by absent or very low insulin. Three features: **hyperglycemia, dehydration with electrolyte loss, and acidosis** from ketones.\n\n## Manifestations\nPolyuria, polydipsia, blurred vision, weakness, headache, orthostatic hypotension, weak rapid pulse, anorexia, nausea, vomiting, abdominal pain, **acetone (fruity) breath**, **Kussmaul respirations** (deep, rapid).\n\n## Findings\nGlucose 300-800 mg/dL, bicarbonate 0-15 mEq/L, pH 6.8-7.3, low PCO2 (10-30 mm Hg), raised creatinine, BUN and hematocrit.\n\n## Management (priority order)\n1. **Rehydrate:** 0.9% saline (handout says 0.65 to 1 per hour for 2-3 hours); 0.45% saline if sodium is high; plasma expanders in severe hypotension. Watch for fluid overload: vital signs, lung sounds, intake and output.\n2. **Potassium:** monitor often; serum K can fall fast as fluids and insulin work; start replacement once levels drop to normal (up to 40 mEq/h may be needed); ECG every 2-4 hours at first.\n3. **Insulin:** slow continuous IV infusion. Flush the tubing and discard the first 50 mL because insulin sticks to the tubing.\n4. **Avoid bicarbonate** in DKA: it causes further sudden drops in potassium.\n\n## Related topics\n[[DKA versus HHNS Comparison]], [[Diabetes Mellitus: Diagnosis and Types]], [[Hypoglycemia in Diabetes]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n\"0.65 to 1 Uh\" in the handout looks like a typo (likely L/hour); verify. The sequence (fluids before insulin) is standard teaching.",

"id": "np4-ge-hhns",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Hyperglycemic Hyperosmolar Nonketotic Syndrome (HHNS)",
"body": "## What it is\nA serious condition in which very high blood glucose and very high osmolality (concentrated blood) dominate, with altered consciousness. Ketosis is minimal or absent. It is also called hyperosmolar hyperglycemic state. It is more common in older clients with type 2 diabetes.\n\n## How to reason about it\nCause -> finding -> action: infection, surgery, stroke or MI (physiologic stress) -> glucose climbs for days -> glucose spills into urine -> osmotic diuresis wastes water and electrolytes -> severe dehydration, high osmolality -> brain cells shrink, giving confusion, seizures or weakness on one side. Since some insulin is present, ketones and acidosis do not form. First action: replace fluid. The trap: choosing insulin first. In HHNS insulin is less important because there is no acidosis to reverse.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Glucose usually above 600 mg/dL (handout range 600-1200); osmolality above 350 mOsm/kg.\n- Onset is slow, over several days; mortality is higher (10-40%) than DKA (under 5%).\n- Ketones absent; arterial pH and bicarbonate normal; BUN and creatinine elevated.\n- Compare directly with [[DKA versus HHNS Comparison]].\n\n## Assessment\n- Hypotension, tachycardia, profound dehydration: dry mucous membranes, poor skin turgor.\n- Altered sensorium (confusion, lethargy), seizures, hemiparesis.\n- Check blood glucose, serum osmolality, electrolytes, and urine output.\n\n## Priority nursing actions (in order, and why)\n1. Airway, breathing and circulation, with seizure precautions and neurologic checks (altered consciousness is a safety risk).\n2. Start IV fluids as ordered: 0.9% or 0.45% saline. Rehydration dilutes the glucose and restores circulation first.\n3. Monitor fluid volume with a central venous or hemodynamic pressure if ordered (handout). Watch for fluid overload in older clients, especially with heart or kidney disease.\n4. Correct electrolytes; potassium is added to IV fluids (handout). Watch ECG and potassium.\n5. Give insulin as ordered (a less central role than in DKA).\n6. Treat the cause (infection) and monitor glucose often.\n\n## Treatment and medications (general terms)\nIV fluid replacement, electrolyte correction, insulin, treatment of the precipitating illness.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nShock, seizures, stroke-like signs, kidney injury, death.\n\n## Client teaching\nDrink fluids when ill, check glucose on sick days, continue diabetes medicines unless told otherwise, and seek care early for infection or confusion. Older adults may not feel thirst, so family should watch intake (standard teaching).\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nAn older type 2 client, dehydrated and confused, glucose far above 600 and no ketones: \"First action?\" Fluids. Distinguish from DKA by absent ketones and normal pH.\n\n## Memory aid (made up)\nHHNS = \"Hot, Hyperosmolar, Needs Saline\".\n\n## Related topics\n[[Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA)]], [[Type 1 versus Type 2 Diabetes Comparison]], [[Endocrine Emergencies: Which Crisis Is Which]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout's DKA and HHNS glucose cut-offs differ slightly in different tables (DKA above 250 vs 300-800). Fluid rates and the timing of insulin and potassium are not given for HHNS. Verify against your textbook.",

"area": "NP4",
"title": "DKA versus HHNS Comparison",
"body": "## What it is\nA side-by-side of the two hyperglycemic emergencies of diabetes. The exam gives a few clues and asks you to decide which one it is and what to do first.\n\n## How to reason about it\nAsk \"is there acid?\" DKA has ketones and low pH, so there is acidosis, Kussmaul breathing and fruity breath. HHNS has no ketones and a normal pH, but extreme glucose and dehydration with altered consciousness. Treatment starts with fluids in both, but only DKA needs insulin to turn off ketone production. The trap: assuming DKA is only type 1 and HHNS only type 2; the handout says DKA occurs in either type.\n\n## Must-know facts (handout values)\n- **Patients most commonly affected:** DKA: more common in type 1 but can occur in either type. HHNS: more common in type 2, especially older clients.\n- **Precipitating event:** DKA: omitted insulin, physiologic stress (infection, surgery, stroke, MI). HHNS: physiologic stress (infection, surgery, stroke, MI).\n- **Onset:** DKA rapid (under 24 hours). HHNS slower, over several days.\n- **Blood glucose:** DKA usually above 250 mg/dL. HHNS usually above 600 mg/dL.\n- **Arterial pH:** DKA low (about 7.3 or less). HHNS normal.\n- **Serum and urine ketones:** DKA present. HHNS absent.\n- **Serum osmolality:** DKA 300-350 mOsm/L. HHNS above 350.\n- **Plasma bicarbonate:** DKA below 15 mEq/L. HHNS normal.\n- **BUN and creatinine:** elevated in both.\n- **Mortality:** DKA under 5%. HHNS 10-40%.\n\n## Assessment\n- DKA: polyuria, polydipsia, nausea, vomiting, abdominal pain, acetone breath, Kussmaul respirations, weak rapid pulse.\n- HHNS: severe dehydration, hypotension, tachycardia, confusion, seizures, hemiparesis.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Airway, breathing, circulation; check glucose and neurologic status.\n2. IV fluids first in both (normal saline, then 0.45% saline if sodium is high).\n3. Monitor potassium closely; start potassium when it falls to normal (DKA); avoid bicarbonate in DKA.\n4. DKA: continuous IV insulin (flush the tubing and discard the first 50 mL). HHNS: insulin has a lesser role.\n5. Watch for fluid overload and treat the trigger (infection).\n\n## Treatment and medications (general terms)\nIV fluids, insulin, potassium, treatment of precipitating illness. See [[Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA)]] and [[Hyperglycemic Hyperosmolar Nonketotic Syndrome (HHNS)]].\n\n## Complications and red flags\nShock, cerebral swelling, hypokalemia, dysrhythmia, fluid overload, death.\n\n## Client teaching\nNever stop insulin when ill; check glucose and ketones on sick days; drink fluids; call early for vomiting, confusion or glucose persistently high.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nA table of findings: ketones present with low pH means DKA; no ketones with very high osmolality means HHNS.\n\n## Memory aid (made up)\nDKA = \"Keto Acid\" (ketones, acid, Kussmaul). HHNS = \"Hyper-Hydration-Needed Severely\".\n\n## Related topics\n[[Type 1 versus Type 2 Diabetes Comparison]], [[Hypoglycemia in Diabetes]], [[Endocrine Emergencies: Which Crisis Is Which]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout lists DKA glucose both as above 250 and as 300-800 elsewhere; the handout's pH 7.3 is the upper edge of a range (6.8-7.3). Confirm values with your course tables.",

"id": "np4-ge-x-thyroid-storm",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Thyroid Storm (Thyrotoxic Crisis)",
"body": "## What it is\nA sudden, life-threatening surge of thyroid hormone effects on top of uncontrolled hyperthyroidism. The handout only says to \"observe for thyroid storm\" after radioactive iodine and lists it as a risk of untreated Graves' disease, so most of the detail below is standard teaching you must confirm.\n\n## How to reason about it\nCause -> finding -> priority action: an overactive thyroid plus a stressor (infection, surgery, stopping antithyroid drugs, radioactive iodine treatment) -> everything speeds up too far (heart, temperature, brain) -> protect airway, breathing and circulation, cool the client, and give the ordered drugs. The trap: the exam shows a hyperthyroid client after thyroid surgery or iodine therapy with a high fever and racing heart, and offers a \"reassure and recheck in an hour\" option. This is an emergency, so act now.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Triggers named or implied in the handout: radioactive iodine therapy, thyroid surgery done before the client is controlled (this is why PTU, propranolol and iodine are given before subtotal thyroidectomy), and omitted antithyroid medicine.\n- It is the extreme end of the same picture as [[Hyperthyroidism and Graves' Disease]]: tachycardia, tremor, heat intolerance, sweating, agitation.\n\n## Assessment\n- Very fast heart rate with palpitations or dysrhythmia, high temperature, flushed sweating skin.\n- Restlessness, agitation, confusion, then possible delirium or coma.\n- Vomiting and diarrhea, leading to dehydration.\n\n## Priority nursing actions (in order, and why)\n1. Airway, breathing, circulation: heart failure and dysrhythmia are the killers. Monitor ECG, blood pressure and oxygen saturation.\n2. Call the provider and prepare for the ordered treatment (antithyroid drug, iodine solution given after the antithyroid drug, beta blocker such as propranolol, corticosteroid, IV fluids). The handout's reasons: antithyroid drugs block hormone making, iodine reduces hormone release, and propranolol controls sympathetic effects.\n3. Cool the client: cool room, cool baths, cool fluids (the handout lists these for hyperthyroid care). Standard teaching: use a cooling blanket; avoid aspirin because it can raise free hormone levels (my addition, check).\n4. Quiet, calm, low-stimulation room; stay with the client.\n5. Replace fluids and watch intake and output.\n\n## Treatment and medications (general terms)\nAntithyroid drugs (PTU, methimazole), iodine compounds, beta blockers, corticosteroids, supportive care. See [[Hyperthyroidism Treatment and Thyroidectomy Care]].\n\n## Complications and red flags\nHeart failure, dysrhythmia, hyperthermia, shock, coma.\n\n## Client teaching\nTake antithyroid medicine exactly as prescribed and never stop it suddenly; report fever, sore throat or mouth ulcers (agranulocytosis); report fast heartbeat, high fever or confusion at once; tell any surgeon or dentist about the thyroid condition.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nPost-thyroidectomy or post-iodine client with high temperature, tachycardia and agitation: \"What is the priority?\" Think thyroid storm, call and treat, cool the client.\n\n## Memory aid\nStorm = \"everything too high\": heat, heart rate, hyperactivity.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Hyperthyroidism and Graves' Disease]], [[Hyperthyroidism Treatment and Thyroidectomy Care]], [[Endocrine Emergencies: Which Crisis Is Which]], [[Antithyroid Drugs]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe signs, triggers beyond the handout, drug order (antithyroid before iodine), the aspirin warning and cooling method are standard teaching not given in the handout. Confirm the exact drug sequence with your textbook.",

"id": "np4-ge-x-myxedema-coma",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Myxedema Coma",
"body": "## What it is\nThe most severe form of hypothyroidism: the body runs on almost no thyroid hormone. The handout describes it as depression, diminished thinking, lethargy and somnolence, depressed breathing (narcosis) and coma. Myxedema itself means the thick, mucus-like swelling of skin and tissues caused by build-up of mucopolysaccharides.\n\n## How to reason about it\nCause -> finding -> action: untreated or undertreated hypothyroidism (often with a stressor such as infection, cold or sedating drugs) -> metabolism slows to a crawl -> slow breathing, low temperature, slow heart rate, confusion -> airway and breathing come first, then warming and thyroid hormone. The trap: giving a sedative or opioid. The handout says analgesics, sedatives and anesthetics act longer in hypothyroidism, so they can tip a drowsy client into respiratory failure.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Opposite emergency to [[Thyroid Storm (Thyrotoxic Crisis)]].\n- Underlying chronic picture: cold intolerance, dry thick skin, hoarseness, bradycardia, weight gain, constipation, masklike face, enlarged tongue (see [[Hypothyroidism and Myxedema]]).\n- The handout calls for arterial blood gases as supportive therapy because breathing is depressed.\n\n## Assessment\n- Level of consciousness falling: lethargy -> stupor -> coma.\n- Slow, shallow breathing; watch oxygen saturation and ABGs.\n- Low body temperature, slow pulse, low blood pressure (standard teaching).\n- Puffy face and hands, dry cool skin.\n\n## Priority nursing actions (in order, and why)\n1. Airway and breathing (oxygenation is the first Maslow need): be ready for ventilatory support; monitor ABGs.\n2. Give ordered IV thyroid hormone (levothyroxine) and treat any triggering infection.\n3. Warm the client gently with blankets, not hot packs or heating pads (standard teaching: fast rewarming can drop blood pressure; check).\n4. Monitor heart: angina, dysrhythmia or MI can appear as metabolism rises (handout).\n5. Avoid or greatly reduce sedatives, opioids and anesthetics; use cautious doses only if ordered.\n6. Monitor fluids, glucose and sodium (standard teaching).\n\n## Treatment and medications (general terms)\nSynthetic thyroid hormone (levothyroxine, e.g. Synthroid), given IV in the crisis; supportive care; steroids and antibiotics as ordered (standard).\n\n## Complications and red flags\nRespiratory failure, cardiac complications, coma.\n\n## Client teaching\nLifelong levothyroxine; do not stop it; report cold intolerance, increasing sleepiness, slow heart rate, or swelling early; tell every provider about the thyroid condition before any sedative or surgery.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nHypothyroid client becomes very drowsy and breathes slowly: \"First action?\" Support airway/breathing and call for help, not \"let the client sleep\".\n\n## Memory aid\nMyxedema coma = \"slow everything\": breathing, heart, thinking, temperature.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Hypothyroidism and Myxedema]], [[Thyroid Blood Tests]], [[Endocrine Emergencies: Which Crisis Is Which]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout gives only the definition and ABG support. IV route, rewarming method, steroid use and the specific vital sign changes are standard teaching; confirm in your textbook.",

"id": "np4-ge-x-addisonian-crisis",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Addisonian (Adrenal) Crisis",
"body": "## What it is\nA life-threatening shortage of cortisol (and aldosterone) that appears as circulatory shock in a client with Addison's disease. The handout describes cyanosis plus pallor, apprehension, rapid weak pulse, rapid breathing and low blood pressure. It follows stress or infection when the usual replacement dose is not enough, or when steroids are missed.\n\n## How to reason about it\nCause -> finding -> action: no cortisol -> blood vessels cannot hold pressure and glucose drops; no aldosterone -> sodium and water are lost, potassium is kept -> shock, low sodium, high potassium, low glucose -> give IV hydrocortisone and fluids fast. The trap: waiting for lab results, or giving potassium. This client already has high potassium.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Background disease: [[Addison's Disease]] (autoimmune or idiopathic atrophy of the adrenal cortex).\n- Lab pattern (handout): low sodium, high potassium, low glucose, high white cell count; early morning cortisol low and ACTH high.\n- Extra steroid is needed during stressful procedures and significant illness to prevent crisis.\n\n## Assessment\n- Hypotension, weak rapid pulse, rapid breathing, pallor, cyanosis, anxiety or apprehension.\n- Weakness, nausea, vomiting, abdominal pain (standard teaching).\n- Skin darkening (chronic feature), low glucose signs: sweating, shakiness, confusion.\n\n## Priority nursing actions (in order, and why)\n1. Circulation is failing, so treat shock: lay the client flat (standard), oxygen as ordered, IV access, continuous monitoring of blood pressure and ECG.\n2. Give the ordered **IV hydrocortisone** (Solu-Cortef) right away, then IV 5% dextrose in normal saline (handout). Steroid replaces the missing hormone; dextrose treats low glucose; saline refills the vessels and replaces sodium.\n3. Vasopressor amines if hypotension continues; antibiotics if infection triggered the crisis (handout).\n4. Monitor potassium and ECG for hyperkalemia changes (standard); avoid potassium-containing fluids unless ordered.\n5. Keep the client calm and at rest; avoid unnecessary activity or stress that could cause another hypotensive episode (handout).\n\n## Treatment and medications (general terms)\nIV glucocorticoid, IV fluids with dextrose, vasopressors if needed, antibiotics for infection, then lifelong oral replacement.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nShock, severe hypoglycemia, dysrhythmia from hyperkalemia, death if untreated.\n\n## Client teaching\nTake steroid replacement as prescribed and never skip it; raise the dose during illness or stress as the provider directs; eat sodium-rich foods during GI illness and hot weather; tell dentists and all providers about steroid use; wear a medical alert bracelet and carry information about the need for corticosteroid.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nAddison's client with infection, low BP and fast weak pulse: \"Priority?\" IV hydrocortisone and fluids, not an oral medicine and not waiting.\n\n## Memory aid\nAddison's = \"Add steroid, Add salt, Add sugar\" (made up).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Addison's Disease]], [[Cushing's Syndrome]], [[Endocrine Emergencies: Which Crisis Is Which]], [[Adrenal, Thyroid and Parathyroid Hormones]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nPosition, oxygen, potassium caution and symptom list beyond the handout are standard teaching. Confirm your course's preferred wording for \"adrenal crisis\".",

"id": "np4-ge-x-type1-vs-type2",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Type 1 versus Type 2 Diabetes Comparison",
"body": "## What it is\nA side-by-side of the two main kinds of diabetes mellitus using the handout's description of each. Both raise blood glucose, but the reason, the emergency each one tends to cause, and the treatment differ.\n\n## How to reason about it\nAsk \"is there any insulin?\" Type 1: almost none -> fat burns -> ketones -> acidosis -> [[Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA)]]. Type 2: some insulin, resisted -> enough to block ketones -> glucose keeps rising and water is lost -> [[Hyperglycemic Hyperosmolar Nonketotic Syndrome (HHNS)]]. The trap is to assume a type 2 client cannot get DKA or a type 1 client cannot get HHNS. The handout says DKA can occur in either type.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- **Cause:** type 1 is destruction of pancreatic beta cells; antibodies attack the body's own tissue as if foreign (autoimmune). Type 2 has increased insulin resistance, decreased insulin sensitivity and impaired insulin secretion, and is called idiopathic in the handout.\n- **Insulin level:** type 1 low or absent. Type 2 present but not effective.\n- **Typical associations:** type 2 clusters with metabolic syndrome (hypertension, high cholesterol, abdominal obesity).\n- **Risk factors for diabetes in general (handout):** family history, obesity, certain ethnic groups, age over 45, hypertension, HDL under 35 mg/dL, triglycerides over 250 mg/dL.\n- **Ketones:** type 1 prone to ketosis; type 2 usually has enough insulin to prevent fat breakdown and ketone production.\n\n## Assessment\n- Classic symptoms for both: polyuria, polydipsia, unexplained weight loss.\n- Glycosuria appears when glucose exceeds the renal threshold of about 180-200 mg/dL (handout), and osmotic diuresis follows.\n- Diagnosis (either type, handout): symptoms plus casual glucose 200 mg/dL or more; or fasting glucose 126 mg/dL or more (no calories for at least 8 hours); or 2-hour glucose 200 mg/dL or more on a 75 g oral glucose tolerance test.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- Type 1: insulin for life, ketone testing when ill or glucose is high, teach the signs of DKA.\n- Type 2: diet and weight loss (a loss of 1-2 pounds per week), regular exercise, oral agents, insulin if needed; teach hypoglycemia signs for drugs that raise insulin.\n- Both: blood glucose self-monitoring, HbA1c reflecting about 2-3 months, foot and skin care (standard teaching).\n\n## Treatment and medications (general terms)\nType 1: insulin. Type 2: diet, exercise, [[Oral Antidiabetic Drugs]], insulin when needed. Details: [[Insulin Types: Onset, Peak and Duration]] and [[Type 2 Diabetes Diet, Exercise and Monitoring]].\n\n## Complications and red flags\nAcute: hypoglycemia, DKA, HHNS. Chronic complications are not in this handout.\n\n## Client teaching\nTake treatment every day, eat on schedule, carry fast sugar, wear identification, never stop insulin when ill without calling the provider (standard).\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n\"A thin young client with weight loss, thirst and fruity breath\" suggests type 1/DKA. \"An older overweight client, dehydrated, confused, glucose very high, no ketones\" suggests type 2/HHNS.\n\n## Memory aid\nType 1 = \"One: none\" (no insulin). Type 2 = \"Two: too little effect\".\n\n## Related topics\n[[Diabetes Mellitus: Diagnosis and Types]], [[DKA versus HHNS Comparison]], [[Hypoglycemia in Diabetes]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nAge of onset, body weight patterns and the C-peptide idea are standard but not in the handout. Gestational diabetes is only named in the handout; read your OB notes.",

"title": "Endocrine Emergencies: Which Crisis Is Which",
"body": "## What it is\nA one-page sorter for the endocrine emergencies in the handout, so you can tell them apart when a question gives only a few clues.\n\n## How to reason about it\nFind the hormone that is too high or too low, picture what the body does, and the first action follows. Always check airway, breathing and circulation before anything endocrine-specific (Maslow: physiological needs first).\n\n## Must-know facts\n- **Thyroid storm** (too much thyroid hormone): racing heart, high temperature, agitation. First: ABC, ordered drugs, cool the client. See [[Thyroid Storm (Thyrotoxic Crisis)]].\n- **Myxedema coma** (too little thyroid hormone): slow breathing, low temperature, drowsy to coma. First: airway and breathing, thyroid hormone, avoid sedatives. See [[Myxedema Coma]].\n- **Addisonian crisis** (too little cortisol): shock, low sodium, high potassium, low glucose. First: IV hydrocortisone and fluids. See [[Addisonian (Adrenal) Crisis]].\n- **Hypocalcemic tetany** after neck surgery (low parathormone): tingling, carpopedal spasm, laryngospasm. First: airway, IV calcium gluconate (kept at the bedside), tracheostomy set available. See [[Hypoparathyroidism and Post-Thyroidectomy Tetany]].\n- **Hypercalcemic crisis** (too much parathormone): neurologic, cardiovascular and renal symptoms, life-threatening. See [[Hyperparathyroidism]].\n- **Hypoglycemia** (too much insulin or too little food): sweating, shakiness, confusion. First: quick sugar if awake, glucagon or IV dextrose if not. See [[Hypoglycemia in Diabetes]].\n- **DKA** (no insulin, ketones): Kussmaul breathing, fruity breath, glucose high. First: fluids, then potassium watch, then IV insulin. See [[Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA)]].\n- **HHNS** (very high glucose, no ketones, osmolality above 350): severe dehydration, altered consciousness. First: fluids. See [[Hyperglycemic Hyperosmolar Nonketotic Syndrome (HHNS)]].\n- **Pheochromocytoma crisis** (surge of catecholamines): severe hypertension, headache, sweating, palpitations. See [[Pheochromocytoma]].\n- **SIADH with severe hyponatremia** (water retained): confusion, seizures, coma. First: seizure safety and fluid restriction. See [[SIADH (Syndrome of Inappropriate ADH)]].\n\n## Assessment\nCompare the vital signs: fast and hot (storm), slow and cold (myxedema), low pressure with high potassium (Addisonian), spasms (low calcium), sweaty and shaky (low glucose), deep rapid breathing (DKA).\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Airway, breathing, circulation.\n2. Treat the life-threatening abnormality (glucose, calcium, volume).\n3. Give the hormone or drug that replaces or blocks what is wrong.\n4. Reassess and prevent the next episode through teaching.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nTwo or three signs plus a recent event: neck surgery (tetany), missed steroid (Addisonian), stress with a known diabetic (DKA or HHNS), thyroid client with fever (storm).\n\n## Memory aid\nHormone high = body races. Hormone low = body crawls (for thyroid). Low cortisol = low pressure, low sugar, low salt, high potassium.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Adrenal, Thyroid and Parathyroid Hormones]], [[DKA versus HHNS Comparison]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nOnly some crises appear in your handout by name; thyroid storm, myxedema coma, Addisonian crisis and pheochromocytoma crisis details are partly standard teaching. Check each against your textbook.",

"body": "## What it is\nOne list of the named signs and classic findings from the GI, liver and pancreas section, so you can match a clue in a question to the disease.\n\n## How to reason about it\nThe exam gives one unusual finding and asks for the condition or the priority. Learn each sign with the organ, then think about why the body produces it.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- **Pyrosis** (burning behind the breastbone): GERD, from a weak lower esophageal sphincter. [[Gastroesophageal Reflux Disease (GERD)]]\n- **Rebound tenderness:** pain when pressure is released; inflammation of the peritoneum, as in appendicitis or peritonitis. [[Appendicitis]], [[Peritonitis]]\n- **Rovsing's sign:** pressing the left lower quadrant causes pain in the right lower quadrant; appendicitis.\n- **Murphy's sign:** pain and a held breath when the examiner's fingers press over the gallbladder during a deep breath; cholecystitis. [[Acute Pancreatitis]]\n- **Caput medusae:** dilated veins radiating from the umbilicus; portal hypertension. [[Portal Hypertension and Ascites]]\n- **Fluid wave and shifting dullness:** ascites.\n- **Asterixis (liver flap):** hands drop and snap back when held out with wrists bent upward; hepatic encephalopathy. [[Hepatic Encephalopathy]]\n- **Fetor hepaticus:** sweet, slightly fecal breath; liver failure.\n- **Constructional apraxia:** cannot draw a simple star; advanced hepatic encephalopathy.\n- **Hematemesis versus melena:** vomiting blood versus black tarry stool. [[Peptic Ulcer Disease: Gastric versus Duodenal Ulcer]]\n- **Steatorrhea:** frothy, foul, fatty stool; pancreatic insufficiency or fat malabsorption. [[Chronic Pancreatitis]]\n- **Cobblestone appearance:** Crohn's disease. [[Crohn's Disease]]\n- **Borborygmi, sweating, palpitations after meals:** dumping syndrome. [[Dumping Syndrome]]\n- **Light stool and dark urine with jaundice:** icteric stage of hepatitis. [[Viral Hepatitis A to E]]\n- **Bright red bleeding with itching:** hemorrhoids. [[Hemorrhoids]]\n\n## Assessment order\nInspect, auscultate, percuss, then palpate (the handout order), because touching can change bowel sounds.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\nAny sign that suggests perforation, hemorrhage or encephalopathy comes before routine care: airway, circulation and neurologic safety first.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nSingle-sign matching (\"Pain in the right lower quadrant when the left is pressed\") or naming the priority once the sign identifies the disease.\n\n## Memory aid\nRovsing pushes on the wrong side; Murphy stops the breath; Cullen is the Cord (umbilicus); Turner is the Torso side.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Digestive Tract Parts and Abdominal Regions]], [[Endocrine Named Signs and Tests Cheat Sheet]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nShifting dullness, melena-versus-hematemesis comparison across ulcers, and the memory aid are my additions; check McBurney's point as well.",

"body": "## What it is\nAll the named signs, special tests and lab clues from the endocrine handout in one place, with the disease each points to.\n\n## How to reason about it\nName -> organ -> hormone high or low. Then decide the nursing priority (airway for laryngospasm, fluids for dehydration, safety for confusion).\n\n## Must-know facts\n- **Chvostek's sign** and **Trousseau's sign (carpopedal spasm)**: low calcium after parathyroid damage. [[Hypoparathyroidism and Post-Thyroidectomy Tetany]]\n- **Exophthalmos, von Graefe's sign (lid lag looking down), Dalrymple's sign (upper lid retraction):** Graves' disease. [[Hyperthyroidism and Graves' Disease]]\n- **Thrill and bruit over the neck; low TSH, high free T4, high radioactive iodine uptake:** hyperthyroidism.\n- **Myxedema:** mucus-like tissue swelling in severe hypothyroidism. [[Hypothyroidism and Myxedema]]\n- **Buffalo hump, moon face, central obesity with thin limbs:** Cushing's. [[Cushing's Syndrome]]\n- **Dexamethasone suppression test:** used in Cushing's work-up.\n- **Dark pigmentation, low sodium, high potassium, low glucose:** Addison's. [[Addison's Disease]]\n- **Five H's:** hypertension, hyperhidrosis, hypermetabolism, hyperglycemia, headache in pheochromocytoma; **24-hour urine VMA** (coffee, tea, bananas, chocolate, vanilla and aspirin can alter it), plasma catecholamines after 30 minutes of supine rest, clonidine suppression test if unclear. [[Pheochromocytoma]]\n- **Water deprivation test and vasopressin test:** diabetes insipidus; the vasopressin test separates central from nephrogenic. [[Diabetes Insipidus]]\n- **Urine specific gravity 1.001-1.005:** diabetes insipidus. **High urine sodium and osmolality with low serum osmolality:** SIADH. [[SIADH (Syndrome of Inappropriate ADH)]]\n- **Acromegaly features:** protruding jaw, thick heel pads, enlarged tongue. [[Acromegaly]]\n- **Galactorrhea and amenorrhea:** hyperprolactinemia in women. [[Pituitary Dwarfism and Hyperprolactinemia]]\n- **Fasting glucose, random glucose, OGTT; HbA1c (2-3 months); urine ketones:** diabetes monitoring. [[Diabetes Mellitus: Diagnosis and Types]]\n- **Kussmaul respirations and acetone breath:** DKA. [[Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA)]]\n- **Stones, bones, groans, moans (made up):** hyperparathyroidism. [[Hyperparathyroidism]]\n\n## Assessment\nCheck the airway when the sign involves spasm or swelling; check glucose when the client is confused.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\nMatching the sign to the correct disease gives the priority: laryngospasm (airway, calcium gluconate), vision or eye exposure (eye protection), hypertensive surge (bed rest, head raised), dehydration (fluids).\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nA named sign plus a surgery history, or a lab clue. Check whether the hormone is high or low before choosing a response.\n\n## Related topics\n[[GI and Liver Named Signs Cheat Sheet]], [[Endocrine Emergencies: Which Crisis Is Which]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nNormal ranges for the tests are not given here; check your lab table.",

"id": "np4-ns-neurons-transmitters",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Neurons and Neurotransmitters",
"body": "## What it is\nThe nervous system controls movement, sensation, autonomic function, thinking and behavior. The central nervous system (CNS) is the brain and spinal cord. The peripheral nervous system (PNS) is the cranial nerves, spinal nerves and the autonomic nervous system.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- The neuron is the basic working unit. Dendrites carry impulses toward the cell body; the axon carries them away.\n- Sensory (afferent) neurons carry impulses from receptors to the CNS. Motor (efferent) neurons carry impulses from the CNS to effectors such as muscles and glands. Interneurons stay inside the CNS and link sensory and motor pathways.\n- Neurotransmitters pass messages from one neuron to another or to a target tissue. They can excite or inhibit.\n\n## Neurotransmitters at a glance\n- Dopamine: excitatory in the handout; complex movement, motivation, cognition, emotional response. Low in Parkinson's disease.\n- Norepinephrine: excitatory; attention, learning, memory, sleep-wake, mood.\n- Epinephrine: excitatory; fight-or-flight.\n- Serotonin: inhibitory; fluid intake, sleep, temperature, pain control, sexual behavior, emotion.\n- Acetylcholine: excitatory or inhibitory; sleep-wake cycle, makes muscles alert. Low in myasthenia gravis and Alzheimer's disease.\n- GABA: inhibitory; calms and modulates other transmitters.\n- Glutamate: excitatory; too much is toxic to neurons (linked to ALS).\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nQuestions usually link a transmitter to a disease: dopamine to Parkinson's, acetylcholine to myasthenia gravis and Alzheimer's, glutamate to ALS. Learn the pairing, then the drug that corrects it.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Parkinson's Disease: Signs and Pathophysiology]], [[Myasthenia Gravis]], [[Amyotrophic Lateral Sclerosis]], [[Brain Regions and Their Functions]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- The handout writes \"glutamine\"; the excitatory transmitter meant here is glutamate.\n- Labeling dopamine as purely excitatory is a simplification; its effect depends on the receptor.",

"id": "np4-ns-brain-regions",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Brain Regions and Their Functions",
"body": "## What it is\nKnowing which region does what lets you predict the deficit from the site of injury, a common exam style.\n\n## Cerebrum (four lobes)\n- Frontal: the largest lobe. Concentration, abstract thought, memory storage, and generation of voluntary movement. Holds Broca's area (motor control of speech).\n- Parietal: sensory function: touch, taste, temperature. This is where sensations are felt.\n- Temporal: hearing and smell; memory of sound, understanding of language and music. Holds Wernicke's area (language comprehension).\n- Occipital: visual interpretation and visual memory.\n\n## Brain stem and others\n- Cerebellum: fine movement, balance, position sense (proprioception).\n- Medulla: cardiac, respiratory and vasomotor centers, plus reflex centers for coughing, sneezing, swallowing and vomiting. Damage here is life threatening.\n- Pons: sits in front of the cerebellum and above the medulla; contains the apneustic and pneumotaxic respiratory centers that set a normal breathing rhythm.\n- Midbrain: visual, auditory and righting reflexes.\n- Hypothalamus: hormone production, body temperature, food and fluid intake, and coordination of the autonomic nervous system.\n- Thalamus: relays sensation and can suppress minor sensations.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Expressive aphasia points to Broca's area (frontal); receptive aphasia points to Wernicke's area (temporal).\n- Visual field loss after a stroke points to the occipital pathway.\n- Priority thinking: medulla and pons control breathing and heart rate, so injury there threatens airway and circulation first.\n\n## Memory aid\nBroca = \"broken speech\" (can't produce words); Wernicke = \"can't understand\". Made-up helper.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Stroke: Deficits and Left versus Right Hemisphere]], [[Increased Intracranial Pressure]], [[Neurons and Neurotransmitters]]",

"title": "Cranial Nerves: Names, Types and Functions",
"body": "## What it is\nTwelve pairs of nerves from the brain. Each is sensory (S), motor (M) or mixed (B for both).\n\n## The twelve\n- I Olfactory (S): smell.\n- II Optic (S): vision and visual acuity.\n- III Oculomotor (M): most eye movement, lifting the eyelid, pupil constriction, lens accommodation.\n- IV Trochlear (M): eye movement (downward and inward gaze; the handout tests upward movement).\n- V Trigeminal (B): facial sensation, corneal reflex, chewing.\n- VI Abducens (M): eye movement (outward).\n- VII Facial (B): facial expression, tearing and salivation, taste on the front of the tongue, ear sensation.\n- VIII Vestibulocochlear (S): hearing and balance.\n- IX Glossopharyngeal (B): taste (back of tongue), throat sensation, pharyngeal muscles, swallowing.\n- X Vagus (B): muscles of pharynx, larynx and soft palate; sensation of ear, throat and organs; parasympathetic supply to chest and abdominal organs.\n- XI Spinal accessory (M): sternocleidomastoid and trapezius (turn head, shrug).\n- XII Hypoglossal (M): tongue movement.\n\n## Memory aid\n\"Some Say Marry Money, But My Brother Says Big Brains Matter More\" gives S, S, M, M, B, M, B, S, B, B, M, M. Widely used, not from the handout.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nMatch a finding to a nerve: dysphagia to IX or X, facial droop to VII, cannot shrug to XI, tongue deviates to XII, double vision to III/IV/VI.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Cranial Nerve Testing and Deficits]], [[Bell's Palsy]], [[Trigeminal Neuralgia]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nVerify the detailed actions of IV (usually described as downward gaze) and the taste territory of VII versus IX.",

"title": "Cranial Nerve Testing and Deficits",
"body": "## What it is\nBedside tests for each cranial nerve and the problem a failed test suggests.\n\n## Test and finding, nerve by nerve\n- I: eyes closed, identify a familiar smell (coffee, cinnamon), one nostril at a time. Problem: anosmia.\n- II: Snellen chart, visual fields, ophthalmoscope. Problem: hemianopia, poor acuity, blindness.\n- III: eye movement toward the nose, conjugate gaze, pupil size and light reaction, lid opening. Problem: dysconjugate gaze, double vision, dilated pupil with or without poor light reaction.\n- IV and VI: follow the examiner's finger through the gaze directions. Problem: dysconjugate gaze, gaze weakness, double vision.\n- V: eyes closed, identify touch on face (three branches); touch a cotton wisp to the cornea (normal: blink and tearing); clench the jaw and palpate muscles. Problem: absent corneal reflex, facial numbness, jaw weakness.\n- VII: frown, smile, wrinkle forehead, check symmetry. Problem: facial weakness, cannot close eyelid, taste loss.\n- VIII: whisper or watch-tick test, Weber, Rinne, Romberg (balance with eyes closed). Problem: hearing loss, poor balance.\n- IX: swallowing and sugar-versus-salt taste on the back third of the tongue. Problem: dysphagia, taste change.\n- X: gag reflex, hoarseness, say \"ah\" and watch the uvula and soft palate rise evenly. Problem: weak gag, dysarthria, hoarseness.\n- XI: turn head and shrug against resistance. Problem: weak shrug, cannot turn head.\n- XII: stick out and move the tongue. Problem: swallowing trouble, slurred speech.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\nBefore feeding a client with an absent gag reflex, stop and report: aspiration risk affects airway, the first priority.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Cranial Nerves: Names, Types and Functions]], [[Hearing Tests: Weber, Rinne and Audiometry]], [[Stroke: Nursing Management]]",

"id": "np4-ns-spinal-ans",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Spinal Nerves and the Autonomic Nervous System",
"body": "## What it is\nSpinal nerves: 31 pairs: cervical 8, thoracic 12, lumbar 5, sacral 5, coccygeal 1. The autonomic nervous system (ANS) regulates organ activity to keep internal balance (homeostasis).\n\n## Two divisions\n- Sympathetic: fight or flight.\n- Parasympathetic: dominates when relaxed and not stressed (\"rest and digest\").\n\n## Effects compared (parasympathetic versus sympathetic)\n- Pupils: constricted versus dilated.\n- Heart rate: decreased versus increased; blood pressure decreased versus increased.\n- Coronary vessels: constricted versus dilated. Skeletal muscle vessels dilate with sympathetic. Skin and abdominal vessels constrict with sympathetic.\n- Bronchioles: constricted versus dilated; breathing rate decreased versus increased.\n- Gut movement: increased versus decreased; sphincters relax versus contract; digestive secretions increased versus little effect.\n- Saliva: thin and watery versus thick and sticky.\n- Liver: sympathetic raises glucose release from glycogen.\n- Bladder wall: contracts (parasympathetic) versus relaxes (sympathetic); sphincters relax versus contract.\n- Sympathetic only: sweating, goose bumps, adrenal medulla releases epinephrine and norepinephrine.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nPredict effects of a drug or injury: anticholinergics block parasympathetic action, so expect dry mouth, urinary retention, dilated pupils, fast heart rate. Loss of sympathetic control above T6 is behind [[Autonomic Dysreflexia]].\n\n## Memory aid\nParasympathetic = \"slow and digest\"; sympathetic = \"fight, flight, dilate lungs and pupils, speed the heart\". Made-up helper.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Spinal Cord Injury]], [[Neurons and Neurotransmitters]], [[Acute Angle-Closure Glaucoma]]",

"title": "Level of Consciousness and Glasgow Coma Scale",
"body": "## What it is\nNeurologic assessment covers cerebral function (level of consciousness and mental status), cranial nerves (motor and sensory) and reflexes. Level of consciousness (LOC) is the most sensitive early sign of neurologic change.\n\n## Assessing cerebral function\n- Judge alertness and how strong a stimulus is needed to get a response.\n- Orientation: ask person, place and time.\n- Use a painful stimulus on the nail bed with a blunt object when needed.\n\n## Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS)\nThree areas are scored:\n- Eye opening (1 to 4): spontaneous 4, to sound 3, to pressure 2, none 1.\n- Verbal (1 to 5): oriented 5, confused 4, words 3, sounds 2, none 1.\n- Motor (1 to 6): obeys 6, localizes pain 5, withdraws 4, abnormal flexion (decorticate) 3, abnormal extension (decerebrate) 2, none 1.\n- Highest score 15 (alert and oriented). Lowest 3 (deep coma). The handout calls a score below 7 comatose.\n- Updated GCS-P (2015) adds pupil reactivity: both pupils unreactive 2, one 1, neither 0; subtract this from the GCS total.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\nTrend scores over time: a falling total means worsening. Report a drop at once; an unwatched drop can mean rising pressure in the skull.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Increased Intracranial Pressure]], [[Meningeal Signs, Babinski and Posturing]], [[Disability and Exposure in the Primary Survey]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nMany sources use 8 or less as the coma cut-off, not below 7. "title": "Meningeal Signs, Babinski and Posturing",
"body": "## What it is\nAbnormal reflexes and postures that point to meningeal irritation or serious brain damage.\n\n## Meningeal irritation\n- Brudzinski sign: client supine, nurse flexes the head toward the chest. Positive when pain and resistance occur and the hips and knees flex on their own.\n- Kernig sign: client supine with hips and knees flexed; extending the knee causes pain or resistance. Positive means meningeal irritation.\n- Both signs are seen in [[Meningitis]].\n\n## Babinski reflex\nStroke the outer edge of the sole. Normal in adults: toes curl down and draw together. Abnormal (positive): toes fan out and the big toe goes up. This points to an upper motor neuron problem. (A fanning response is normal in infants.)\n\n## Posturing\n- Decorticate: arms pulled in, elbows, wrists and fingers flexed, legs extended and turned in, feet fixed. Lesion at or above the upper brain stem.\n- Decerebrate: arms and legs extended, arms turned in. More dangerous; lower brain-stem damage.\n- A change from decorticate to decerebrate means worsening injury.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\nReport new posturing immediately; check airway, breathing and pupils, then GCS.\n\n## Memory aid\nDecorticate = \"toward the core\" (flexed in); decerebrate = \"extended out\". Made-up helper.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Level of Consciousness and Glasgow Coma Scale]], [[Increased Intracranial Pressure]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe infant Babinski note is added from general knowledge.",

"title": "Neurologic Diagnostic Tests and Nursing Care",
"body": "## What it is\nImaging and recording tests used to find brain and spinal disorders.\n\n## Tests\n- Skull and spine X-ray: shows fracture, dislocation, compression. Care: support a confused or combative client, remove metal items, keep the spine immobilized.\n- CT scan: finds hemorrhage, brain shrinkage, tumors, skull fractures, abscesses. Care: ask about iodine allergy (if contrast is used), explain to lie still on a moving table, warn of a hot flush and metallic taste, remove hairpins and metal.\n- MRI: good for degenerative disease and intracranial or spinal abnormalities; not helpful for bone problems. Safety point: remove all metal (ask about pacemakers and implants).\n- EEG: records brain electrical activity through scalp electrodes. Care: withhold drugs that interfere (anticonvulsants, sedatives, stimulants) as ordered; adults sleep no more than 5 hours the night before.\n- Cerebral angiography: dye injected into the carotid, vertebral, femoral or brachial artery, then X-ray; shows tumors, aneurysm, blocked vessels, hematomas, abscesses.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\nBefore any dye test: allergy check first (anaphylaxis risk). After angiography: check the puncture site, distal pulses and neurologic status (standard practice, not in the handout).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Stroke: Types, Causes and Risk Factors]], [[Seizure Types and Epilepsy]], [[Increased Intracranial Pressure]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Pacemaker and implant safety for MRI and post-angiography checks are added from general knowledge.\n- Ask whether your instructor wants the EEG medication-hold rule stated as \"per order\".",

"title": "Headache Types and Migraine Phases",
"body": "## What it is\nHeadache (cephalgia) is a symptom, not a disease. Primary headaches have no organic cause; secondary headaches come from a cause such as a tumor or aneurysm.\n\n## Primary headaches\n- Migraine: repeated attacks of severe throbbing, pounding pain lasting 4 to 72 hours in adults. Classic migraine has a pre-headache aura (vision change, speech difficulty, numbness or tingling). Common migraine has no aura.\n- Tension-type: the most common; chronic and less severe; pressure or tight feeling in the temple area.\n- Cluster: severe vascular headache; more common in men.\n\n## Four phases of migraine\n- Prodrome (hours to days before): depression, irritability, feeling cold, loss of appetite, activity change, more urination, diarrhea or constipation.\n- Aura (less than 1 hour): flashes and bright spots, numbness or tingling of lips, face or hands, mild confusion, slight weakness, drowsiness, dizziness.\n- Headache (hours, up to 4 to 72 hours): throbbing pain, light sensitivity, nausea and vomiting.\n- Recovery (postdrome): pain fades, neck muscle contraction, tenderness, exhaustion.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nSpot a \"worst headache of life\" or a new headache with neurologic signs as secondary (possible bleed), not migraine: that is the safety-first answer.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Migraine Drugs and Headache Nursing Care]], [[Increased Intracranial Pressure]], [[Stroke: Types, Causes and Risk Factors]]",

"body": "## What it is\nDrug approaches and comfort care for migraine and other headaches.\n\n## Two drug approaches\n- Abortive: stops or limits a headache at onset or while it is happening. Best for people with infrequent attacks.\n- Preventive: for frequent attacks at regular or predictable intervals, or when a medical condition rules out abortive drugs.\n\n## Triptans\n- Sumatriptan is the most widely used; works in acute migraine and cluster headache. Others in the class: naratriptan, rizatriptan, zolmitriptan, almotriptan.\n- They narrow blood vessels, reduce inflammation and may lessen pain signaling.\n- Contraindicated in ischemic heart disease because they can cause chest pain. Ask about heart disease before giving.\n\n## Nursing care\n- Goals: relieve pain, treat the acute attack, prevent repeat attacks.\n- Quiet, dark room; head of bed raised to 30 degrees; local heat or massage; analgesics as ordered.\n- Stress reduction, biofeedback, exercise and meditation give a sense of control.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Headache Types and Migraine Phases]], [[Cranial Nerves: Names, Types and Functions]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout lists ondansetron, granisetron and dolasetron as \"serotonin receptor agonists\" for migraine. "title": "Increased Intracranial Pressure",
"body": "## What it is\nRise in pressure inside the skull because brain tissue, cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) or blood increases in bulk. Normal is 0 to 10 mm Hg; 15 mm Hg is the upper limit.\n\n## Causes\nBrain abscess, hemorrhage, edema (swelling), hydrocephalus.\n\n## Findings\n- Early: change in level of consciousness (earliest sign), pupil changes (slow or fixed), slowed speech, restlessness, confusion, increasing drowsiness.\n- Late: decorticate then decerebrate posturing.\n- Cushing's triad: bradycardia, hypertension, bradypnea. A late, ominous sign of herniation.\n\n## Complications\nBrain-stem herniation, diabetes insipidus, SIADH.\n\n## Priority nursing actions (and why)\n- Airway first: keep it patent; oxygen as ordered. Low oxygen and high carbon dioxide raise pressure.\n- Raise head of bed about 30 degrees (unless contraindicated) with head midline so veins drain.\n- Prevent Valsalva and other pressure-raising activities (straining, coughing, neck flexion).\n- Drugs: mannitol (osmotic diuretic), corticosteroids, anticonvulsants. Medical goals: reduce cerebral edema, lower CSF volume (CSF drainage).\n- Tests: CT and MRI most common, angiography, PET.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n\"What is the earliest sign?\" Answer: change in level of consciousness. \"Which finding means herniation?\" Cushing's triad.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Level of Consciousness and Glasgow Coma Scale]], [[Meningeal Signs, Babinski and Posturing]], [[Stroke: Nursing Management]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout's head-of-bed range is garbled (\"30 to -15\"); 30 degrees is the usual value. Cushing's triad is often described with a widened pulse pressure. The handout says 100% oxygen: confirm your protocol.",
