"id": "np4-ns-stroke-types-risks",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Stroke: Types, Causes and Risk Factors",
"body": "## What it is\nA stroke (cerebrovascular accident) is a loss of brain function when the blood supply to the brain is cut off. A transient ischemic attack (TIA) is a sudden motor or sensory loss that lasts less than 1 hour and clears; it is a warning of a coming stroke.\n\n## Ischemic stroke (blocked vessel)\nUsually a thrombus (commonest) or embolus.\n- Large artery thrombotic: plaque in large brain vessels.\n- Small penetrating artery thrombotic: the most common ischemic type.\n- Cardiogenic embolic: linked to dysrhythmia, usually atrial fibrillation.\n- Cryptogenic: cause unknown.\n\n## Hemorrhagic stroke (bleeding)\nMost often from hypertension.\n- Intracerebral hemorrhage: hypertension and cerebral atherosclerosis.\n- Intracranial aneurysm: weak, ballooned arterial wall.\n- Arteriovenous malformation (AVM): tangle of arteries and veins without a capillary bed, from development.\n- Subarachnoid hemorrhage: usually a leaking aneurysm near the Circle of Willis or an AVM.\n\n## Risk factors\nHypertension (major), atrial fibrillation, high lipids, diabetes, age over 55, smoking, carotid stenosis, obesity, heavy alcohol. Handout also lists African-American race.\n\n## Diagnosis\nCT scan (first, to tell bleed from clot), MRI, angiography.\n\n## Treatment (general)\nClot-dissolving (thrombolytic) therapy, platelet-inhibiting drugs; carotid endarterectomy for TIA or mild stroke; carotid stenting for severe stenosis.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Stroke: Deficits and Left versus Right Hemisphere]], [[Stroke: Nursing Management]], [[Neurologic Diagnostic Tests and Nursing Care]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nClot-dissolving drugs are only for ischemic stroke after CT rules out bleeding, within a strict time window; the handout gives no window, so check yours.",

"title": "Stroke: Deficits and Left versus Right Hemisphere",
"body": "## What it is\nDeficits depend on which brain area loses blood supply.\n\n## Types of deficits\n- Cognitive: confusion, altered level of consciousness.\n- Visual: homonymous hemianopsia (half the visual field lost in each eye), loss of peripheral vision, double vision.\n- Motor: hemiplegia (most common), hemiparesis, reduced deep tendon reflexes, ataxia.\n- Communication: dysarthria (hard to articulate), dysphasia (impaired language), apraxia (cannot do a learned action).\n- Sensory: paresthesia.\n- Emotional: lability, low stress tolerance, depression, withdrawal, fear, anger, isolation.\n\n## Aphasia\n- Expressive: understands but cannot form understandable words; may use single words.\n- Receptive: cannot understand speech; speaks but may not make sense.\n- Global: both.\n\n## Left versus right hemisphere\n- Left stroke: right-sided weakness, right visual field loss, aphasia, altered intellect, slow and cautious behavior.\n- Right stroke: left-sided weakness, left visual field loss, spatial-perceptual problems, distractibility, impulsive and poor judgment, unaware of deficits.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nRemember the crossover: brain side opposite to body side. Right-brain patients are the higher fall risk because they are impulsive and unaware of deficits; left-brain patients need communication strategies.\n\n## Memory aid\nLeft brain = Language (aphasia); right brain = Reckless. Made-up helper.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Brain Regions and Their Functions]], [[Stroke: Nursing Management]], [[Stroke: Types, Causes and Risk Factors]]",

"area": "NP4",
"title": "Stroke: Nursing Management",
"body": "## What it is\nCare after stroke follows priorities: airway and breathing, neurologic monitoring, safety, then rehabilitation.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- Keep the airway patent. Handout gives 100% oxygen to help lower intracranial pressure.\n- Quiet, restful room; monitor vital signs, GCS and pupil size.\n- Positioning: lateral initially, then low Fowler's with neck aligned once stable (per handout). Prevent shoulder adduction.\n\n## Hemianopsia safety\n- Approach from, and place food and belongings on, the unaffected (seeing) side.\n- Teach and remind the client to turn the head toward the blind side to scan.\n\n## Dysphagia (aspiration risk)\n- Check the gag reflex before feeding.\n- Calm, unhurried; upright position; place food on the unaffected side of the mouth; soft foods; mouth care before and after meals.\n\n## Motor deficits\nKeep needed objects within reach on the unaffected side; strengthen the unaffected side; range-of-motion for the affected side; keep body in functional alignment.\n\n## Verbal deficits\nSpeak slowly and clearly in short sentences; use gestures or pictures; try reading, writing and repeating alphabet sounds.\n\n## Cognitive deficits\nReorient often to time, place and situation; keep familiar objects.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Stroke: Deficits and Left versus Right Hemisphere]], [[Increased Intracranial Pressure]], [[Cranial Nerve Testing and Deficits]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nCurrent guidance gives oxygen only if oxygen saturation is low, and usually raises the head of bed for pressure; the handout's wording conflicts. Follow your school's reference.",

"area": "NP4",
"title": "Meningitis",
"body": "## What it is\nInflammation of the covering (meninges) of the brain and spinal cord. Causes: bacteria (Neisseria meningitidis), viruses, other organisms. Spread by blood, CSF, or directly from nearby fractures of the frontal or facial bones.\n\n## Findings\n- Early: headache and fever.\n- Positive Kernig and Brudzinski signs, photophobia, nuchal (neck) rigidity, opisthotonus (arched back).\n\n## Diagnosis\nLumbar puncture: culture and Gram stain of CSF and blood.\n\n## Treatment\nLarge IV antibiotic doses (vancomycin, cephalosporins), dexamethasone, fluid expanders.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- Respiratory isolation for 24 hours after antibiotics start (protects others, first because it is infectious).\n- Bed rest, dark quiet room, analgesics for headache.\n- Frequent vital signs and neurologic checks.\n- Fluid and electrolyte balance, daily weight; high-calorie, high-protein, small frequent meals.\n- Prevent pressure injury and pneumonia.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nNuchal rigidity with fever and headache means meningitis; the isolation answer is droplet-type respiratory isolation for 24 hours after treatment begins.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Meningeal Signs, Babinski and Posturing]], [[Encephalitis]], [[Increased Intracranial Pressure]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nVerify the precaution type and post-lumbar-puncture positioning, which the handout does not cover.",

"title": "Encephalitis",
"body": "## What it is\nAcute inflammation of brain tissue. Herpes simplex virus is most common; also fungi (Cryptococcus neoformans) and arthropod-borne viruses (carried by insects).\n\n## Findings\nHeadache and fever (most common), nuchal rigidity, confusion, lowered consciousness, seizures, light sensitivity, ataxia, sleep changes, tremors, hemiparesis.\n\n## Complications\nCognitive disability, personality change, motor deficits, blindness.\n\n## Diagnosis\nCT, MRI, lumbar puncture, EEG.\n\n## Treatment\nAnticonvulsants, antipyretics, analgesics, sedatives, and the antiviral acyclovir.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- Monitor vital signs and neurologic status often.\n- Care for confused or unconscious client.\n- Dim lights, reduce noise, give analgesics.\n- Injury prevention is key: falls and seizures.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nTell it from meningitis: encephalitis affects thinking and behavior (confusion, personality change) while meningitis is dominated by meningeal signs.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Meningitis]], [[Seizure Nursing Care]], [[Neurologic Diagnostic Tests and Nursing Care]]",

"area": "NP4",
"title": "Seizure Types and Epilepsy",
"body": "## What it is\nA seizure is a sudden abnormal burst of electrical discharge in the brain that changes movement, awareness or sensation. Epilepsy is a chronic disorder of repeated seizures.\n\n## Causes\nIdiopathic (genetic or developmental), brain injury, infection, vascular disease, drugs, poisons, drug or alcohol withdrawal, allergies.\n\n## Classification\n- Partial (focal): begins locally. Repetitive purposeless acts (lip smacking, picking at clothes).\n  - Simple: no loss of consciousness; motor, sensory (hallucinations) or autonomic (flushing, fast pulse) signs; under 1 minute.\n  - Complex (psychomotor): consciousness lost; 2 to 15 minutes.\n- Generalized: whole cortex involved.\n  - Absence (petit mal): brief staring spells, mostly children; triggered by stress, low sugar, fatigue, hyperventilation.\n  - Tonic-clonic (grand mal): 30 to 60 seconds; rigidity, fixed dilated pupils, clenched hands and jaw, breathing may pause, urinary incontinence.\n  - Clonic-type: repeated shock-like muscle jerks (handout writes \"cyclonic\").\n\n## Status epilepticus\nA series of grand mal seizures without waking in between: an emergency (handout: over 30 minutes).\n\n## Diagnosis\nEEG (most useful), CT, MRI.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Seizure Nursing Care]], [[Encephalitis]], [[Neurologic Diagnostic Tests and Nursing Care]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nModern definitions treat status epilepticus as about 5 minutes or more; \"cyclonic\" is probably myoclonic. Verify both.",

"area": "NP4",
"title": "Seizure Nursing Care",
"body": "## What it is\nThe nurse protects the client from injury and the airway from aspiration; never forces anything into the mouth.\n\n## During a seizure\n- Stay with the client, note the time.\n- Ease the client to the floor; protect the head.\n- Remove harmful objects; loosen tight clothing.\n- Do not restrain. Do not place a tongue blade or insert an oral airway.\n- Observe and record duration, body parts involved, and behaviors before and after.\n\n## After the seizure\n- Side-lying position to prevent aspiration.\n- Suction equipment ready; bed in low position.\n- Document events; reorient the client, allow rest.\n- Check airway and breathing first, since aspiration is the main risk (ABC).\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nThe wrong answers are usually \"restrain the arms\", \"insert a tongue blade\" or \"give water\". Pick the answer that protects the head and positions the client on their side.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Seizure Types and Epilepsy]], [[Encephalitis]], [[Increased Intracranial Pressure]]",

"title": "Myasthenia Gravis",
"body": "## What it is\nA defect in impulse transmission at the nerve-muscle junction, with too little usable acetylcholine; cause unknown or autoimmune. Worse with activity, better with rest.\n\n## Findings\nDiplopia and ptosis (earliest), dysphonia, dysarthria, generalized weakness; respiratory paralysis is the cause of death.\n\n## Diagnosis\n- Edrophonium (Tensilon) test: fast-acting acetylcholinesterase inhibitor; positive when facial weakness and ptosis clear within about 5 minutes. Atropine sulfate treats edrophonium toxicity.\n- EMG shows delayed or failed neuromuscular transmission.\n\n## Treatment\nPyridostigmine (first line), neostigmine, plasmapheresis, thymectomy.\n\n## Drugs to avoid\nMuscle relaxants, barbiturates, morphine, tranquilizers, neomycin.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- Airway first; watch breathing.\n- Check gag reflex before feeding; semi-upright position; thick fluids; flex neck while eating; start with cold drinks.\n- Give medicine 20 to 30 minutes before meals, on schedule.\n- Protect from falls and infection; plan rest.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Myasthenic Crisis versus Cholinergic Crisis]], [[Neurons and Neurotransmitters]], [[Guillain-Barre Syndrome]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout blames increased acetylcholine destruction; textbooks describe antibodies against acetylcholine receptors. Verify.",

"area": "NP4",
"title": "Myasthenic Crisis versus Cholinergic Crisis",
"body": "## What it is\nTwo opposite emergencies in a client on anticholinesterase therapy.\n\n## Myasthenic crisis\n- Cause: undermedication (too little drug).\n- Signs: severe weakness, higher blood pressure and heart rate, more secretions, breathing trouble.\n- Handout intervention: give neostigmine (the drug that was missing).\n\n## Cholinergic crisis\n- Cause: overmedication.\n- Signs: weakness with difficulty swallowing (the handout; cramps, salivation, slowed heart rate are also classic).\n- Handout intervention: stop all cholinergic drugs.\n\n## Priority actions\nBoth threaten breathing: check airway and breathing first, call for help, prepare for ventilation. Then identify which crisis, since the treatments are opposite.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nLook at the clue: \"missed dose\" means myasthenic; \"extra dose\" means cholinergic. A short-acting edrophonium test separates them: improvement means myasthenic; worsening means cholinergic.\n\n## Memory aid\nMyasthenic = Missing drug; Cholinergic = Cause too much. Made-up helper.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Myasthenia Gravis]], [[Guillain-Barre Syndrome]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nAtropine is the antidote for cholinergic crisis in most references; the handout lists only stopping the drug. Verify.",

"title": "Multiple Sclerosis",
"body": "## What it is\nA chronic, slowly progressive disease that strips the myelin from nerve fibers (demyelination), with periods of remission and exacerbation. Cause unknown; may follow a viral infection.\n\n## Findings\n- Charcot's triad: scanning speech, intention tremor, nystagmus.\n- Vision: blurring, double vision, patchy or total blindness.\n- Sensory: paresthesia, loss of position sense, pain.\n- Cognitive: memory loss, poor concentration, dementia, poor abstract reasoning.\n- Cerebellum and basal ganglia: ataxia, tremor, throat and face weakness.\n- Others: bowel and bladder dysfunction, muscle hypertonicity.\n\n## Diagnosis\nMRI, CSF electrophoresis, EEG.\n\n## Treatment\nInterferon beta, methylprednisolone, other steroids, baclofen (drug of choice for spasticity).\n\n## Nursing care\n- Promote mobility: walking, assistive devices; swimming and stationary cycling ease spasticity.\n- Avoid strenuous exercise (may trigger flare) and hot baths (burn risk because sensation is reduced).\n- Prevent cuts and burns; eye patch for double vision; respiratory precautions; bowel and bladder program.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nPick \"avoid overheating and fatigue\". Fatigue and heat worsen symptoms.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Guillain-Barre Syndrome]], [[Trigeminal Neuralgia]], [[Amyotrophic Lateral Sclerosis]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout lists \"warm packs\" for spasticity but also warns against hot baths; confirm. EEG as a test is questionable. Overheating avoidance is added knowledge.",

"title": "Guillain-Barre Syndrome",
"body": "## What it is\nAn autoimmune attack on peripheral nerve myelin: rapid demyelination of peripheral and some cranial nerves. Weakness starts in the legs and ascends. More frequent in males. Cause unknown or after a viral infection.\n\n## Findings\nClumsiness (initial), diminished reflexes, weakness moving upward, paresthesia, ataxia, dysphagia, eye muscle paralysis, diaphragm paralysis and respiratory depression.\n\n## Complications\nRespiratory failure, cardiac dysrhythmias, transient hypertension, orthostatic hypotension, pulmonary embolism.\n\n## Diagnosis\nEMG, CSF analysis, ECG.\n\n## Treatment\nPlasmapheresis; corticosteroids (handout).\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- Breathing first: maintain ventilation, incentive spirometry, chest physiotherapy; watch for failure and prepare for ventilation.\n- Monitor vital signs and heart rhythm; check cranial nerve function.\n- Gag reflex before feeding; range-of-motion exercise.\n- Care is mostly supportive; recovery is usually gradual.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n\"Ascending paralysis\" means GBS; the first concern is respiratory function.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Myasthenia Gravis]], [[Multiple Sclerosis]], [[Respiratory Failure Recognition]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nCorticosteroids are not shown to help in most references; immune globulin is the other standard treatment. "id": "np4-ns-parkinson-signs",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Parkinson's Disease: Signs and Pathophysiology",
"body": "## What it is\nA slowly progressing movement disorder tied to low dopamine, eventually disabling.\n\n## Pathophysiology\nDopamine-making cells in the substantia nigra die, so dopamine stores fall and the dopamine pathway degenerates. In the corpus striatum, acetylcholine (excitatory) outweighs dopamine (inhibiting), disturbing the extrapyramidal tracts that control complex movement.\n\n## Causes\nUsually idiopathic or degenerative; also viral infection, head trauma, antipsychotic drugs, free-radical buildup.\n\n## Findings\n- Cardinal signs: tremor, rigidity, bradykinesia (slow movement), postural instability.\n- Others: pill-rolling, mask-like face, monotone speech, drooling, heavy sweating, festinating gait, gastric and urinary retention, micrographia (tiny writing), dysphonia.\n\n## Diagnosis\nMostly clinical; PET and SPECT scans.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nPair \"dopamine low\" with levodopa treatment. Antipsychotic drugs (which block dopamine) can cause parkinson-like signs.\n\n## Memory aid\nTRAP: Tremor, Rigidity, Akinesia/bradykinesia, Postural instability. Widely used.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Parkinson's Disease: Drugs, Surgery and Nursing Care]], [[Neurons and Neurotransmitters]], [[Huntington's Disease]]",

"body": "## What it is\nTreatment replaces or imitates dopamine or blocks acetylcholine.\n\n## Drugs\n- Levodopa (most effective, mainstay), usually with carbidopa.\n- Amantadine (antiviral).\n- Dopamine agonists: bromocriptine, pergolide.\n- Antihistamine: diphenhydramine (Benadryl).\n- Anticholinergics: benztropine (Cogentin), trihexyphenidyl (Artane), Akineton.\n\n## Surgery\nThalamotomy (common complications ataxia and hemiparesis), pallidotomy (destroying part of the globus pallidus in advanced disease), pacemaker-like brain implants.\n\n## Levodopa teaching\n- Side effects: nausea and vomiting, orthostatic hypotension, insomnia, agitation, confusion.\n- Drugs that block its effect: phenothiazines, reserpine, vitamin B6 (pyridoxine).\n- Handout foods to avoid (vitamin B6-rich): tuna, pork, dried beans, salmon, beef liver.\n\n## Nursing care\n- Mobility: walking, stationary bike, swimming, gardening; warm baths and massage.\n- More fluid and fiber to prevent constipation.\n- Aspiration precautions; semi-solid diet and thick fluids.\n- Safety: orthostatic hypotension means rise slowly.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Parkinson's Disease: Signs and Pathophysiology]], [[Acute Angle-Closure Glaucoma]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nVitamin B6 limits matter mainly for levodopa without carbidopa. \"Renal damage\" listed by the handout as a levodopa effect is doubtful. "body": "## What it is\nLou Gehrig's disease: a progressive degeneration of motor neurons controlling voluntary muscles. Cause unknown; 5 to 10% inherited; too much of the transmitter glutamate is suspected.\n\n## Findings\nFatigue, muscle weakness, cramps, fasciculations (twitching), dysphagia, breathing difficulty, inappropriate laughing or crying, constipation, urinary urgency.\n\n## Diagnosis\nEMG, muscle biopsy, MRI, EEG, CSF.\n\n## Treatment\nRiluzole (glutamate antagonist); baclofen, dantrolene or diazepam for spasticity; mechanical ventilation as respiratory muscles fail.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- Respiratory: keep airway clear, support gas exchange (oxygen, ventilatory assistance), prevent infection.\n- Nutrition and swallowing safety.\n- Maximize function: prevent immobility problems, self-care, communication aids, assistive devices.\n- Support client and family.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Neurons and Neurotransmitters]], [[Multiple Sclerosis]], [[Guillain-Barre Syndrome]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThinking and senses usually stay intact in ALS (added). "title": "Spinal Cord Injury",
"body": "## What it is\nInjury to the cord causing loss of sensation and movement below the level of damage. Causes: vehicle crashes, gunshots, falls, sports. Risk: young age, alcohol and drug use, male.\n\n## Level of injury\n- C1 to C4: respiratory depression (ventilator risk).\n- C1 to C8: quadriplegia (some arm and hand movement at lower levels).\n- T1 to T6: paraplegia, some trunk movement.\n- T7 to T12: paraplegia, good upper back and abdominal strength.\n- Lumbar, sacral, coccygeal: bowel, bladder and sexual dysfunction.\n\n## Tests\nX-ray, CT, MRI.\n\n## Complications\nSpinal and neurogenic shock, deep vein thrombosis, pressure ulcers, orthostatic hypotension, [[Autonomic Dysreflexia]].\n\n## Priority nursing actions (and why)\n- Breathing first, especially in cervical injury.\n- Immobilize on flat, firm surface; cervical collar if neck injury suspected; move as a unit; do not realign body parts. Prevents further cord damage.\n- Careful suctioning.\n- Turn at least every 2 hours; intermittent catheterization for bladder distention.\n- High-calorie, high-protein, high-fiber diet; fluids; bowel training.\n- Anticoagulants and anti-embolism stockings against clots.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Primary vs Secondary Survey]], [[Spinal Nerves and the Autonomic Nervous System]], [[Types of Shock]]",

"body": "## What it is\nA life-threatening complication in clients with spinal injury at or above T6: a harmful stimulus below the injury triggers an uncontrolled sympathetic response the brain cannot balance.\n\n## Triggers\nBladder distention (most common), bowel impaction, urinary tract infection, ingrown toenails, pressure ulcers.\n\n## Findings\nPounding headache, profuse sweating, nasal congestion, goose bumps, bradycardia, blurred vision; severe hypertension is the danger (stroke risk).\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Sit the client upright to lower blood pressure.\n2. Find and remove the trigger: check the bladder (catheterize or unkink catheter) and check for fecal impaction.\n3. Monitor blood pressure often.\n4. "area": "NP4",
"title": "Alzheimer's Disease: Stages and Nursing Care",
"body": "## What it is\nProgressive, irreversible degeneration of the brain, starting with gradual loss of thinking and changes in behavior and mood.\n\n## Causes and brain changes\nUnknown; possibly infection, Down syndrome (trisomy 21) in the 40s, and low acetylcholine activity in the cortex and hippocampus. Cortical atrophy, enlarged ventricles, amyloid plaques and neurofibrillary tangles.\n\n## Warning signs\nMemory loss that affects work, difficulty with familiar tasks, language and abstract thinking problems, disorientation, mood and personality change.\n\n## Stages\n- Early: looks healthy; subtle personality change, forgetfulness, restlessness.\n- Middle: memory deficit, may get lost, impaired language, cannot do daily activities, poor judgment, sundowning (more agitation and wandering in late day), cannot identify objects by touch, cannot write.\n- Final: total dependence, loss of language and bowel and bladder control.\n\n## Treatment\nCholinesterase inhibitors for mild to moderate disease (donepezil, rivastigmine, tacrine), memantine (NMDA antagonist); antidepressants, antipsychotics, anti-anxiety drugs for behavior.\n\n## Nursing care\n- Calm, predictable, familiar, noise-free environment; memory aids; clear short sentences.\n- Safety: remove hazards, avoid restraints (they increase agitation), secure doors, ID bracelet.\n- Simplify routines; encourage decisions; one dish at a time, small food pieces.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Alzheimer's Disease]], [[Delirium versus Dementia]], [[Neurons and Neurotransmitters]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nBrain biopsy is listed as confirmatory, but diagnosis is clinical with confirmation after death. Tacrine is rarely used because of liver toxicity. Cholinesterase inhibitors work by preventing breakdown of acetylcholine.",

"title": "Bell's Palsy",
"body": "## What it is\nOne-sided inflammation of the facial nerve (cranial nerve VII) causing facial weakness and paralysis with rapid onset. Adults under 45; both sexes. Cause unknown; autoimmune, viral (herpes simplex or zoster) or bacterial.\n\n## Pathophysiology\nInflamed, swollen nerve gets squeezed, blood supply is blocked and the nerve is damaged (ischemic necrosis).\n\n## Findings\nCannot close the eye fully on that side, ptosis, ear or jaw pain, one-sided weakness, ringing in the ear, difficulty eating, loss of taste on the front of the tongue, flattened nasolabial fold.\n\n## Diagnosis\nHistory and physical, EMG.\n\n## Treatment\nPrednisone (7 to 10 days), analgesics, antivirals; heat, gentle massage, electrical nerve stimulation.\n\n## Nursing care\n- Soft diet; chew on the unaffected side; avoid hot food and drink (reduced sensation).\n- Artificial tears to protect the cornea.\n- Facial exercises: grimacing, wrinkling, whistling, puffing cheeks.\n- Reassure: usually improves.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nDo not confuse with stroke: Bell's palsy affects the whole side of the face, including forehead.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Cranial Nerves: Names, Types and Functions]], [[Trigeminal Neuralgia]], [[Stroke: Deficits and Left versus Right Hemisphere]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nStroke-versus-Bell's forehead sparing and eye-patching at night are added general knowledge.",

"title": "Trigeminal Neuralgia",
"body": "## What it is\nTic douloureux: sudden attacks of one-sided facial pain along the branches of cranial nerve V (second and third branches most common). Linked to multiple sclerosis.\n\n## Causes\nChronic nerve compression or irritation, degeneration of the Gasserian ganglion, blood vessels pressing on the nerve.\n\n## Findings\nIntense jabbing, burning or knife-like pain that begins and ends suddenly and lasts seconds to minutes.\n\n## Diagnosis\nHistory, observation of an attack, CT, MRI.\n\n## Treatment\n- Anti-seizure drugs: carbamazepine, phenytoin; alcohol or phenol injection.\n- Surgery: microvascular decompression, radiofrequency thermal coagulation, percutaneous balloon microcompression.\n\n## Nursing care\n- Prevent triggers: very hot or cold food, jarring the bed, washing face, combing hair, brushing teeth.\n- Use cotton pads and room-temperature water; rinse mouth after eating; chew on the unaffected side; soft foods.\n- After surgery: sensory loss means do not rub the eye, check for redness, use artificial tears.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Bell's Palsy]], [[Multiple Sclerosis]], [[Cranial Nerves: Names, Types and Functions]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout says trigeminal neuralgia is 400 times more common in MS; confirm this figure.",

"id": "np4-ns-eye-anatomy",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Eye Anatomy",
"body": "## What it is\nParts of the eye and what each does.\n\n## Structures\n- Cornea: clear, blood-vessel-free dome; main refracting surface.\n- Aqueous humor: made by the ciliary body; nourishes the cornea.\n- Uvea: iris, ciliary body and choroid.\n- Iris: the colored part. Pupil: opening that widens and narrows with light.\n- Lens: clear, biconvex; focuses near and far (accommodation).\n- Choroid: blood vessel layer feeding the outer retina.\n- Vitreous humor: fills about two-thirds of the eye and keeps its shape.\n- Retina: neural tissue, an extension of the optic nerve. The macula gives central vision. Rods: night vision. Cones: bright light and color.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nLink structure to disease: lens clouding is cataract; blocked outflow of aqueous humor is glaucoma; macula damage is central vision loss; detached retina is a floaters-and-curtain emergency.\n\n## Memory aid\nRods = \"rod, dark\"; cones = \"color\". Made-up helper.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Cataract]], [[Chronic Open-Angle Glaucoma]], [[Retinal Detachment]], [[Macular Degeneration]]",

"id": "np4-ns-vision-tests",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Vision Tests and Eye Examination Tools",
"body": "## What it is\nCommon eye tests and what each measures.\n\n## Tests\n- Snellen chart: distance vision. 20/20 is normal. 20/200 means you see at 20 feet what a normal eye sees at 200 feet.\n- Ophthalmoscope: fundus, optic cup, retina periphery, macula.\n- Slit lamp: binocular microscope magnifying 10 to 40 times.\n- Tonometer: measures eye pressure by flattening a small area of the cornea. Normal intraocular pressure (IOP) is 10 to 21 mm Hg.\n- Perimetry: visual field testing.\n- Ishihara plates: color blindness; colored dots form numbers or shapes.\n- Amsler grid: for macular problems; patient fixes on the central spot, and faded squares or wavy lines indicate trouble.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nMatch the test to the disease: tonometry for glaucoma, Amsler for macular degeneration, slit lamp for cataract, fluorescein for corneal abrasion.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Macular Degeneration]], [[Chronic Open-Angle Glaucoma]], [[Cataract]], [[Corneal Abrasion and Keratitis]]",

"body": "## What it is\nLoss of the surface layer of the cornea from injury (fingernail, branch, spark, overwearing contact lenses). "title": "Uveitis (Iritis)",
"body": "## What it is\nInflammation inside the eye. Anterior uveitis (iris or iris and ciliary body) is most common and usually one-sided. Intermediate involves structures behind the lens. Posterior involves choroid, retina or vitreous and is usually both eyes.\n\n## Causes\nImmune-mediated disease, idiopathic, ankylosing spondylitis, Crohn's disease, Reiter's syndrome, lupus, trauma.\n\n## Findings\nSudden deep eye pain, light sensitivity, red conjunctiva, small pupil that reacts sluggishly.\n\n## Treatment and care\n- Urgent ophthalmology review.\n- Topical corticosteroid plus a cycloplegic.\n- Teach drop technique and strict schedule to prevent permanent damage.\n- Sunglasses for light sensitivity.\n- Follow-up IOP checks because steroids can raise eye pressure.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nCompare pupils: uveitis has a small pupil; acute glaucoma has a mid-dilated, fixed pupil.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Acute Angle-Closure Glaucoma]], [[Corneal Abrasion and Keratitis]]",

"title": "Cataract",
"body": "## What it is\nClouding of the lens that blocks vision.\n\n## Types and risks\nSenile (age), congenital, traumatic. Risk: diabetes, ultraviolet light, radiation, corticosteroids, phenothiazines, some chemotherapy.\n\n## Findings\nBlurred or distorted vision, glare, slow painless loss of vision, a pupil that looks milky or white.\n\n## Diagnosis\nSlit lamp, ophthalmoscopy to rule out retinal disease, perimetry (fields normal), Snellen test.\n\n## Treatment\nSurgical removal of the lens, usually under local anesthesia. Eye drops before surgery for pain control and less movement; oral drugs may lower IOP. Lens implant (IOL) at the time of surgery.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Cataract Surgery and Lens Replacement]], [[Vision Tests and Eye Examination Tools]], [[Eye Anatomy]]",

"title": "Cataract Surgery and Lens Replacement",
"body": "## What it is\nSurgical techniques and care for cataract extraction.\n\n## Techniques\n- Intracapsular: lens and capsule removed.\n- Extracapsular: capsule opened, nucleus and cortex removed, posterior capsule left to hold an implant.\n- Cryosurgery: supercooled probe (about -35 degrees C) freezes the lens for removal.\n- Phacoemulsification: ultrasound liquefies the lens, then it is suctioned out.\n\n## Lens replacement\nAphakic glasses (objects look 25% bigger and closer), contact lenses, intraocular lens implants (usual choice: monofocal, multifocal, accommodative).\n\n## Before surgery\nOrient and explain; do not touch the eyes; ordered drops (antibiotic, mydriatic-cycloplegic), sedative, antiemetic.\n\n## After surgery\n- Prevent rise in eye pressure: no coughing, sneezing, rapid movement or bending from the waist; head up 30 degrees, lie on the unaffected side.\n- Report sudden pain with restlessness and fast pulse (pressure) or fever (infection).\n- Pain and nausea control; wear glasses or metal shield as instructed.\n- Wipe the closed eye from the inner corner outward with clean tissue.\n- Help the client move around safely with one eye patched for 1 to 2 days.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Cataract]], [[Acute Angle-Closure Glaucoma]]",

"title": "Acute Angle-Closure Glaucoma",
"body": "## What it is\nBlocked fluid outflow at the drainage angle causes severely high pressure. An eye emergency that can cause blindness quickly.\n\n## Findings\nEye pain, red congested eye, nausea and vomiting, slow pulse, sweating, peripheral vision loss, very high IOP, corneal swelling, pupil oval, fixed and semi-dilated.\n\n## Diagnosis\nTonometry, ophthalmoscopy, gonioscopy, perimetry.\n\n## Treatment\n- Acetazolamide (Diamox) to lower pressure by diuresis; pilocarpine (constricts pupil) and beta-blocker drops.\n- Laser iridotomy; the other eye is also treated to prevent an attack.\n\n## Teaching\nLong-term drops; follow-up; return at once for severe pain, light sensitivity, heavy tearing; avoid corticosteroids and anticholinergics (e.g. Akineton, diphenhydramine, benztropine).\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nSudden severe eye pain with halos and vomiting equals an emergency; priority is rapid pressure reduction, not pain medicine alone.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Chronic Open-Angle Glaucoma]], [[Uveitis (Iritis)]], [[Parkinson's Disease: Drugs, Surgery and Nursing Care]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nAcetazolamide is a carbonic anhydrase inhibitor, not a hyperosmotic agent as the handout says.",

"title": "Retinal Detachment",
"body": "## What it is\nThe sensory retina (rods and cones) separates from the pigment layer under it, often after holes or tears, trauma, inflammation or tumor.\n\n## Findings\nFloaters, flashes of light, blank areas of vision, a veil or curtain. If untreated: loss of central and side vision, legal blindness.\n\n## Diagnosis\nIndirect ophthalmoscopy (gray retina), slit lamp, ultrasound or optical coherence tomography.\n\n## Surgery\nScleral buckling (band indents the eye wall), pneumatic retinopexy (gas bubble or oil pushes the retina back), cryopexy (freezing probe makes a scar that seals it).\n\n## After surgery\n- Avoid bumping the head, coughing, sneezing, straining and bending head below the waist; avoid rapid eye movements for weeks.\n- Pain and nausea control; encourage safe activity; diversion such as radio.\n- Driving restricted; light activity at 3 weeks, heavier activity at 6 weeks.\n- Warm moist washcloth for comfort; clean technique for eye drops.\n- Report new floaters, flashes or shadows. First follow-up at 2 weeks.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Macular Degeneration]], [[Cataract Surgery and Lens Replacement]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nPost-gas-bubble positioning (often a specific head position) is not given; also avoid air travel and altitude after a gas bubble. "title": "Macular Degeneration",
"body": "## What it is\nThe leading cause of vision loss over 60: damage to the macula, causing loss of central vision while side vision remains.\n\n## Types\n- Dry (nonexudative): slow breakdown of outer retina; drusen deposits; gradual blurring, noticed while reading. No cure.\n- Wet (exudative): abnormal vessels grow under the retina and leak; sudden onset; straight lines look wavy or letters look broken.\n\n## Treatment\n- Dry: antioxidants (vitamins C, E, beta-carotene) and zinc may slow progression in high-risk people.\n- Wet: ranibizumab (Lucentis) or bevacizumab (Avastin).\n\n## Nursing care\nTeach the Amsler grid at home: look at it one eye at a time, with glasses on, several times a week. "title": "Penetrating Eye Injuries and Splash Injuries",
"body": "## What it is\nWounds that pierce or bruise the globe, and splashes of chemicals.\n\n## Findings\nMarked vision loss, chemosis, conjunctival tear, shallow anterior chamber with distorted pupil, hyphema (blood in front chamber), vitreous hemorrhage.\n\n## First aid for penetrating injury\n- Do not try to remove the object; keep it from moving.\n- No pressure and no patch on the eye.\n- Protect with a metal shield or a stiff paper cup until treatment.\n- Prevent bleeding and rising pressure.\n\n## Treatment\nTopical steroids, aminocaproic acid for clots, tetanus protection, antibiotics. "id": "np4-ns-ear-anatomy",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Ear Anatomy",
"body": "## What it is\nThe ear has three parts for hearing, and the inner ear also handles balance.\n\n## Parts\n- External ear: auricle collects sound; ear canal about 2.5 cm long, with hair, oil glands and wax (cerumen) glands.\n- Middle ear: tympanic membrane (eardrum), about 1 cm, normally pearly gray and translucent; conducts vibrations to the ossicles: malleus, incus, stapes (the smallest bones in the body).\n- Inner ear: in the temporal bone. Cochlea (hearing) and semicircular canals (balance), in the bony labyrinth filled with perilymph. Organ of Corti in the cochlea turns vibrations into nerve signals and separates frequencies. Cochlear and vestibular nerves join to form cranial nerve VIII.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nLocate a disease: otitis externa is in the canal, otitis media behind the drum, Meniere's in the inner ear.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Hearing Tests: Weber, Rinne and Audiometry]], [[Otitis Externa]], [[Meniere's Disease]], [[Cranial Nerves: Names, Types and Functions]]",

"title": "Hearing Tests: Weber, Rinne and Audiometry",
"body": "## What it is\nExamining the ear and testing hearing.\n\n## Inspection\nCheck the auricle for deformity, lesions, discharge, size and symmetry. Pain on moving the auricle suggests external otitis; mastoid tenderness suggests mastoiditis; flaky scaling suggests seborrheic dermatitis.\n\n## Tools\n- Otoscope: views the canal and drum.\n- Whisper test: cover one ear, whisper 1 to 2 feet away; a normal person repeats it.\n- Weber: tuning fork on the forehead; tests which ear hears bone-conducted sound better.\n- Rinne: compares air conduction (fork near the ear) and bone conduction (fork on mastoid).\n- Audiometry: pure-tone (louder needed means more loss) and speech.\n\n## Interpreting\n- Normal: Weber equal in both ears; Rinne air louder than bone.\n- Conductive loss: Weber louder in the affected ear; Rinne bone longer than air.\n- Sensorineural loss: Weber louder in the better ear; Rinne air longer than bone.\n\n## Memory aid\nConductive = \"Weber toward the bad ear\"; sensorineural = \"away from it\". Made-up helper.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Ear Anatomy]], [[Otosclerosis and Stapedectomy]], [[Meniere's Disease]], [[Cranial Nerve Testing and Deficits]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout says to hold the otoscope in the right hand; technique varies (adult ear: pull up and back). Verify.",

Stapedectomy removes the stapes (or its upper part and part of the footplate) and replaces it with a tissue graft and prosthesis.\n\n## Findings\nProgressive conductive loss, possible tinnitus, normal-looking drum, bone conduction better than air on Rinne.\n\n## After surgery\n- Balance disturbance or true vertigo may last several days, so protect from falls.\n- Standard care (not from handout): avoid blowing the nose hard, sneezing with mouth closed, straining, flying and loud noises; report severe dizziness, sudden hearing loss or drainage.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nConductive hearing loss with a normal drum and Rinne bone better than air.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Hearing Tests: Weber, Rinne and Audiometry]], [[Acute Otitis Media]], [[Ear Anatomy]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout gives the surgery under the \"otitis media\" heading; confirm the condition and the activity restrictions with your textbook.",

"title": "Meniere's Disease",
"body": "## What it is\nAbnormal inner-ear fluid balance (endolymph).\n\n## Findings\n- Meniere's triad: tinnitus (roaring), vertigo with nausea and vomiting, sensorineural hearing loss.\n- Fluctuating hearing loss and ear fullness.\n\n## Diagnosis\nCaloric testing (can bring on an attack); audiogram shows sensorineural loss; CT or MRI to exclude acoustic neuroma.\n\n## Treatment\n- Diary of attacks.\n- Diuretics (acetazolamide, per handout); vestibular suppressants: meclizine (up to 25 mg four times a day), diphenhydramine 25 to 50 mg three to four times a day, diazepam (habit-forming); promethazine for nausea.\n- Gentamicin or streptomycin to destroy vestibular function if vertigo is uncontrollable.\n- Surgery: endolymphatic sac decompression; labyrinthectomy (total deafness in that ear); vestibular nerve section.\n\n## Complications\nIrreversible hearing loss, social isolation, falls.\n\n## Nursing care\n- During an attack: lie still in a safe place (fall prevention).\n- Low salt and sugar; limit alcohol; avoid aspirin.\n- Teach medicines and side effects; keep a log of attacks and triggers; hearing follow-up.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Hearing Tests: Weber, Rinne and Audiometry]], [[Ear Anatomy]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nAcetazolamide is a carbonic anhydrase inhibitor, not an osmotic diuretic. "body": "## What it is\nEar wax packed in the canal, or objects and insects in the ear. Wax is drier in older people; cotton swabs push it deeper; children insert beans, beads, toys.\n\n## Findings\nReduced hearing, plugged feeling, pain, fever, drainage; an insect may cause a low rumbling.\n\n## Management of cerumen\n- Irrigate (not with a perforated drum or inflamed canal); direct the stream behind the wax.\n- Soften first with warm glycerin, mineral oil or half-strength peroxide for about 30 minutes.\n- Curette, suction or microscope if needed.\n\n## Foreign objects\n- Insects: put in mineral oil.\n- Do not irrigate where this could swell or push the object (handout says irrigation is contraindicated for insects).\n- Mechanical removal or suction; general anesthesia may be needed.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Otitis Externa]], [[Ear Anatomy]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nHandout wording on irrigation is unclear; usual rule is no irrigation for vegetable matter or with a perforated eardrum.",

"id": "np4-ns-x-tension-cluster-headaches",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Tension-Type and Cluster Headaches",
"body": "## What it is\nHeadache (cephalgia) is a symptom, not a disease. Primary headaches have no organic cause that can be found. Tension-type and cluster headaches are primary, like migraine. A secondary headache is a symptom of another problem, such as a brain tumor or an aneurysm.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Tension-type: the most common headache. It tends to be chronic and less severe. The client describes pressure and a tight feeling, often around the temples, sometimes with nausea.\n- Cluster: a severe vascular headache. The handout says it is most frequent in men. Sumatriptan is listed as effective for cluster headache as well as acute migraine.\n- Migraine for contrast: throbbing, pounding pain lasting 4 to 72 hours in adults, with phases (see [[Headache Types and Migraine Phases]]).\n- Secondary headache: tied to a cause such as tumor or aneurysm, so the cause must be found and treated.\n\n## Assessment\nAsk about location, quality (pressure versus throbbing), severity, duration, triggers, nausea, light sensitivity, and any new neurologic signs. Check vital signs and level of consciousness.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Rule out danger first: sudden severe headache, a change in level of consciousness, new weakness or vision change, or fever with a stiff neck is not a routine headache. Report at once, because bleeding, raised pressure or infection must come before comfort.\n2. Once safe, give comfort measures: quiet dark room, head of bed raised to 30 degrees, local heat or massage, analgesics as ordered.\n3. Teach stress reduction, biofeedback, exercise and meditation to give the client a sense of control.\n\n## Treatment and medications (general terms)\nAbortive drugs stop an attack that has begun. Preventive drugs are for frequent, predictable attacks. Triptans (sumatriptan and others) narrow vessels and reduce inflammation, but are avoided in ischemic heart disease. See [[Migraine Drugs and Headache Nursing Care]].\n\n## Client teaching\nKeep a headache diary of triggers, timing and what helped. Use relaxation methods. Report any change in the usual pattern.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nA \"most common headache\" item points to tension-type. A man with severe, repeated vascular headaches in clusters points to cluster headache. A headache with a neurologic sign is the one that needs urgent action.\n\n## How to reason about it\nCause (primary versus secondary) -> finding (pattern and neurologic signs) -> action (exclude a dangerous cause first, then comfort). The trap: treating a secondary headache as a simple primary one.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Headache Types and Migraine Phases]], [[Migraine Drugs and Headache Nursing Care]], [[Increased Intracranial Pressure]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout gives little detail on cluster headache (timing, eye symptoms, oxygen treatment). Fill these in from your textbook. Preventive drug names are not in the handout.",

"id": "np4-ns-x-tia",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Transient Ischemic Attack",
"body": "## What it is\nA transient ischemic attack (TIA) is a short-lived neurologic deficit caused by a temporary loss of blood supply to part of the brain. In the handout it is a sudden loss of motor function, sensory function or both, typically lasting less than 1 hour.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- The deficit clears completely, but the event is a warning that a full stroke may follow.\n- The causes and risk factors match ischemic stroke: hypertension (the major risk factor), atrial fibrillation, high lipids, diabetes, age over 55, smoking, carotid stenosis, obesity and heavy alcohol use.\n- Carotid endarterectomy is listed for TIA and mild stroke; carotid stenting is for severe stenosis (see [[Carotid Endarterectomy and Stenting]]).\n- Platelet-inhibiting drugs are part of management.\n\n## Assessment\nRecord exactly what happened: which side, which function (weakness, numbness, speech, vision), when it began and how long it lasted. Check vital signs, rhythm, level of consciousness (use [[Level of Consciousness and Glasgow Coma Scale]]) and blood pressure.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Treat a new deficit as a possible stroke until proven otherwise. Note the time it started and report quickly: the time matters for clot-dissolving treatment.\n2. Airway and breathing, then neurologic checks and safety (fall precautions, since weakness may return).\n3. Prepare for CT, MRI or angiography as ordered.\n4. Teach risk-factor control after the event.\n\n## Treatment and medications (general terms)\nPlatelet-inhibiting drugs, control of blood pressure, lipids and glucose, and carotid surgery or stenting when the carotid artery is narrowed.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nA TIA that returns, lasts longer, or leaves any lasting deficit is a stroke. Rhythm problems such as atrial fibrillation raise the risk of clots.\n\n## Client teaching\nStop smoking, limit alcohol, control blood pressure, diabetes and weight, take prescribed antiplatelet or other drugs, and call for help at once if weakness, trouble speaking or vision loss starts again.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n\"Symptoms disappear within an hour\" is TIA. The teaching point is that it is a warning and risk factors must be controlled.\n\n## How to reason about it\nCause (temporary clot or narrowing) -> finding (brief deficit that clears) -> priority (find and treat the cause before a stroke happens). The trap: treating a resolved TIA as \"nothing is wrong now\".\n\n## Related topics\n[[Stroke: Types, Causes and Risk Factors]], [[Stroke: Nursing Management]], [[Carotid Endarterectomy and Stenting]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe one-hour limit is the handout's figure; newer definitions focus on absence of brain injury on imaging. Check which your school uses.",

"id": "np4-ns-x-hemorrhagic-stroke-sah",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Hemorrhagic Stroke: Aneurysm, AVM and Subarachnoid Hemorrhage",
"body": "## What it is\nHemorrhagic stroke is bleeding into or around the brain. The handout says it is caused commonly by hypertension. Bleeding adds volume inside the skull and can raise intracranial pressure quickly.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Intracerebral hemorrhage: bleeding inside brain tissue. Most common in clients with hypertension and cerebral atherosclerosis.\n- Intracranial aneurysm: a bulging dilation of a cerebral artery wall that forms where the wall is weak.\n- Arteriovenous malformation (AVM): a tangle of arteries and veins with no capillary bed between them, due to abnormal development before birth.\n- Subarachnoid hemorrhage: bleeding into the space around the brain. The most common cause is a leaking aneurysm near the Circle of Willis, or a congenital AVM.\n- Diagnosis: CT scan, MRI and angiography.\n\n## Assessment\nSudden severe headache, decreased level of consciousness, neck stiffness, vomiting, focal deficits. Trend neurologic status and pupils.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Airway and breathing first; a falling level of consciousness is the earliest sign of rising pressure.\n2. Monitor level of consciousness, GCS, pupils and vital signs often. Watch for Cushing's triad (see [[Increased Intracranial Pressure]]).\n3. Quiet, dark, restful room; avoid straining, Valsalva and anything that raises pressure.\n4. Head of bed raised to about 30 degrees with the head midline, unless told otherwise.\n5. Give blood-pressure drugs and others only as ordered: hypertension drives bleeding.\n\n## Treatment and medications (general terms)\nControl blood pressure; medicines for pressure and seizures; surgery or a procedure to secure an aneurysm or AVM as the team decides. Clot-dissolving drugs and anticoagulants are not used in bleeding.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nRe-bleeding, rising intracranial pressure, herniation, seizures. A sudden \"worst headache\" or falling level of consciousness needs immediate action.\n\n## Client teaching\nControl hypertension, avoid straining and heavy lifting if advised, take medicines as prescribed, report sudden headache or neurologic change.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nKnow the four named causes, that hypertension is the common cause, and why a clot-buster would be dangerous in a bleed.\n\n## How to reason about it\nCause (ruptured vessel or aneurysm) -> finding (sudden headache, low consciousness) -> priority (airway, neuro checks, lower pressure, keep quiet). The trap: giving a drug that thins blood or raises pressure.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Stroke: Types, Causes and Risk Factors]], [[Increased Intracranial Pressure]], [[Aneurysms]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout lists no drugs or surgery for hemorrhagic stroke, and no seizure or vasospasm care; those details here are general. Confirm blood-pressure targets and positioning in your reference.",

"title": "Carotid Endarterectomy and Stenting",
"body": "## What it is\nProcedures that open or protect a narrowed carotid artery to prevent stroke. Carotid endarterectomy is removal of an atherosclerotic plaque or thrombus from the carotid artery. Carotid stenting is used for severe stenosis.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- The handout names endarterectomy for TIA and mild stroke, and stenting for severe stenosis.\n- Asymptomatic carotid stenosis is on the stroke risk-factor list.\n- Medical management of ischemic stroke also includes thrombolytic therapy and platelet-inhibiting drugs.\n\n## Assessment\nBefore: neurologic baseline, blood pressure, allergies (dye), medicines. After: neurologic status, speech, grip strength, neck swelling, airway.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. After neck surgery, watch the airway for swelling or bleeding (airway comes first).\n2. Neurologic checks often, compared with the baseline; a new deficit is reported at once.\n3. Check the neck wound for bleeding or swelling and monitor blood pressure.\n4. Pain control and early activity as ordered.\n\n## Treatment and medications (general terms)\nAntiplatelet drugs and blood-pressure and lipid control before and after.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nNew stroke signs, neck hematoma with breathing difficulty, wound bleeding, very high or low blood pressure.\n\n## Client teaching\nStop smoking, control blood pressure and cholesterol, take prescribed drugs, report weakness, speech change or neck swelling.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nMatch the procedure to the indication: TIA or mild stroke leads to endarterectomy; severe stenosis leads to stenting.\n\n## How to reason about it\nCause (plaque narrowing) -> risk (clot or stroke) -> action (open the vessel, then protect the airway and brain). The trap: forgetting airway watch after neck surgery.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Transient Ischemic Attack]], [[Stroke: Types, Causes and Risk Factors]], [[Thrombolytic Drugs]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nPost-procedure monitoring and complications here are general knowledge, not handout content. "id": "np4-ns-x-aphasia-communication",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Aphasia, Dysarthria and Apraxia",
"body": "## What it is\nCommunication problems after a stroke come in several forms that sound alike but are different. The exam expects you to tell them apart.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Dysarthria: difficulty speaking because of weak or poorly controlled speech muscles. The words are understood by the speaker but sound slurred.\n- Dysphasia or aphasia: impaired language itself.\n  - Expressive aphasia: understands, but cannot form understandable words; may manage single words. Linked to the frontal speech area (Broca).\n  - Receptive aphasia: cannot understand spoken words; can speak but may not make sense. Linked to the temporal area (Wernicke).\n  - Global (mixed) aphasia: both together.\n- Apraxia: cannot perform a previously learned action though the muscles work.\n- Aphasia is typical of a left hemispheric stroke (see [[Stroke: Deficits and Left versus Right Hemisphere]]).\n\n## Assessment\nTest whether the client follows commands, names objects, repeats words and writes. Separate \"cannot understand\" from \"cannot speak\". Hearing and consciousness need to be ruled out first.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Keep safety first: a client who cannot call for help or understand warnings needs close watch and a reliable call method.\n2. Speak slowly and clearly in short, simple sentences.\n3. Use gestures, pictures, writing and reading where the client is able.\n4. Ask the client to repeat alphabet sounds as practice.\n5. Allow time to answer; do not finish sentences or talk down to the client.\n6. Keep calm, face the client, reduce noise.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nFrustration, depression, withdrawal and emotional lability. Sudden new speech loss in a recovering client is a red flag for a new event.\n\n## Client teaching\nTeach family the same strategies: short sentences, patience, visual cues, and treat the client as an adult.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nA client \"understands but cannot find words\" is expressive; \"talks fluently but makes no sense and does not follow commands\" is receptive. The best nursing answer is the communication method that fits the type.\n\n## How to reason about it\nSite of damage -> type of language loss -> matched communication method. The trap: using only spoken instructions with a client who cannot understand speech.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Stroke: Deficits and Left versus Right Hemisphere]], [[Brain Regions and Their Functions]], [[Stroke: Nursing Management]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nFluent versus non-fluent wording and the exact speech areas are textbook details beyond the handout.",

"title": "Levodopa Therapy: Teaching and Interactions",
"body": "## What it is\nLevodopa is the most effective drug and the mainstay of treatment for Parkinson's disease. It is converted to dopamine in the brain to replace the missing supply. It is usually paired with carbidopa.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Side effects (handout): nausea and vomiting, orthostatic hypotension, insomnia, agitation, mental confusion. The handout also lists renal damage, which is doubtful.\n- Drugs that block its effect: phenothiazines, reserpine and pyridoxine (vitamin B6).\n- Foods to avoid (listed in the handout): tuna, pork, dried beans, salmon, beef liver. These are rich in vitamin B6.\n- Other Parkinson drug groups: amantadine, dopamine agonists (bromocriptine, pergolide), antihistamines, anticholinergics (benztropine, trihexyphenidyl, biperiden).\n\n## Assessment\nWatch tremor, rigidity and bradykinesia before and after doses; blood pressure sitting and standing; mood and confusion; nausea.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Safety first: check for orthostatic hypotension and teach slow position changes to prevent falls.\n2. Give the drug on schedule; effect depends on steady levels.\n3. Check the medicine list for blockers (antipsychotics such as phenothiazines, reserpine, vitamin B6).\n4. Monitor for confusion, agitation and abnormal movements.\n5. Support nutrition and prevent constipation with fluids and fiber.\n\n## Treatment and medications (general terms)\nSee also [[Antiparkinson Drugs: Levodopa, Carbidopa and Anticholinergics]]. Surgery (thalamotomy, pallidotomy, brain implants) is for advanced cases.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nSevere confusion or hallucinations, sudden drops in blood pressure, worsening movement control.\n\n## Client teaching\nRise slowly; take with the schedule given; avoid listed foods and vitamin B6 supplements unless the prescriber agrees; avoid drugs that block dopamine; report confusion, severe nausea or movement changes.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n\"Which statement shows understanding?\" Correct answers mention rising slowly and avoiding vitamin B6 foods or supplements. Wrong answers add vitamin B6 for energy.\n\n## Memory aid\n\"B6 blocks it\": tuna, pork, beans, salmon, liver. Made-up helper.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Parkinson's Disease: Drugs, Surgery and Nursing Care]], [[Parkinson's Disease: Signs and Pathophysiology]], [[Antiparkinson Drugs: Levodopa, Carbidopa and Anticholinergics]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nVitamin B6 interaction matters mainly when levodopa is given without carbidopa. "id": "np4-ns-x-neuromuscular-compared",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Neuromuscular and Demyelinating Diseases Compared",
"body": "## What it is\nA side-by-side study sheet for four diseases the exam likes to mix up.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Myasthenia gravis (MG): defect at the nerve-muscle junction, too little usable acetylcholine; autoimmune or unknown. Earliest signs diplopia and ptosis. Worse with activity. Respiratory paralysis is the cause of death. Test: edrophonium (Tensilon). Drugs: pyridostigmine first line, neostigmine. Also plasmapheresis, thymectomy.\n- Multiple sclerosis (MS): demyelination of nerve fibers in the central nervous system, remissions and exacerbations. Charcot's triad: scanning speech, intention tremor, nystagmus. Drugs: interferon beta, steroids, baclofen for spasticity.\n- Guillain-Barre syndrome (GBS): autoimmune attack on peripheral nerve myelin, rapid ascending weakness, reflexes diminished. Respiratory failure is the main danger. Treatment: plasmapheresis, supportive care.\n- Amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS): progressive loss of motor neurons; weakness, fasciculations, dysphagia, breathing trouble. Riluzole (glutamate antagonist).\n\n## Assessment focus\n- MG: ptosis, double vision, voice, swallowing, strength that fades with use.\n- MS: vision, balance, tremor, bowel and bladder, fatigue.\n- GBS: weakness moving upward, breathing, heart rate and blood pressure, swallowing.\n- ALS: muscle weakness, cramps, twitching, swallowing, breathing.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\nAcross all four, airway and breathing come first, then swallowing safety, then mobility and safety. In MG, plan around medicine timing; in GBS, watch vital capacity and rhythm; in MS, avoid heat and fatigue; in ALS, support breathing and communication.\n\n## Treatment and medications (general terms)\nSee each disease note. Avoid, in MG, muscle relaxants, barbiturates, morphine, tranquilizers and neomycin.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nRespiratory failure (MG crisis, GBS, ALS), aspiration, falls.\n\n## Client teaching\nRest and pace activity (MG, ALS), avoid overheating and strenuous exercise (MS), prevent infection (MG, GBS).\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nClues: \"ptosis and diplopia, worse at day's end\" is MG; \"scanning speech\" is MS; \"weakness starting in the feet and climbing\" is GBS; \"twitching and weakness with intact thinking\" is ALS.\n\n## Memory aid\nM = Muscle junction (MG); M = Myelin in the brain (MS); G = Goes up (GBS); A = Anterior horn/Atrophy (ALS). Made-up helper.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Myasthenia Gravis]], [[Multiple Sclerosis]], [[Guillain-Barre Syndrome]], [[Amyotrophic Lateral Sclerosis]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nVerify the ALS statement that intellect is spared, and the exact acetylcholine mechanism in MG.",

"area": "NP4",
"title": "Refractive Errors and Visual Acuity Terms",
"body": "## What it is\nRefractive errors are focusing problems: the light does not land correctly on the retina. The handout defines several terms briefly; this note gathers them.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Emmetropia: normal vision.\n- Myopia: nearsighted (near objects clear, distance blurred).\n- Hyperopia: farsighted.\n- Astigmatism: irregular curve of the cornea.\n- Aphakia: no lens (after cataract removal without a lens implant).\n- Acuity is read from the Snellen chart: 20/20 is normal; a person at 20/200 sees at 20 feet what a normal eye sees at 200 feet.\n- Legal blindness: best corrected acuity of 20/200 or worse.\n- Strabismus: eyes not aligned. Diplopia: double vision. Nystagmus: involuntary eye oscillation. Ptosis: drooping lid.\n- The lens focuses near and far by accommodation.\n\n## Assessment\nSnellen chart for distance acuity, visual fields by perimetry, ophthalmoscope and slit lamp for structure (see [[Vision Tests and Eye Examination Tools]]).\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Safety for clients with poor vision: orient to the room, keep the path clear, call light within reach.\n2. Teach glasses or contact lens use and care.\n3. After cataract removal without an implant, expect aphakic glasses or contact lenses.\n4. Report sudden vision change at once: this can be an emergency.\n\n## Treatment and medications (general terms)\nEyeglasses, contact lenses or surgery. Aphakic glasses magnify objects by about a quarter, making them look closer (see [[Cataract Surgery and Lens Replacement]]).\n\n## Client teaching\nRegular eye checks, protective eyewear, clean hands when handling lenses.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nMatch the term to the meaning: \"without lens\" is aphakia; \"uneven corneal curve\" is astigmatism.\n\n## Memory aid\nMyopia = \"My eyes see near\"; hyperopia = far. Made-up helper.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Eye Terms and Eye Removal Surgeries]], [[Vision Tests and Eye Examination Tools]], [[Cataract Surgery and Lens Replacement]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout lists \"blindness\" as acuity \"ranges from 20/400\" and proptosis as downward displacement; both look garbled. Verify. The cause of myopia and hyperopia (eyeball length) is textbook knowledge beyond the handout.",

"title": "Eye Medications and Drop Instillation",
"body": "## What it is\nA summary of the eye drug groups the handout uses, and how clients use them safely.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Antibiotic drops or ointment: blepharitis, bacterial conjunctivitis, corneal abrasion, after surgery. Antiviral drops for herpes simplex dendritic keratitis.\n- Cycloplegics: paralyze the ciliary muscle to relieve pain from spasm (corneal abrasion, uveitis). Mydriatic-cycloplegic drops may be given before cataract surgery. Cycloplegics are deferred in a ruptured globe.\n- Topical corticosteroids: uveitis and eye injuries. They can raise intraocular pressure, so pressure checks are needed.\n- Pilocarpine (miotic): narrows the pupil and increases aqueous outflow; used in glaucoma, including the other eye in acute angle-closure.\n- Beta-blockers (such as betaxolol) and carbonic anhydrase inhibitors (acetazolamide): lower pressure by reducing aqueous production.\n- Antihistamines, vasoconstrictors and mast cell stabilizers: allergic conjunctivitis.\n- Ranibizumab and bevacizumab: wet macular degeneration.\n- Aminocaproic acid: stabilizes clot after traumatic bleeding in the eye.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Safety first for emergencies: acute angle-closure glaucoma needs pressure-lowering drugs quickly.\n2. Wash hands before and after; teach the client to do the same.\n3. Teach the client the drop technique and the schedule: skipping doses can cause permanent damage in uveitis and glaucoma.\n4. Use a clean technique; do not let the bottle tip touch the eye.\n5. Do not use steroids or anticholinergic drugs in glaucoma unless the benefit outweighs the risk.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nReturn of severe eye pain, light sensitivity or heavy tearing; sudden vision change; rising pressure with steroids.\n\n## Client teaching\nEye drops are medicine, and glaucoma is a chronic illness needing lifelong drops even without symptoms. Keep follow-up visits. Tell every health provider about the eye condition.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nMatch the drug to the condition; know that pilocarpine constricts the pupil and cycloplegics relieve pain from spasm; steroids need pressure checks.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Acute Angle-Closure Glaucoma]], [[Uveitis (Iritis)]], [[Chronic Open-Angle Glaucoma]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout calls acetazolamide a hyperosmotic agent, but it is a carbonic anhydrase inhibitor. The detailed eye-drop steps (pressing the inner corner, spacing drops) are general knowledge, not handout content.",

"area": "NP4",
"title": "Conductive versus Sensorineural Hearing Loss",
"body": "## What it is\nHearing loss is either conductive (sound is blocked on its way through the outer or middle ear) or sensorineural (the cochlea or nerve is damaged). The tuning fork tests help tell them apart.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Normal: Weber is heard equally in both ears; Rinne shows air-conducted sound louder than bone-conducted.\n- Conductive loss: Weber is louder in the affected ear; Rinne shows bone conduction lasting longer than air.\n- Sensorineural loss: Weber is heard in the better ear; Rinne shows air conduction longer than bone.\n- Conductive examples in the handout: impacted cerumen, foreign body, otitis externa and media, and stapes fixation (otosclerosis).\n- Sensorineural example: Meniere's disease (with tinnitus and vertigo).\n- Audiometry: pure-tone (the louder the tone needed, the greater the loss) and speech audiometry.\n\n## Assessment\nWhisper test, tuning fork tests, otoscope view of canal and drum, audiogram. Ask about pain, discharge, tinnitus, vertigo.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Safety: vertigo and hearing loss raise fall risk.\n2. Communicate well: face the client, speak clearly, reduce background noise, do not shout.\n3. Treat the cause: remove cerumen or foreign body, treat infection.\n4. Refer for hearing evaluation.\n\n## Treatment and medications (general terms)\nRemove blockage, antibiotic or antifungal drops for infection, stapedectomy for otosclerosis, medicines for Meniere's. See each ear note.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nSocial isolation, falls, permanent loss in Meniere's.\n\n## Client teaching\nDo not clean the canal with cotton swabs; protect from loud noise; keep follow-up hearing tests.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nGiven Weber and Rinne results, name the type. Rule: conductive loss makes the bad ear sound louder on Weber; sensorineural makes the good ear sound louder.\n\n## Memory aid\nConductive = \"the Bad ear Booms\" on Weber. Made-up helper.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Hearing Tests: Weber, Rinne and Audiometry]], [[Meniere's Disease]], [[Otosclerosis and Stapedectomy]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout lists the Rinne result for a middle-ear infection oddly (see [[Acute Otitis Media]]). Verify.",

"id": "np4-ns-x-hyphema-sympathetic",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Hyphema, Eye Contusion and Sympathetic Ophthalmia",
"body": "## What it is\nBlunt injury to the eyeball (contusion) and penetrating wounds can cause bleeding and damage inside the eye. Hyphema is hemorrhage within the anterior chamber. Sympathetic ophthalmia is inflammation in the uninjured eye caused by the injured eye, which can lead to blindness in the good eye.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Findings after contusion or penetration: marked vision loss, hemorrhagic chemosis (edema of the conjunctiva), conjunctival laceration, shallow anterior chamber with an off-center pupil, hyphema, vitreous hemorrhage.\n- Do not remove a foreign object. Protect it from movement. Do not put pressure or a patch on the eye. Cover with a metal shield or a stiff paper cup.\n- Topical corticosteroids reduce inflammation. Aminocaproic acid (Amicar) is an antifibrinolytic that stabilizes clot.\n- Enucleation (removal of the eyeball and part of the optic nerve) is considered when the globe cannot be repaired and has no light perception. It is done within about 2 weeks to prevent sympathetic ophthalmia.\n\n## Assessment\nVision, pupil shape, depth of anterior chamber, bleeding, pain. Ask the mechanism of injury.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Protect the eye with a shield; do not press on it.\n2. Keep the client calm and still; avoid coughing, straining and bending, which raise pressure.\n3. Prevent bleeding and rising pressure; keep the head elevated as ordered.\n4. Report pain, vision change or bleeding immediately; prepare for urgent ophthalmology.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nRe-bleeding, raised eye pressure, loss of vision, sympathetic ophthalmia in the other eye.\n\n## Client teaching\nWear protective eyewear; report new redness, pain or vision change in either eye after an old injury.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n\"Metal shield, no patch, do not remove the object\" is the penetrating-injury answer; \"irrigate\" is for chemical splashes.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Penetrating Eye Injuries and Splash Injuries]], [[Eye Terms and Eye Removal Surgeries]], [[Orbital Trauma and Fractures]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nHyphema positioning and activity restrictions are not in the handout; check your reference.",

"title": "Status Epilepticus",
"body": "## What it is\nStatus epilepticus is a neurologic emergency: one seizure, or a series of seizures, without the client regaining consciousness in between. The handout describes it as one or a series of grand mal seizures lasting more than 30 minutes without waking intervals.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- A tonic-clonic seizure normally lasts 30 to 60 seconds, with rigidity, fixed dilated pupils, clenched jaw and hands, a pause in breathing and possible incontinence.\n- Causes of seizures include brain injury, infection, vascular disease, drugs, poisons, and drug or alcohol withdrawal.\n- EEG is the most useful test.\n- Anticonvulsant drugs are used (see [[Anticonvulsants: Phenytoin, Diazepam and Phenobarbital]]).\n\n## Assessment\nTime the seizure from the start; note body parts involved, eye position, breathing and color. Afterward check consciousness and orientation.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Airway and breathing: this is the main danger when seizures do not stop. Side-lying position, suction ready, oxygen as ordered.\n2. Protect from injury: ease the client to the floor, protect the head, remove hazards, loosen clothing, bed low. Do not restrain or put anything in the mouth.\n3. Call for help; give anticonvulsant drugs and other emergency orders quickly.\n4. Document timing and events.\n5. Check blood glucose and look for the cause, as ordered.\n\n## Treatment and medications (general terms)\nIV anticonvulsants as ordered. Doses are not in the handout; follow orders.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nHypoxia, aspiration, injury, brain damage. Long seizures need urgent medical care.\n\n## Client teaching\nTake anticonvulsants exactly as prescribed; do not stop suddenly; avoid triggers such as alcohol and sleep loss.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nA seizure that continues or repeats without recovery is an emergency: airway first.\n\n## How to reason about it\nCause (seizure that will not stop) -> risk (hypoxia and brain damage) -> action (airway, oxygen, protect, drug). The trap: restraining or placing an object in the mouth.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Seizure Types and Epilepsy]], [[Seizure Nursing Care]], [[Anticonvulsants: Phenytoin, Diazepam and Phenobarbital]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nModern definitions treat status epilepticus as a much shorter continuous seizure than 30 minutes. Verify the time your school teaches.",

"body": "## What it is\nA quick lookup of drugs named in the neurologic and sensory handouts, grouped by disease, so you can match drug to condition.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Migraine and cluster: sumatriptan and other triptans (not in ischemic heart disease).\n- Increased intracranial pressure: mannitol, corticosteroids, anticonvulsants.\n- Meningitis: vancomycin, cephalosporins, dexamethasone. Dopamine low = levodopa. ACh low = pyridostigmine (MG) or donepezil (AD). Made-up helper.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Multiple Sclerosis]], [[Myasthenia Gravis]], [[Parkinson's Disease: Drugs, Surgery and Nursing Care]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nDose, class and antidote statements here follow the handout; the migraine \"serotonin receptor agonist\" label, the hyperosmotic label for acetazolamide, and tacrine use should be verified.",

"id": "np4-ns-x-eye-emergencies-compared",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Red Eye and Eye Emergencies Compared",
"body": "## What it is\nA comparison to help decide which eye problem is an emergency and what the nurse does first.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Acute angle-closure glaucoma: emergency. Severe eye pain, red congested eye, nausea and vomiting, halos, mid-dilated fixed oval pupil, very high eye pressure. Needs pressure-lowering drugs (acetazolamide, pilocarpine, beta-blocker) and laser iridotomy.\n- Anterior uveitis: urgent. Sudden deep pain, light sensitivity, red eye, small sluggish pupil. Steroid drops plus cycloplegic.\n- Corneal abrasion: urgent. Pain, tearing, foreign body feeling, light sensitivity; fluorescein stain. Antibiotic drops, cycloplegic, patch.\n- Retinal detachment: emergency. Floaters, flashes, curtain or veil, painless. Surgery: scleral buckling, retinopexy, cryopexy.\n- Conjunctivitis: usually less urgent. Redness, itching, burning, discharge; contagious (bacterial, viral) or allergic.\n- Penetrating injury: shield, do not remove the object, no patch. Chemical splash: irrigate immediately.\n- Chronic open-angle glaucoma: painless; slow loss of side vision.\n\n## Assessment\nPain (severe or mild), vision change, pupil size and shape, discharge, light sensitivity, history of injury, contact lens use.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Treat severe pain with vision loss and nausea as an emergency; call the provider immediately.\n2. Chemical in the eye: irrigate first, ask questions later.\n3. Penetrating injury: shield only, no pressure.\n4. Avoid actions that raise eye pressure (straining, bending, coughing).\n5. Teach hand washing for infectious conditions.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nBlindness, raised eye pressure, spread of infection, loss of the other eye after trauma.\n\n## Client teaching\nSeek care at once for severe eye pain, sudden vision change, floaters with flashes, or injury. Never squeeze or rub the eye.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nCompare pupils: small and sluggish (uveitis), mid-dilated and fixed (acute glaucoma). Painless vision loss with a curtain is retinal detachment.\n\n## How to reason about it\nCause (pressure, inflammation, tear, infection) -> finding (pain, pupil, vision pattern) -> priority (save vision first). The trap: patching a penetrating injury or delaying treatment for acute glaucoma.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Acute Angle-Closure Glaucoma]], [[Uveitis (Iritis)]], [[Retinal Detachment]], [[Corneal Abrasion and Keratitis]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe retinal detachment \"painless\" feature and the chemical-irrigation rule are standard teaching and only partly in the handout. Verify.",

"id": "np4-or-bone-anatomy",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Bone Anatomy, Bone Cells and Remodeling",
"body": "## What it is\nThe adult skeleton has 206 bones. Knowing the vocabulary lets you decode exam stems about fractures, osteoporosis and bone tumors.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Four bone shapes: long (weight bearing and movement), short (spongy core with a thin hard shell), flat (make blood cells and shield vital organs) and irregular (shapes matched to their job).\n- Three bone cells, easy to mix up:\n  - Osteoblasts build: they lay down bone matrix.\n  - Osteocytes maintain: mature cells sitting in small pockets (lacunae).\n  - Osteoclasts break down: large multinucleated cells that dissolve and reabsorb bone.\n- Parts of a long bone: the shaft is the diaphysis; the two ends are the epiphyses; the periosteum is the tough outer covering; the endosteum lines the marrow cavity; marrow sits in the medullary cavity; cartilage with no blood supply caps the ends; the osteon is the working unit of hard (cortical) bone.\n- Ossification is how the matrix is made hard: calcium and phosphorus crystals bind to collagen fibers. Osteogenesis (bone formation) starts before birth.\n- Remodeling is the constant, simultaneous resorption and new bone formation that keeps bone strong. The handout says the whole skeleton turns over about every 10 years in adults.\n\n## What keeps bone healthy\n- Weight-bearing activity stimulates bone formation.\n- Enough dietary calcium.\n- Hormones: calcitriol (active vitamin D), parathyroid hormone, calcitonin, thyroid hormone, cortisol, growth hormone, estrogen and testosterone.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nExpect \"which cell is responsible for...\". Think: blast builds, clast crushes, cyte keeps. A disease where clasts outpace blasts (osteoporosis, Paget's) thins or disorganises bone.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Fracture Healing Stages]], [[Osteoporosis]], [[Paget's Disease of Bone]], [[Osteomalacia]]",
