"id": "np4-or-perthes",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Legg-Calve-Perthes Disease",
"body": "## What it is\nLegg-Calve-Perthes disease is a childhood hip problem in which the blood supply to the head of the femur (the ball of the hip joint) is reduced, so the bone tissue dies (avascular necrosis). The handout places it in children between about 2 and 12 years old. It is also called coxa plana. It is self-limiting: given time the bone heals and the child recovers fully once the disease has run its course.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Three stages in the handout:\n  1. Avascular stage: blood supply to the femoral head is interrupted (stated as within about 1 year).\n  2. Revascularization: \"creeping substitution\", where connective and vascular tissue grow into the dead bone and living, not yet calcified bone replaces it.\n  3. Healing: the bone ossifies (hardens) over about 3 years.\n- Self-limiting does not mean \"ignore\": the goal is to protect the hip shape while it heals.\n- A Scottish Rite brace is listed in the handout for coxa plana / Perthes (see [[Splints and Braces]]).\n- Total hip replacement is listed among later options for a damaged hip (see [[Total Hip Replacement Care]]).\n\n## Assessment\n- Pain in the groin and in the front of the thigh.\n- Limp or reluctance to bear weight on the leg.\n- Radiograph: decreased bone mass, ossification centers, and possibly a subchondral fracture (a small break under the joint cartilage).\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- Relieve pain with analgesics as ordered: comfort first so the child can take part in care.\n- Perform and teach range-of-motion activities to keep the hip moving normally.\n- Teach crutch use so the child can protect the hip from full weight (see [[Canes and Crutch Safety]]).\n- Support the family: explain that the condition is self-limiting and the child will fully recover. Reassurance is a priority nursing action because the course is long.\n\n## Treatment and medications (general terms)\n- Physical therapy to restore motion and analgesics for pain.\n- Containment is often done with a brace or cast; surgery may be used for some children (beyond the handout).\n\n## Complications and red flags\n- A misshapen femoral head can lead to early arthritis later (beyond the handout, check).\n- Worsening pain or loss of motion should be reported.\n\n## Client teaching\nTeach parents the three stages, why protection of the hip matters, safe crutch use, and that recovery takes years but is expected.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nRecognition: a young child with groin or thigh pain and a limp, or the cause (loss of blood supply to the femoral head), or the stage names. Also the key reassurance statement: self-limiting, full recovery.\n\n## How to reason about it\nReduced blood -> dead bone -> pain, limp -> protect the hip, keep motion, relieve pain, reassure. Trap: confusing it with a hip dislocation or infection; the question will mention a child with gradual hip or knee-area pain.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Developmental Dysplasia of the Hip]], [[Range of Motion Exercises]], [[Splints and Braces]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nStage timings in the handout look garbled (\"within 1 year\", \"over 3 years\"); confirm the durations. Pain in the knee from referred hip pain is common but not in the handout.",

"id": "np4-or-fractures",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Fractures: Types and Emergency Management",
"body": "## What it is\nA fracture is a break in the continuity of a bone. It happens when stress on the bone is greater than it can absorb. Causes in the handout: direct blows, crushing forces, sudden twisting, extreme muscle contraction, and weak bones (decalcification, as in [[Osteoporosis]]).\n\n## Must-know facts\nTypes:\n- Complete: the break goes through the whole bone and is often displaced (out of position).\n- Incomplete: only part of the bone is broken, for example a greenstick fracture (typical in children).\n- Comminuted: several bone fragments.\n- Closed (simple): the skin is intact.\n- Open (compound or complex): a skin or mucous membrane wound reaches the broken bone, so infection risk is high.\n\nReduction means restoring the bone to correct alignment:\n- Closed reduction: manual alignment of the fragments, then immobilisation (cast, splint, traction).\n- Open reduction: surgery inserts internal fixation such as rods, wires or pins to hold alignment while healing.\n\n## Assessment\nAcute pain, loss of function, deformity, shortening of the limb, local swelling and bruising (ecchymosis). Check pulses, colour, warmth, sensation and movement beyond the injury.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- Immobilise the part before the client is moved (prevent more damage). Splint the joints above and below the fracture so fragments cannot move.\n- Assess neurovascular status first and again after splinting (see [[Cast Care and Neurovascular Checks]]).\n- Cover an open wound with a clean or sterile dressing; control bleeding with pressure.\n- Elevate and apply ice as ordered; give analgesia.\n- Prepare for reduction, cast or surgery and monitor for complications.\n\n## Treatment and medications (general terms)\nReduction, immobilisation (cast, splint, traction, external fixator), analgesics, and for open fractures antibiotics (general practice).\n\n## Complications and red flags\nCompartment syndrome, nerve or vessel injury, infection (especially open fractures), fat embolism, and delayed union. Pain not relieved by usual measures, pallor, coolness, numbness: report at once (see [[Compartment Syndrome and Cast Complications]]).\n\n## Client teaching\nFollow weight-bearing limits, keep the cast dry, do muscle-setting exercises, and report warning signs promptly.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nDefine the fracture type; first nursing action (immobilise, check circulation before moving); reduction type.\n\n## How to reason about it\nSuspect fracture -> do not move -> immobilise joints above and below -> neurovascular check. Trap: attempting to realign or straighten the limb.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Fracture Healing Stages]], [[Cast Types and Casting Materials]], [[External Fixator Care]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout does not list spiral, oblique or transverse types or fat embolism; add from your text.",

"priority"

"id": "np4-or-bone-tumors",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Bone Tumors",
"body": "## What it is\nA bone tumor is a neoplasm of the musculoskeletal system. It can be benign, malignant and primary, or metastatic (spread from elsewhere).\n\n## Must-know facts\nBenign:\n- More common than malignant primary tumors; slow-growing, well circumscribed, encapsulated.\n- Osteochondroma is the most common benign bone tumor: a large bony projection at the end of long bones near the knee or shoulder.\n\nMalignant primary:\n- Rare; arise from connective and supportive tissue (sarcomas) or marrow.\n- Osteosarcoma (osteogenic sarcoma) is the commonest and most often fatal; appears most in children, adolescents and young adults, in fast-growing bones.\n- Common sites: distal femur, proximal tibia, proximal humerus.\n- Prognosis depends on spread to the lungs.\n\nMetastatic (secondary) bone disease:\n- More common than primary bone tumors.\n- Most common primary sites: kidney, prostate, lung, breast, ovary and thyroid.\n- Most often in the skull, spine, pelvis, femur and humerus.\n\n## Assessment\n- Pain from mild and occasional to constant and severe; disability; weight loss, malaise, fever.\n- Labs: raised alkaline phosphatase in osteosarcoma; raised acid phosphatase in prostate cancer spread; hypercalcemia with bone metastases from breast, lung or kidney.\n- Surgical biopsy gives the definite diagnosis.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- Prepare and support the client through painful tests; give IV or epidural analgesia early after surgery.\n- Support and handle affected limbs gently to avoid pathologic fracture.\n- Aseptic dressing technique and prophylactic antibiotics to prevent osteomyelitis and wound infection.\n- Recognise hypercalcemia early and report (see [[Metastatic Bone Disease and Hypercalcemia]]).\n- Watch the lungs: those with metastatic disease have a higher risk of pulmonary congestion after surgery.\n\n## Treatment and medications (general terms)\n- Primary malignant: surgical excision, radiation, chemotherapy; chemotherapy begins before surgery and continues after to treat micrometastases; osteosarcoma may receive intra-arterial chemotherapy.\n- Metastatic: palliative, aiming to relieve pain and keep quality of life.\n- Amputation may be needed (see [[Amputation Care]]).\n\n## Complications and red flags\nPathologic fracture, hypercalcemia, spread to lungs, infection.\n\n## Client teaching\nExplain the plan, pain control, fracture precautions and emotional support. Report new pain, confusion or constipation (possible hypercalcemia).\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nMost common benign (osteochondroma), most common malignant (osteosarcoma), which lab goes with which cancer.\n\n## How to reason about it\nYoung person + knee or shoulder pain -> osteosarcoma; older adult with known cancer + bone pain -> metastasis. Protect the limb first.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Musculoskeletal Imaging and Bone Scan]], [[Amputation Care]], [[Fractures: Types and Emergency Management]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout says treatment of metastatic disease is palliative; confirm how radiation fits in your text.",

"musculoskeletal",
"oncology",
"bone"

"id": "np4-or-x-ortho-surgery-basics",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Orthopedic Surgery: Preventing Infection and Promoting Ambulation",
"body": "## What it is\nGeneral nursing care for clients having joint replacement or other orthopedic surgery, usually for severe joint pain and disability. The handout organises it around two themes: preventing infection and promoting ambulation.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Skin preparation often begins one or two days before surgery.\n- A prophylactic antibiotic is given about 60 minutes before the incision, which is an effective way to prevent postoperative infection.\n- The joint may be cultured during surgery to identify later infections.\n- A persistent infection at the prosthesis usually needs removal of the implant and joint revision.\n- Walking and transfers are progressed step by step toward independence.\n\n## Assessment\n- Wound, drain, temperature and white blood cell signs of infection.\n- Neurovascular status and pain.\n- Mobility, strength and understanding of weight-bearing limits.\n- Skin condition before surgery.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- Give the preoperative antibiotic on time, exactly as scheduled (timing is the key point).\n- Use aseptic technique for every dressing change and drain.\n- Monitor wound drainage; report more than expected.\n- Encourage transfer to a chair several times a day for short periods, then walking farther each time.\n- Prevent clots with stockings and anticoagulants as ordered, and early movement (see [[Total Hip Replacement Care]]).\n- Control pain so the client can participate in movement.\n\n## Treatment and medications (general terms)\nProphylactic and therapeutic antibiotics, anticoagulants, analgesics, physical therapy. Severe infection may need surgical debridement or prosthesis removal.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nSurgical site infection, DVT, dislocation, excessive bleeding, nerve damage. Fever, increasing pain, redness, pus or foul drainage need prompt reporting.\n\n## Client teaching\nKeep the incision clean and dry, take antibiotics fully, tell all future providers about the implant, and report infection signs.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nTiming of the pre-op antibiotic (about 60 minutes before incision); what a persistent prosthesis infection requires; goal of independent ambulation.\n\n## How to reason about it\nForeign material in the body -> infection stakes are high -> prevent first (antibiotic, asepsis), then mobilise early to prevent clots and stiffness.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Total Hip Replacement Care]], [[Total Knee Replacement and Joint Surgery Basics]], [[Osteomyelitis]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout wording about pre-op skin preparation is incomplete; confirm local policy.\n",

"id": "np4-or-x-phantom-limb-residual",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Phantom Limb Pain and Residual Limb Care",
"body": "## What it is\nAfter an amputation, many clients feel the missing limb is still there. Phantom limb sensations are unusual feelings such as numbness, tingling, cramping, or the sense that the limb is crushed, twisted or in an odd position. Phantom pain is caused by severing of peripheral nerves. The handout stresses that these feelings are real, not imagined.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- The residual limb (stump) is shaped into a cone with an elastic compression wrap so the prosthesis fits well and is comfortable.\n- Sockets of the prosthesis are adjusted during the first 6 months to a year after surgery.\n- Wrapping controls edema and promotes healing.\n- Joint contracture comes from poor positioning and a protective flexion pattern with pain and muscle imbalance.\n\n## Assessment\n- Character and timing of phantom sensations versus surgical wound pain.\n- Wound, drainage and skin of the residual limb.\n- Mood, coping, willingness to look at and touch the limb.\n- Range of motion and positioning.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- Acknowledge the sensation as real and let the client talk about it (therapeutic communication).\n- Keep the client active; use distraction; gentle kneading massage to desensitise the limb; early intensive rehabilitation.\n- Wrap the limb with elastic compression to prevent edema and shape it.\n- Prevent contractures: no pillow under the residual limb of a leg amputee, avoid hip abduction, external rotation and flexion, lie prone periodically, keep legs together, and start ROM early.\n- Encourage the client to look at, feel and care for the stump (body image).\n- Support grieving.\n- Monitor hemorrhage risk: keep a large tourniquet visible at the bedside; mark drainage on the bandage to see if it is increasing.\n- Wash and dry the healed limb gently at least twice daily.\n\n## Treatment and medications (general terms)\nOpioid analgesics for surgical pain; a light sandbag on the limb may counter muscle spasm; local anesthetics, beta-blockers for burning discomfort, and antidepressants to improve mood and coping.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nBleeding, infection (increasing drainage), skin breakdown from the prosthesis, contractures, depression.\n\n## Client teaching\nDaily limb inspection and wash, correct wrap technique, positioning, wear non-skid shoes, and cotton T-shirt under shoulder harness for arm prostheses to absorb sweat.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nBest response to \"my foot hurts\" (acknowledge, it is real), why no pillow under the stump (hip flexion contracture), emergency tool at bedside (tourniquet).\n\n## How to reason about it\nReal nerve-based pain -> validate, treat, move; position to avoid contracture because the prosthesis depends on a straight, well-shaped limb.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Amputation Care]], [[Range of Motion Exercises]], [[Canes and Crutch Safety]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nTreatments for phantom limb pain vary; the handout's drug list is brief. Tourniquet use: confirm hospital policy.\n",

"musculoskeletal",
"amputation",
"pain"

"Amputation: minimizing altered sensory perceptions",
"Promoting wound healing",
"Prosthesis fitting"

"id": "np4-or-x-metastatic-hypercalcemia",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Metastatic Bone Disease and Hypercalcemia",
"body": "## What it is\nMetastatic bone disease means cancer that began elsewhere has spread into bone. It is more common than primary bone tumors. Breakdown of bone releases calcium, producing hypercalcemia, a dangerous high blood calcium.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Common sources: kidney, prostate, lung, breast, ovary and thyroid.\n- Common bone sites: skull, spine, pelvis, femur, humerus.\n- Hypercalcemia occurs with bone metastases from breast, lung or kidney cancer.\n- Treatment of secondary bone cancer is palliative: relieve pain and discomfort while promoting quality of life.\n- Acid phosphatase rises in prostate cancer spread.\n\n## Assessment\n- Pain of bone origin, disability, weight loss, malaise, fever.\n- Hypercalcemia signs (beyond the handout, check): confusion, fatigue, nausea, constipation, increased urination, muscle weakness, dysrhythmias.\n- Serum calcium, alkaline phosphatase and imaging results.\n- Respiratory status after surgery: clients are at higher risk for pulmonary congestion.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- Recognise hypercalcemia signs promptly and begin treatment quickly (airway and heart rhythm come first in an unstable client).\n- Hydrate with IV normal saline and encourage mobilisation; diuresis as ordered.\n- Handle and support affected limbs gently to avoid pathologic fracture.\n- Give analgesics (IV or epidural early after surgery).\n- Aseptic dressing technique and antibiotics as ordered to prevent osteomyelitis and wound infection.\n\n## Treatment and medications (general terms)\nIV normal saline, diuresis, mobilisation, bisphosphonates such as pamidronate, and calcitonin. Surgery and radiation may be used for pain or stability.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nPathologic fracture, dehydration, cardiac rhythm changes, confusion or coma from severe hypercalcemia, pulmonary congestion.\n\n## Client teaching\nFluid intake as advised, fracture precautions, report constipation, confusion, nausea or new bone pain, and pain-control plan.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nWhich cancers spread to bone; first treatment for hypercalcemia (hydration); drugs (bisphosphonates, calcitonin); gentle handling.\n\n## How to reason about it\nBone breakdown -> calcium floods blood -> fluids dilute, drugs stop bone resorption, and keep the client moving.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Bone Tumors]], [[Hypercalcemia]], [[Paget's Disease of Bone]], [[Musculoskeletal Imaging and Bone Scan]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout does not list the symptoms of hypercalcemia, so those are added from general knowledge. Confirm with your text.\n",

"id": "np4-or-x-cast-techniques",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Cast Techniques: Windowing, Bivalving and Reinforcing",
"body": "## What it is\nThree procedures done to adapt a cast after it has been applied. They answer a problem: a wound underneath, a cast that is too tight, or a cast that has weakened.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Windowing: a hole is cut in the cast over an open wound of the casted limb. Purposes: visualisation, inspection, dressing, and applying medications.\n- Bivalving: the cast is cut into two halves from top to bottom. Purposes: relieve cast tightness, allow x-ray, and inspect the limb.\n- Reinforcing: more plaster of Paris is applied. Purpose: restore strength when a wet cast has become unstable.\n- Tools for application or removal: stockinette, wadding (padding) sheet and gauze bandage, plaster of Paris or fiberglass, trimming knife, cast spreader, cast cutter (Stryker), bandage scissors.\n- A mold (posterior splint-type cast) is used for an affected part with infection, swelling or an open wound, as noted in the handout.\n\n## Assessment\n- Pain not relieved by elevation or analgesics, tight cast, swelling.\n- Drainage staining, odor, warmth on the cast surface.\n- Neurovascular status before and after the cut.\n- Cast integrity (cracks, softening, wetness).\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- If compartment syndrome is suspected, the cast is bivalved at once while keeping the limb aligned and the limb is kept at no higher than heart level (arterial flow). Notify the physician.\n- Re-check neurovascular status after any cast cutting.\n- Support the limb and handle gently during cutting; reassure the client about the saw noise and that it vibrates but will not cut skin.\n- Cover or treat the window area as ordered so skin does not bulge through (beyond handout).\n\n## Treatment and medications (general terms)\nAnalgesics; wound care and topical medicines through a window as ordered.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nSkin injury from cutting, swelling that bulges through a window, loss of alignment after bivalving.\n\n## Client teaching\nDo not cut or alter a cast yourself; report a wet, soft or cracked cast and tightness, odor or drainage.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nWhich technique solves which problem: wound access = windowing; tightness or x-ray = bivalving; wet or weak cast = reinforcing.\n\n## How to reason about it\nMatch the fix to the problem: look at it, loosen it, or strengthen it. If circulation is threatened, loosen first.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Cast Types and Casting Materials]], [[Compartment Syndrome and Cast Complications]], [[Cast Care and Neurovascular Checks]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nHandout list of reasons for bivalving is partly cut off; verify.\n",

"musculoskeletal",
"cast"

"Cast techniques: windowing, bivalving, reinforcing",
"Materials and instruments in cast application or removal"

"id": "np4-or-x-traction-complications",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Complications of Traction and Immobility",
"body": "## What it is\nA client in traction is on bed rest for a long time, so problems from immobility threaten more than the bone. The handout lists lungs, gut, bladder and veins.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Atelectasis and pneumonia: listen to the lungs every 4 to 8 hours; teach deep breathing and coughing to expand the lungs and clear secretions.\n- Constipation and anorexia: high-fibre, high-fluid diet; stool softeners, laxatives, suppositories or enemas may be ordered; include food preferences within the diet to improve appetite.\n- Urinary stasis and infection: encourage fluids to prevent dehydration and hemoconcentration.\n- Venous thromboembolism: watch for one-sided calf tenderness, warmth, redness and swelling (increased calf circumference).\n- Hypostatic pneumonia and pressure ulcers (balanced suspension): deep breathing, keep back dry, frequent turning, wrinkle- and crumb-free linen, lift buttocks with the overhead trapeze.\n- Joint contracture and muscle atrophy: ankle exercises, static quadriceps exercises, and active ROM to the unaffected limbs.\n\n## Assessment\nBreath sounds, bowel sounds and stool pattern, urine output and colour, calf measurements, skin over bony points, appetite.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- Airway and breathing first: deep breathing and coughing.\n- Foot exercises: ankle flexion-extension and calf-pumping about 10 times an hour while awake to reduce venous stasis.\n- Anti-embolism stockings, compression devices and anticoagulants as ordered.\n- Fluids, fibre, and regular bedpan times.\n- Meet nutritional needs: vitamin C, calcium and protein.\n- Provide diversional activity; attend to complaints.\n- Never remove skeletal traction weights except in a life-threatening situation.\n\n## Treatment and medications (general terms)\nAnticoagulants, bowel regimens, antibiotics if infection develops.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nSudden chest pain, shortness of breath (pulmonary embolism); calf swelling; fever and cough; cloudy or foul urine; abdominal distention.\n\n## Client teaching\nDeep breathe and cough, drink fluids, do foot exercises, report calf pain or chest pain.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nWhich complication matches the sign; which action prevents it (fluids for urinary stasis; fibre for constipation; leg exercises for VTE).\n\n## How to reason about it\nImmobility slows lungs, bowels, bladder, veins and skin; for each, one preventive habit is listed. Airway first.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Skeletal Traction Care and Complications]], [[Balanced Suspension Traction]], [[Deep Vein Thrombosis]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nSpecific timings for chest checks vary; the handout's figures are used.\n",

"id": "np4-or-x-spine-braces",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Spinal Braces and Collars by Level of the Spine",
"body": "## What it is\nA quick map of which brace or cast supports which part of the spine, the way the exam presents it (\"which device for which problem?\").\n\n## Must-know facts\nNeck (cervical):\n- Collar brace (Schantz, Philadelphia).\n- Collar cast: cervical spine.\n- Minerva cast: cervical and upper dorsal spine.\n- Four-poster brace: cervical and upper thoracic.\n- Crutchfield tongs (skeletal traction): upper dorsal and cervical spine, inserted at the parietal area.\n- Head halter (skin traction): cervical spine.\n\nChest and back:\n- Knight-Taylor brace: upper thoracic spine.\n- Body cast: lower thoracic and upper lumbar spine.\n- Jewett brace: dorsolumbar and upper lumbar.\n- Chair-back brace: lumbosacral spine.\n- Pelvic girdle traction: lumbosacral spine and herniated nucleus pulposus.\n\nScoliosis:\n- Risser's jacket: thoraco-lumbar cast.\n- Milwaukee brace: curves T9 and below.\n- Yamamoto brace: curves T9 and above.\n- Cotrel traction: head halter plus pelvic girdle.\n- Halo-pelvic (C-type) and halo-femoral (S-type) traction.\n\n## Assessment\nSkin under straps, neurovascular status of limbs, comfort, breathing, ability to eat and swallow with a collar.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- Assess neurovascular status before and after application.\n- Check skin under collars and braces; keep padding clean and dry.\n- Explain purpose and what the client will feel.\n- Keep the spine aligned (log roll) when ordered.\n- Pin care for halo devices; do not move the client by the halo frame (beyond handout).\n\n## Treatment and medications (general terms)\nAnalgesics, muscle relaxants, as ordered.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nPressure sores on chin, occiput or bony points; numbness or weakness in arms or legs; breathing difficulty.\n\n## Client teaching\nWear schedule, skin care, activity limits, and when to call.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nMatch device to spine region or condition.\n\n## How to reason about it\nThink top to bottom: neck devices are collars and tongs; trunk devices are body casts and jackets; scoliosis devices are Milwaukee and Yamamoto.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Splints and Braces]], [[Scoliosis]], [[Cast Types and Casting Materials]], [[Spinal Cord Injury]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nSpinal level assignments in the handout are unusual and some names (SOMI, Forester) may be misspelled; verify.\n",
